Potential theory and ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} -extensions
Damien THOMINE

Abstract
We study hitting probabilities for ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} -extensions of Gibbs-Markov maps. The goal is to estimate, given a finite Σ ⊂ ℤ d \Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d} and p p , q ∈ Σ q\in\Sigma , the probability P p ​ q P_{pq} that the process starting from p p returns to Σ \Sigma at site q q . Our study generalizes the methods available for random walks. We are able to give in many settings (square integrable jumps, jumps in the basin of a Lévy or Cauchy random variable) asymptotics for the transition matrix ( P p ​ q ) p , q ∈ Σ (P_{pq})_{p,q\in\Sigma} when the elements of Σ \Sigma are far apart. We use three main tools: a variant of the balayage identity using a transfer operator as a Markov transition kernel, a study inspired from fast-slow systems and the hitting time of small sets in hyperbolic systems to relate transfer operators and the transition matrices we seek to compute, and finally Fourier transform and perturbations of transfer operators à la Nagaev-Guivarc’h to effectively compute these transition matrices in an asymptotic regime.



Introduction

Large classes of uniformly hyperbolic dynamical systems, when endowed with suitable probability measures, behave similarly to Markov chains. Many theorems pertaining to sequences of i.i.d. random variables have found generalizations for sequences of observables of such systems: the law of large numbers (Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem), the central limit theorem
[
36
,
47
,
37
,
22
]
, the local central limit theorem and asymptotics of occupation times
[
11
,
43
,
1
,
48
,
16
]
and large deviation principles
[
38
,
39
,
53
]
, to cite only the most widely known.

This article is part of a project to understand how an important part of the general theory of Markov processes, namely the probabilistic theory of potentials, can be adapted to dynamical systems. The specific problem we are interested in is inspired by the computation of
hitting probabilities for random walks
. Given a recurrent random walk
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and a finite set
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, one can compute the transition probabilities between points of
\Sigma
– in other words, the probability, given any
p
,
q\in\Sigma
, that a random walk starting from point
p
returns to
\Sigma
at point
q
.

A
B
C

Figure 1:
Three sites
A
,
B
,
C
in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and a transition from
A
to
C
.

This problem has an interpretation in terms of resistances in electrical networks, and may be solved using potential theory, more precisely the so-called
balayage identity
(see e.g.
[
45
, Corollary 1.11]
, or
Theorem
1.1
below). With this method, for the simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and the subset
\Sigma=\{A,B,C\}
of Figure
1
, one finds that the matrix of transition probabilities is

P_{\{A,B,C\}}=\frac{1}{-\pi^{2}+8\pi-4}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{2}+4\pi-4&-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{2}+3\pi&\pi\\
-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{2}+3\pi&-\pi^{2}+6\pi-4&\frac{1}{2}\pi^{2}-\pi\\
\pi&\frac{1}{2}\pi^{2}-\pi&-\frac{3}{2}\pi^{2}+8\pi-4\\
\end{array}\right).

(0.1)

A natural generalization of random walks is the class of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions of dynamical systems. Starting from a probability-preserving ergodic dynamical system
(A,\mu,T)
and a jump function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, its
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions is the system
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
defined by:

•

State space:
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]:=A\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}
;

•

Measure:
\widetilde{\mu}:=\mu\otimes\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\delta_{p}
;

•

Transformation:
\widetilde{T}(x,p):=(T(x),p+F(x))\in[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
for all
(x,p)\in[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
.

This class of systems include
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-valued random walks,
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions of Markov chains
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
(sometimes used as toy models for more realistic processes),
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions of subshifts of finite type
[
43
]
as well as the collision map for Lorentz gases
[
48
,
49
]
. Using well-chosen sections, the study of the Lorentz gas flow or of the geodesic flow on Abelian covers of negatively curved compact manifolds may also be reduced to the study of such extensions
[
44
,
16
]
.

In the setting of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions, the study of hitting probabilities can be transposed as follows. Given a finite subset
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
p
,
q\in\Sigma
, what is the probability (under
\mu
) that the trajectory
(\widetilde{T}^{n}(x,p))_{n\geq 0}
returns to
A\times\Sigma
at the site
A\times\{q\}
?

While an explicit computation of the matrix of transition probabilities, as in Equation (
0.1
), was possible for the simple random walk, such an explicit formula seems to be out of reach for more general dynamical systems. As for the central limit theorem, the local central limit theorem or the principle of large deviations, elementary formulas are available in an asymptotic regime. For hitting probabilities, we propose to study the asymptotics of the transition probabilities for families
(\Sigma_{t})_{t>0}
of subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
whose points get farther and farther apart when the parameter
t
goes to infinity:

Figure 2:
Some elements of a family of subset
(A\times\Sigma_{t})_{t>0}
growing farther apart while keeping the same shape.

One particularly interesting case, pictured in Figure
2
, occurs when those points keep the same shape, that is

\Sigma_{t}=\left\{t\sigma(i)+o(t):\ i\in I\right\}

for some finite set
I
and some injective function
\sigma:I\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This kind of limit theorems is in line with a few former studies
[
32
,
40
]
.

In this setting, hitting probabilities could be obtained in
[
40
]
for families of subsets of size
2
. However, these results were byproducts of distributional limit theorems for additive functionals of the process
(\widetilde{T}^{n})_{n\geq 0}
. As a consequence, the methods involved were extremely heavy, limited in scope as they could not produce the matrix of transition probabilities but only its symmetrization, and obscured the underlying potential theory. A second article
[
41
]
laid down the foundation for the use of probabilistic potential theory for hyperbolic dynamical systems; in particular, we need to use the transfer operator as a Markov operator instead of the Koopman operator.

The current contribution has two main parts. First, parametrizing by
\varepsilon=t^{-1}
instead of
t
, we define
potential operators

(Q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
acting on Lipschitz functions on
A\times\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. We are able to relate these potential operators to the matrices of transition probabilities when
\varepsilon
vanishes:

Theorem 0.1
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
I
be finite. For
\varepsilon>0
, let
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Let
P_{\varepsilon}
be the matrix of transition probabilities on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
.

The following properties are equivalent:

•

there exists an irreducible (in the sense of Definition
1.8
) bi-L-matrix
R
such that
P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
;

•

there exists an irreducible (in the sense of Definition
1.13
) matrix
S
such that
Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}S+o(\varepsilon^{-1})
.

If any of these properties holds, then, in addition,
S=R_{0}^{-1}
.

One of the main features of this theorem is that its hypotheses are quite general: recurrence is necessary for
P_{\varepsilon}
to be a stochastic matrix, while ergodicity ensures (among other things) that
P_{\varepsilon}
is irreducible. No assumption is made on the shape of the subsets
(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
nor on the jumps
F
. Note that, for random walks, we have
Q_{\varepsilon}=(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})^{-1}
, so that the conclusion of Theorem
0.1
then holds even in the non-asymptotic regime.

The proof of Theorem
0.1
relies on the theory of
hitting time of small targets

[
42
,
26
,
9
,
6
]
: when
\varepsilon
vanishes, the transitions between different sites of
A\times\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
become rare events
1
1

1

This point of view explains the use of the parameter
\varepsilon
, which is the size of the targets, instead of
t
, for most of this article.
, so the process induced by
(\widetilde{T}^{n})_{n\geq 0}
looks like a Markov process on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
with transitions at times of order
\varepsilon^{-1}
. We give a probabilistic proof of Theorem
0.1
, inspired by previous works on fast-slow systems
[
9
,
12
]
and using a coupling argument to control these transitions. Note however, that a more analytic approach could be possible, along the line adopted by G. Keller and C. Liverani
[
29
]
, or by D. Dolgopyat and P. Wright
[
17
]
(the later work being closer to our setting, as the operators involved have a degeneracy of higher order than those studied in
[
29
]
).

The potential operators
(Q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
can be estimated, in the asymptotic regime
\varepsilon\to 0
, from the perturbations of the main eigenvalue of the transfer operator associated with
(A,\mu,T)
. When the jump function
F
is well-behaved, we get explicit expressions for the operator
S
in Theorem
0.1
, and from there, a first order expansion of the transition matrices
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
. We investigate four cases, when
d=1
and
F
is in the basin of attraction of a Lévy stable distribution (distinguishing the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
, general Lévy and Cauchy cases), and when
d=2
and
F
is square integrable. For instance, in the later case, we get

Proposition 0.2
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{2}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\mu,T)
with jump function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Assume furthermore that
\left\|F\right\|\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(A,\mu)
. Let
\Cov>0
be that asymptotic covariance matrix of
F
defined by Equation (
6.14
).

Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
be injective. For
t>0
, let
\sigma_{t}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be such that
\sigma_{t}(i)=t\sigma(i)+o(t)
for all
i\in I
. Let
P_{t}
be the transition matrix of the Markov chain induced on
\Sigma_{t}:=\sigma_{t}(I)
. Then

P_{t}=_{t\to+\infty}\Id-\frac{\pi\sqrt{\det(\Cov)}}{\ln(t)}R+o(\ln(t)^{-1}),

where
R
is the irreducible, symmetric bi-L-matrix defined by Equation (
6.12
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcll}R_{ij}&=&-\frac{1}{|I|}&\quad\text{whenever }i\neq j\in I,\\
R_{ii}&=&\frac{|I|-1}{|I|}&\quad\forall i\in I.\end{array}\right.

This text also includes a few results which may be of independent interest, such as a structural result on the periodic components of a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map, which has the curious particularity of being different for
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-extensions (see Proposition
3.3
).

The structure of this article is as follows. Section
1
is an introduction. We present a general method to compute transition matrices for random walks, ours setting (
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions, induced systems, Gibbs-Markov maps and transfer operators), and give our strategy to get ours results. Sections
2
,
3
and
4
give a proof of Theorem
0.1
; this decomposition in three steps shall be explained in Sub-subsection
1.5.3
. Afterwards, we shall obtain Proposition
0.2
and similar propositions in Sections
5
and
6
; the former will give a general formula for the potential operator
Q_{\varepsilon}
using Fourier transform, while the later will apply this formula to various settings. Finally, an appendix compiles some technical results on Gibbs-Markov maps.

1
Setting and main results

1.1
Hitting probabilities for recurrent random walks

1.1.1
General theory

Consider a sequence of independent and identically distributed
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-valued random variables
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, and set
S_{n}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}X_{k}
. The sequence of random variables
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is a random walk in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
starting from
0
. Let
P
be its transition kernel:

P_{pq}=\mathbb{P}(X_{0}=q-p).

This transition kernel acts on functions by
P(f)(p)=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}P_{pq}f(q)
.

In what follows, we shall assume that
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is recurrent and ergodic, which implies in particular that
d\in\{1,2\}
. Given
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\Sigma
, we define the hitting time of
\Sigma
as the random variable

\overline{\varphi}_{\Sigma}(p):=\inf\{n\geq 0:\ p+S_{n}\in\Sigma\},

and the
hitting point
as
p+S_{\overline{\varphi}_{\Sigma}(p)}\in\Sigma
. Then, by recurrence, the hitting point is a
\Sigma
-valued random variable. Its distribution can be computed in the following way: given any bounded function
f:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
, extend it to a bounded function
g:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}g(r)&=f(r)&\quad\forall r\in\Sigma,\\
(\Id-P)(g)(r)&=0&\quad\forall r\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\Sigma.\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

Then, by a martingale argument,

g(p)=\mathbb{E}(f(p+S_{\overline{\varphi}_{\Sigma}(p)}));

specializing e.g. to
f=\mathbf{1}_{q}
for
q\in\Sigma
gives in principle the distribution of the hitting point, as long as one is able to solve Equation (
1.1
).

In this article, we are interested in a slightly different problem:
\Sigma
is finite, and we want to understand transition probabilities ; that is, the starting point
p
belongs to
\Sigma
, and we consider the
hitting time

\varphi_{\Sigma}(p):=\inf\{n\geq 1:\ p+S_{n}\in\Sigma\}.

The problem of computing transition probabilities can be reframed in the following way. Taking a trajectory
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
of the random walk and keeping only the times at which the random walk belongs to
\Sigma
yields, by the strong Markov property, a
\Sigma
-valued
induced Markov chain
.

A
B
C
A
B
C

Figure 3:
A transition on
\{A,B,C\}
induced by a path in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

This induced Markov chain has transition matrix

P_{\Sigma,pq}=\mathbb{P}(p+S_{\varphi_{\Sigma}(p)}=q),

and we want to compute the operator
P_{\Sigma}
. This can be done thanks to a variation of Equation (
1.1
) adapted to starting points belonging to
\Sigma
, the so-called
balayage identity
. The version we quote is close to
[
45
, Corollary 1.11]
, and can be found in
[
41
]
:

Theorem 1.1

(Balayage identity)
.

Let
\Omega
be a Polish space,
P
a Markov kernel on
\Omega
, and
\mu
a measure which is stationary, recurrent and ergodic for
P
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\Omega
be measurable, with
\mu(\Sigma)>0
. Let
g\in\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
and
f\in\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mu_{|\Sigma})
. Let
f\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma}:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
be the function which coincides with
f
on
\Sigma
and is null everywhere else.

If

(\Id-P)g=f\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma},

then:

(\Id-P_{\Sigma})g_{|\Sigma}=f.

Theorem
1.1
relates the solution of the Poisson
equation for the kernel
P
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to the solutions of the Poisson equation for the kernel

P_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
. In the framework of electrodynamics,
f
is an intensity, while
g
is an associated potential.

A first remark is that
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, seen as a Markov chain on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, preserves the counting measure, is ergodic and recurrent. Moreover, for general recurrent Markov chains with stationary measure
\mu
, the restricted measure
\mu_{|\Sigma}
is stationary for the Markov chain induced on
\Sigma
. Consequently, in the context of random walks,
P_{\Sigma}
preserves the uniform measure
\mu_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
.

When acting on functions,
P_{\Sigma}
preserves constant functions. Since
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is ergodic, so is
P_{\Sigma}
, so
\Ker(\Id-P_{\Sigma})=\Vect(\mathbf{1})
has dimension
1
. Finally, since
P_{\Sigma}
preserves
\mu_{\Sigma}
, the eigenprojection onto
\Vect(\mathbf{1})
is given by
f\mapsto\mu_{\Sigma}(f)\mathbf{1}
, whence we have a
P_{\Sigma}
-invariant splitting
\mathbb{C}^{\Sigma}=\Vect(\mathbf{1})\oplus\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
, with
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}:=\Ker(\mu_{\Sigma})
. Hence, all we need is to understand the action of
P_{\Sigma}
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
.

Now, take any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
. Extend it to
f\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma}:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
. Find a function
g:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
(\Id-P)g=f\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma}
and
\mu_{|\Sigma}(g)=0
; such a function always exists under our hypotheses. Then
g_{|\Sigma}\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
and
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})g_{|\Sigma}=f
by the balayage identity. Doing this operation for all
f
in a basis of
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
allows us, in theory, to compute the operator
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
. Then, invert
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
, and from there get the action of
P_{\Sigma}
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
.

All is left is to effectively compute
g
given
f
. At this point the algebraic structure of the random walk plays a role, as we can use the Fourier transform. The main idea is that the operator
\widehat{P}
, which corresponds to the action of
P
in the frequency domain, is merely the multiplication operator by the characteristic function
\Psi(\xi):=\mathbb{E}(e^{i\langle\xi,X\rangle})
of
X
. Up to an additive constant,

\hat{g}(\xi)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\widehat{P}^{n}(\xi)\hat{f}(\xi)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\Psi(\xi)^{n}\hat{f}(\xi)=\frac{\hat{f}(\xi)}{1-\Psi(\xi)},

whence, writing
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}_{/\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
,

g(p)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\frac{\hat{f}(\xi)}{1-\Psi(\xi)}e^{i\langle\xi,p\rangle}\;{\rm d}\xi,

(1.2)

still up to an additive constant. If the kernel
P
is nice enough, as is the case for the simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, such integrals can even be explicitly computed.

To sum up, this strategy to compute the Markov kernel
P_{\Sigma}
has three main ingredients:

•

the balayage identity (Theorem
1.1
) which relates the Markov kernels
P
and
P_{\Sigma}
through the solutions to their respective Poisson equations;

•

the ability to solve the Poisson equation on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, using for instance the Fourier transform;

•

the ability to invert
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
.

In the case of random walks,
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
is finite dimensional, so the last step may seem comparatively insignificant. However, in the remainder of this article, we shall work with operators acting on infinite-dimensional Banach spaces, for which getting explicit information on their inverse is much tougher.

1.1.2
An example with the simple random walk

Now, let us delve deeper into the method described in Sub-subsection
1.1.1
by applying it to the example of Figure
1
. We take for
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
the simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and
\Sigma=\{A,B,C\}
with
A=(0;0)
,
B=(-1;0)
and
C=(1;1)
.

The simple random walk has a transition kernel
P_{p}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{q\leftrightarrow p}\delta_{q}
, where
q\leftrightarrow p
if and only if
p
an
q
are neighbours. Its action on functions
f:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
is thus

P(f)(p)=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{q\leftrightarrow p}f(q),

and the simple random walk is indeed ergodic, recurrent, and preserves the counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Let
P_{\Sigma}
be the transition matrix for the Markov chain induced on
\Sigma
. Then
P_{\Sigma}
preserves the uniform measure on
\Sigma
denoted by
\mu_{\Sigma}
. Thus we have a
P_{\Sigma}
-invariant splitting
\mathbb{C}^{\Sigma}=\Ker(\mu_{\Sigma})\oplus\Vect(\mathbf{1})
.

Now, we need to understand the action of
P_{\Sigma}
on
\Ker(\mu_{\Sigma})=\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
. It is enough to understand its action on a basis, say,
e_{1}:=\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{B}
and
e_{2}:=\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{C}
. Note however that Equation (
1.2
) only let us compute
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}e_{j}
up to an additive constant. To sidestep this issue, we compute the quantities

\langle e_{i},(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}e_{j}\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\Sigma)}:=\sum_{p\in\Sigma}e_{i}(p)\cdot\left[(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}e_{j}\right](p)

for
i
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, which is enough to reconstruct the action of
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
. Since all the
e_{i}
are in
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
, the indetermination of
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
up to constants does not matters. We illustrate this computation with
i=j=1
. By Theorem
1.1
,

\displaystyle\langle e_{1},(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}e_{1}\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in\Sigma}e_{1}(p)\cdot\left[(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}e_{1}\right](p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(e_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma})(p)\cdot\left[(\Id-P)^{-1}(e_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma})\right](p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\widehat{(\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{B})}(-\xi)\cdot\frac{\widehat{(\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{B})}(\xi)}{1-\Psi(\xi)}\;{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(1-e^{-i\xi_{1}})\cdot\frac{(1-e^{i\xi_{1}})}{1-\frac{e^{i\xi_{1}}+e^{-i\xi_{1}}+e^{i\xi_{2}}+e^{-i\xi_{2}}}{4}}\;{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\frac{1-\cos(\xi_{1})}{2-\cos(\xi_{1})-\cos(\xi_{2})}\;{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=2.

The other three computations are similar. We recover from there the action of
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
, then the action on
P_{\Sigma}
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
after inversion. Finally, adding the rank one map
\mathbf{1}\otimes\mu_{\Sigma}
yields the matrix of Equation (
0.1
).

1.2
Dynamical systems: extension and induction

1.2.1
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions of dynamical systems

A common generalization of random walks on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, in the context of ergodic theory, is that of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension
. Let
(A,\mu,T)
be a probability-preserving dynamical system, and
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
a measurable function (which we call the
jump function
). Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension as defined at the beginning of this text.

Let us write
S_{n}F(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}F(T^{k}(x))
the Birkhoff sums of
F
. Then

\widetilde{T}^{n}(x,p)=(T^{n}(x),p+S_{n}F(x)).

This setting includes random walks: take
A=(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
, then
T((x_{n})_{n\geq 0})=(x_{n+1})_{n\geq 0}
and
\mu=\mu_{0}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
a product measure on
A
. Choose
F((x_{n})_{n\geq 0})=x_{0}
; then, under
\mu\otimes\nu\in\mathcal{P}([\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
, the second coordinate is a random walk whose starting point has distribution
\nu
and whose jumps have distribution
\mu_{0}
. If one replaces the full shift by a subshift, or take a Markov measure instead of the product measure, we get a
random walk with internal states
, which is a special case of hidden Markov models
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
. If
(A,\mu,T)
is the collision map of a Sinai billiard, then
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is the collision map of a Lorentz gas, a popular diffusion model.

1.2.2
Inductions of dynamical systems

Let
(B,\nu,S)
be a measure-preserving dynamical system. For any measurable
C\subset B

with positive measure, we define the
first hitting time
of
C
as:

\varphi_{C}(x):=\inf\{n\geq 1:\widetilde{T}(x)\in C\ \}.

The system
(B,\nu,S)
is said to be ergodic if it has no non-trivial strictly invariant subset, and
recurrent
if
\varphi_{C}(x)<+\infty
for almost every
x\in C
, for any measurable
C\subset[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
.

If
(B,\nu,S)
is recurrent and
C\subset B
has positive measure, the
induced map

T_{C}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}C&\to&C\\
x&\mapsto&\widetilde{T}^{\varphi_{C}(x)}(x)\end{array}\right.

is well-defined almost everywhere, recurrent, and preserves the restricted measure
\nu_{C}:=\nu_{|C}

[
41
, Corollary 1.1]
. If in addition
(B,\nu,S)
is ergodic, then so is
(C,\nu_{C},T_{C})
. In what follows, we shall often make this assumption:

Hypothesis 1.2

(Recurrence)
.

The
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic and recurrent.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a recurrent
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension. For any
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we shall write
[\Sigma]:=A\times\Sigma\subset[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
. If
\Sigma=\{\sigma\}
is a singleton, we shall dispense with the brackets, and write
[p]=A\times\{p\}
. In this article, we will work with three types of systems:

•

the initial probability-preserving dynamical system
(A,\mu,T)
;

•

a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
with jump function
F
;

•

induced maps
([\Sigma],\mu_{[\Sigma]},T_{[\Sigma]})
for
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

We try as much as possible to keep our notations coherent: objects with a tilde refer to the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension, and objects with a set as an index refer to induced maps.

In this context, we define transition probabilities as follows:

Definition 1.3

(Transition probabilities)
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a recurrent
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a dynamical system
(A,\mu,T)
. Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be finite.

The matrix of transition probabilities
P_{\Sigma}
is defined by:

P_{\Sigma,pq}:=\mu\left(x\in A:T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[q]\right).

As long as the extension is recurrent, the matrix
P_{\Sigma}
is
bi-stochastic
: the sum of each of its rows and of each of its columns is
1
.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a recurrent
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a probability preserving
dynamical system. Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be non-empty. Then
P_{\Sigma}
is bi-stochastic.

Proof.

The fact that
P_{\Sigma}
is stochastic is a translation of the fact that
\widetilde{T}
is recurrent.
For any
p\in\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\Sigma}P_{\Sigma,pq}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\in\Sigma}\mu\left(x\in A:T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[q]\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(x\in A:T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[\Sigma]\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mu}\left([p]\cap\{\varphi_{[\Sigma]}<+\infty\}\right)

\displaystyle=1.

The bi-stochasticity of
P_{\Sigma}
comes from the fact that
T_{[\Sigma]}
preserves
\mu_{[\Sigma]}
.
For any
q\in\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\Sigma}P_{\Sigma,pq}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in\Sigma}\mu\left(x\in A:T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[q]\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{[\Sigma]}\left(T_{[\Sigma]}^{-1}([q])\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{[\Sigma]}\left([q]\right)

\displaystyle=1.\qed

1.3
Gibbs-Markov maps

If no assumption is made on
(A,\mu,T)
nor on
F
, the behaviour of the trajectories of the system
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
can be extremely diverse: for instance, the rate of diffusion in Ehrenfest’s wind-tree model depends on the geometry of the scatterers
[
14
,
19
]
. However, if
(A,\mu,T)
is in some sense chaotic and
F
regular, the trajectories of the system
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
will be very similar to random walks on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

In this article, we choose to work with Gibbs-Markov maps. This family of transformations is Markovian, which brings the benefits of avatars of the strong Markov property, for instance Corollary
A.7
. These transformations are still flexible enough to give rise to a wide variety of examples and, using Young’s construction
[
54
]
, they appear in many geometric examples such as the geodesic flow on negatively curved manifolds, finite horizon Sinai billiards, and Collet-Eckmann unimodal transformations.

The family of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions we get is much more general than random walks and random walks with internal states as in
[
32
,
33
]
, while still more restrictive than Sinai billiards or unimodal maps, since we do not work with Young towers.

1.3.1
Definition

In what follows, we shall assume that
(A,\mu,T)
is a
Gibbs-Markov map
:

Definition 1.5

(Measure-preserving Gibbs-Markov maps)
.

Let
(A,d^{\prime})
be a metric bounded Polish space,
\mathcal{A}
its Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
\mu
a probability measure on
(A,\mathcal{A})
. Let
\alpha
be a partition of
A
in subsets of positive measure (up to a null set). Let
T:\ A\to A
be a
\mu
-preserving map, Markov with respect to the partition
\alpha
, and such that
\alpha
is generating. Such a map is said to be
Gibbs-Markov
if it also satisfies:

•

Big image property:
\inf_{a\in\alpha}\mu(Ta)>0
;

•

Expansivity: there exists
\Lambda>1
such that
d^{\prime}(Tx,Ty)\geq\Lambda d(x,y)

for all
a\in\alpha
and
(x,y)\in a\times a
;

•

Bounded distortion: there exists
C>0
such that, for all
a\in\alpha
, for almost every

(x,y)\in a\times a
:

\left|\frac{\;{\rm d}\mu}{\;{\rm d}\mu\circ T}(x)-\frac{\;{\rm d}\mu}{\;{\rm d}\mu\circ T}(y)\right|\leq Cd^{\prime}(Tx,Ty)\frac{\;{\rm d}\mu}{\;{\rm d}\mu\circ T}(x).

(1.3)

Given a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\alpha,d^{\prime},\mu,T)
, a function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is said to be
Markov
if it is constant on elements of the partition
\alpha
. In this case, we also say that the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is Markov.

By encoding, Gibbs-Markov maps can model sequences of independent and identically distributed
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-valued random variables, as well as finite state Markov chains. As such, Markov extensions of Gibbs-Markov maps include all random walks, with or without internal states, on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. They are however more general, including for instance the Gauss maps

T:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}(0,1]&\to&[0,1]\\
x&\mapsto&1/x-\lfloor 1/x\rfloor\end{array}\right.,

and appear in various encoding schemes used to study geodesic flows in negative sectional curvature as well as Sinai billiards.

A comprehensive presentation of the properties of Gibbs-Markov maps we use in this article can be found in Appendix
A
, in particular their properties related to the extension and induction operations described in Subsection
1.2
.

1.3.2
Transfer operators

One of our main tools is the
transfer operator

\mathcal{L}
, defined as the dual of the composition (or Koopman) operator
\mathcal{K}:g\mapsto g\circ T
:

\int_{A}\mathcal{L}(f)\cdot g\;{\rm d}\mu:=\int_{A}f\cdot g\circ T\;{\rm d}\mu,

(1.4)

for all
p\in[1,\infty]
, all
f\in\mathbb{L}^{p}(A,\mu;\mathbb{C})
and all
g\in\mathbb{L}^{q}(A,\mu;\mathbb{C})
, with
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. Since the Koopman operator is an isometry on
\mathbb{L}^{q}
, the transfer operator is a weak contraction on
\mathbb{L}^{p}
.

As specified in Sub-subsection
1.2.2
, the operator
\mathcal{L}
acts on
\mathbb{L}^{p}(A,\mu;\mathbb{C})
,
the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
on
\mathbb{L}^{p}([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu};\mathbb{C})
and the operator
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}

on
\mathbb{L}^{p}([\Sigma],\mu_{[\Sigma]};\mathbb{C})
.

One of the main feature of Gibbs-Markov maps is that the transfer operator acts quasi-compactly on a Banach space of
Lipschitz functions. We present here the most important properties of the action; again, details and references can be found in Appendix
A
.

Definition 1.6

(Lipschitz functions)
.

Let
\alpha^{*}:=\bigvee\{T(a):\ a\in\alpha\}
be the image partition
2
2

2

In many applications,
\alpha^{*}
is trivial –
consider for instance the Gauss map, where any interval
((n+1)^{-1},n^{-1}]
is sent onto
[0,1)
.
of
\alpha
by
T
. The
Lipschitz seminorm of a function
f:A\to\mathbb{C}
is

|f|_{\mathcal{B}}:=\sup_{a\in\alpha^{*}}|f|_{\Lip(a,d^{\prime})},

where
|f|_{\Lip(a,d^{\prime})}
is the Lipschitz constant of
f
on
a
. The Lipschitz norm is then given by

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}:=\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}+|f|_{\mathcal{B}},

and the space of Lipschitz functions
\mathcal{B}
by
\{f:A\to\mathbb{C},\ \left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}<+\infty\}
.

Finally, we write
\mathcal{B}_{0}
for the kernel of
\mu
in
\mathcal{B}
.

A typical result (see
[
21
, Proposition 1.1.17 and Corollary 1.1.21]
) is then:

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
(A,\alpha,d^{\prime},\mu,T)
be a Gibbs-Markov map with expansivity constant
\Lambda>1
. The transfer operator
\mathcal{L}
acts on
\mathcal{B}
, with essential spectral radius
\rho_{\ess}(\mathcal{L}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})\leq\Lambda^{-1}
.

If
(A,\mu,T)
is also mixing, then the spectral radius of
\mathcal{L}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}_{0}
is strictly less than
1
: there exist constants
C>0
and
\rho\in(0,1)
such that
\left\|\mathcal{L}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{0}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}}\leq C\rho^{n}
.

Another important feature of Gibbs-Markov maps is that they behave very well under induction. Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a recurrent and ergodic Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map, and
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be finite. Following the strategy of e.g.
[
51
, Lemma 1.6]
, one may endow the system
([\Sigma],\mu_{[\Sigma]},T_{[\Sigma]})
with a Gibbs-Markov structure, with expansivity and distortion constants independent from
\Sigma
. From there one could get a family of Banach spaces depending on
\Sigma
. However, we shall not use directly this construction, but choose a closely related strategy: we make all the transfer operators
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}
act on the same Banach space, with relevant bounds (such as the essential spectral radius) uniform in
\Sigma
. This let us compare more easily all the operators
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}
.

We associate to
([\Sigma],\mu_{[\Sigma]},T_{[\Sigma]})
a Banach space of Lipschitz functions

\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma]}:=\left\{f:[\Sigma]\to\mathbb{C},\ f(\cdot,i)\in\mathcal{B}\ \forall i\in\Sigma\right\},

(1.5)

endowed with the norm
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma]}}=\max_{i\in I}\left\|f(\cdot,i)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}
. We denote by
\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma],0}
the kernel of
\mu_{[\Sigma]}
.

Not that the spaces
\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma]}
depend only on
\Sigma
through its cardinality: if two subsets
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
have the same cardinality, then
\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma_{1}]}
and
\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma_{2}]}
are naturally isomorphic.
By Proposition
A.8
, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}
acts quasi-compactly on
\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma]}
, with essential spectral radius at most
\Lambda^{-1}
. In particular, by ergodicity,
1
is a simple eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}
corresponding to constant functions, so that
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]})\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma],0}
is invertible.

1.4
Shapes and matrices

1.4.1
Limit shapes

As we argued in the introduction, it seems extremely hard to state anything non-trivial about the matrix of transition probabilities
P_{[\Sigma]}
for any fixed
\Sigma
. Instead, we shall be interested in subsets
\Sigma
whose sites are very far apart. We formalize this by fixing a finite set
I
, considering a family of injections
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
whose values get apart when
\varepsilon
vanishes, and looking at the matrix of transition probabilities of
[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]:=[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(I)]
.

A simple way of generating such functions
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is to fix some injection
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and to define
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)
as one of the points of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
closest to
\varepsilon^{-1}\sigma(i)
– or, more generally, any point at distance
o(\varepsilon^{-1})
of
\varepsilon^{-1}\sigma(i)
. As
\varepsilon
vanishes, the subsets
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}(I)
become growing realizations of a fixed shape (see Figure
2
).

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a canonical bijection
\sigma_{\varepsilon*}:(x,i)\mapsto(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i))
between
[I]:=A\times I
and
[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]=A\times\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. We use this bijection to transport objects from
[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]
to
[I]
:

•

\mu_{I}:=|I|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I}\mu\otimes\delta_{i}
is conjugated with
\mu_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}
and does not depend on
\varepsilon
;

•

T_{\varepsilon}:[I]\to[I]
is conjugated with
T_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}
;

•

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\curvearrowright\mathbb{L}^{p}(|I],\mu_{I})
is conjugated with
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}\curvearrowright\mathbb{L}^{p}(|\Sigma],\mu_{[\Sigma]})
;

•

\mathcal{B}_{I}
is conjugated with
\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}
, and by definition does not depend on
\varepsilon
;

•

P_{\varepsilon}
is conjugated with
P_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}
, etc.

The general rule is that objects defined on
[I]
have
I
as a subscript if they do not depend on
\varepsilon
,
and
\varepsilon
as a subscript if they do. This point of view allows us to keep some objects fixed (the space, the measure, the Banach space
\mathcal{B}_{I}
, and later on functions) while
\varepsilon
, and thus
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
, changes.

1.4.2
Transfer operator and transition probabilities

The matrices
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
can be recovered from the Koopman operators

(\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
. Given
\varepsilon>0
and
i
,
j\in I
,

\displaystyle P_{\varepsilon,ij}

\displaystyle=\mu\left([i]\cap T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([j])\right)

\displaystyle=|I|\int_{[I]}\mathbf{1}_{[i]}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{1}_{[j]})\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}.

(1.6)

Using transfer operators, this relationship can be rewritten

P_{\varepsilon,ij}=|I|\int_{[I]}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{1}_{[i]})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[j]}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I},

(1.7)

and made more systematic by introducing an averaging operator. Let:

\Pi_{*}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})&\to&\mathbb{C}^{I}\\
f&\mapsto&\left(\int_{A}f(x,i)\;{\rm d}\mu(x)\right)_{i\in I}\end{array}\right.,

which to a function
f
associate its averages over each
[i]
, and define a right inverse
\Pi^{*}

of
\Pi_{*}
:

\Pi^{*}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{C}^{I}&\to&\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})\\
f&\mapsto&\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[i]}\end{array}\right..

The operators
\Pi_{*}
and
\Pi^{*}
satisfy the relation
\Pi_{*}\Pi^{*}=\Id_{\mathbb{C}^{I}}
, while

\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*}
is a rank
|I|
averaging operator on
\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})
:

(\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*}f)(x,i)=\int_{A}f(y,i)\;{\rm d}\mu(y).

Equations (
1.6
) and (
1.7
) can then be recast as

P_{\varepsilon}=\Pi_{*}\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*}\text{ and }{\vphantom{P}}^{\mathit{t}}{P}_{\varepsilon}=\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*}.

(1.8)

1.4.3
Asymptotics of the transition matrices and irreductibility

By
[
40
, Lemma 3.17]
, if the minimal distance between distinct sites in
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
increases to
+\infty
when
\varepsilon
vanishes, then the transitions from one site to another becomes increasingly unlikely, so that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}P_{\varepsilon}=\Id
.
In this article, we are interested in the next order asymptotics of the family
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
when
\varepsilon
vanishes. By Lemma
1.4
, the matrices
P_{\varepsilon}
are bi-stochastic, so that the differences
(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})
are what we shall call
bi-L-matrices
. In other words, up to a sign, bi-L-matrices make up the tangent cone at
\Id
to the space of bi-stochastic matrices.

Definition 1.8

(L-matrix)
.

A
L-matrix
is a square matrix such that
3
3

3

The usual definition of L-matrix does not require that the sum of each line be zero.
:

•

R_{ii}\geq 0
for all
i\in I
;

•

R_{ij}\leq 0
for
i\neq j\in I
;

•

\sum_{j\in I}R_{ij}=0
for all
i\in I
.

We shall call a L-matrix a
bi-L-matrix
if, in addition,
\sum_{i\in I}R_{ij}=0
for all
j\in I
.

A L-matrix is
irreducible
if, for all
i
,
j\in I
, there exists a finite sequence
i=i_{0}
,
i_{1}
,
\ldots
,
i_{n}=j
such that
R_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}<0
for all
0\leq k<n
.

Using Perron-Frobenius theory, eigenvalues of a L-matrix always have non-negative real part. For irreducible L-matrices, we can be more accurate.

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
R
be a
n\times n
irreducible L-matrix. Then:

•

0
is a simple eigenvalue of
R
with eigenfunction
\mathbf{1}
.

•

there exists a unique probability vector
\nu
such that
\nu R=0
.

•

the matrix
R
acts on
\Ker(\nu)
, and
\Sp(R\curvearrowright\Ker(\nu))\subset\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ \Re(z)>0\}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be an irreducible L-matrix. Set
t:=\max_{i}R_{ii}
. Then
\Id-t^{-1}R
is an
irreducible nonnegative matrix. The lemma follows from Perron-Frobenius theory applied to
\Id-t^{-1}R
.
∎

Let us write
R_{0}
for the restriction of
R
to
\Ker(\nu)
. A L-matrix
R
is never invertible. However, if
R
is also irreducible, then
R_{0}
is invertible. Given an irreducible L-matrix
R
, we shall write

\rho_{R}:=\min\{\Re(\lambda):\ \lambda\in\Sp(R\curvearrowright\Ker(\nu))\}=\min\{\Re(\lambda):\ \lambda\in\Sp(R_{0})\}.

We shall often assume that the family
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
admits a first order asymptotics at
\varepsilon=0
.

Hypothesis 1.10

(Asymptotics of the transition matrices)
.

There exists an irreducible bi-L-matrix
R
such that

P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon).

1.4.4
Asymptotics of the potential and irreductibility

One issue with Hypothesis
1.10
is that we do not know
a priori
the matrices
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
; indeed, our goal is to compute them! As described in Subsection
1.1
, what can be computed are potentials. We shall now define matrices related to these potential, as well as a related irreducibility condition.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension satisfying Hypothesis
1.2
. By the discussion at the very end of Sub-subsection
1.3.2
, for any function
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a unique function
g\in\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
, its associated potential, such that
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})g=f
.

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}
be the canonical scalar – and not Hermitian – product on
\mathbb{C}^{I}
. In order to get a finite-dimensional version of
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}
, we define an operator acting on
\mathbb{C}^{I}_{0}=\left\{f:I\to\mathbb{C},\ \sum_{i\in I}f_{i}=0\right\}=\Ker(\mu_{I})
by:

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}:=\langle\Pi_{*}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f),g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

where the inverse
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}
is taken in
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
. This operator is well-defined
since
\Pi^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{I}_{0})\subset\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
.

The operator
Q_{\varepsilon}
is characterized by the integrals:

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=|I|\int_{[I]}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\left(\Pi^{*}(f)\right)\cdot\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}=|I|\langle(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f),\Pi^{*}(g)\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}([I],\mu_{I})}.

(1.9)

Remark 1.11
.

Let us justify our choice of definition for
Q_{\varepsilon}
. We want to adapt the construction we have for Markov maps to dynamical systems, using the Koopman operator
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
instead of the Markov operator. Since the Koopman operator does not acts well on function spaces, we introduce its dual, defined by Equation (
1.4
). The operator
\Pi_{*}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\Pi^{*}
is thus a discretization of the potential operator associated with the dual of
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
. We make it act of the left to cancel those dualities, so that the resulting operator
Q_{\varepsilon}
acts as a discretization of the potential operator associated with
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
.

As we formulated asymptotics and irreducibility conditions for the family
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
,
let us do so for the family
(Q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
.

Hypothesis 1.12

(Asymptotics of the potential matrices)
.

There exists a matrix
S
acting on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
such that

Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}S+o(\varepsilon^{-1}).

Definition 1.13

(Irreducible potentials)
.

A matrix
S
acting on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
is said to be irreducible if, for all

i\neq j\in I
,

\langle(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j}),S(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}>0.

The notion of irreducibility of Definition
1.13
is very easy to check in the computations of Section
6
, and quite convenient in the proof of Theorem
0.1
. In the later theorem, we shall conclude that
S=R_{0}^{-1}
for an irreducible bi-L-matrix
R
; it is thus expected that this notion of irreducibility is related to the notion of irreducibility of bi-L-matrices of Sub-subsection
1.4.3
.

Lemma 1.14
.

Let
R
be an irreducible bi-L-matrix in the sense of Definition
1.8
. Then
R_{0}^{-1}
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
.

Proof.

Our proof is probabilistic in nature, using potential theory for finite state Markov chains. Let
I
be the index set of the matrix
R
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
such that
P:=\Id-\varepsilon R
is a stochastic matrix. Since
R
is irreducible, so is
P
. Since
R
is a bi-L-matrix,
P
preserves the counting measure
on
I
.

Let
i\neq j\in I
. Let
P_{\{i,j\}}
be the transition matrix for the induced Markov chain on
\{i,j\}
. Then, by
[
41
, Corollary 1.1]
, and using the fact that all solutions of the Poisson equation on
\{i,j\}
differ by a constant since
P
(and thus
P_{\{i,j\}}
) is irreducible,

\sum_{k\in\{i,j\}}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})(k)\cdot(\Id-P)^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})(k)=\sum_{k\in\{i,j\}}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})(k)\cdot(\Id-P_{\{i,j\}})^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})(k).

(1.10)

On the one hand,
(\Id-P)^{-1}=\varepsilon^{-1}R_{0}^{-1}
. On the other hand,
since
P_{\{i,j\}}
is stochastic, irreducible and preserves the counting measure, there exist
t>0

such that

P_{\{i,j\}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1-t&t\\
t&1-t\end{array}\right).

In particular,
P_{\{i,j\}}f=(1-2t)f
for all
f\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\{i,j\}}
.
Equation (
1.10
) becomes:

\langle(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j}),R_{0}^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\varepsilon\langle(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j}),(2t)^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{\varepsilon}{t}>0.\qed

1.5
Strategy and main results

Now that all the necessary objects have been introduced, let us present the strategy we follow
and the main results that come along. We recall that, as sketched at the end of Sub-subsection
1.1.1
, the strategy to compute the Markov kernel
P_{\Sigma}
on
[\Sigma]
from a Markov kernel
\widetilde{P}
on
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
has three main steps:

•

the balayage identity, which relates
(\Id-\widetilde{P})^{-1}
and
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
;

•

the ability to solve the Poisson equation on
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
, using the Fourier transform;

•

the ability to invert
(\Id-P_{\Sigma})^{-1}
, or at least extract some information from it.

1.5.1
Koopman operator and Poisson equation

First, one must choose the right Markov kernel. A first choice is to use

\widetilde{P}_{x}:=\delta_{\widetilde{T}(x)},

whose action on functions is the Koopman operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
.

However, we then want to apply Theorem
1.1
, which requires us to find solutions
g\in\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu})
to the equation

f\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma]}=(\Id-\widetilde{P})(g)=(\Id-\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})(g)=g-g\circ\widetilde{T}\quad\widetilde{\mu}-\text{almost everywhere},

(1.11)

where
f\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma]}
is supported on
[\Sigma]
. The existence of solutions to this equation means that
f\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma]}
is a coboundary for
\widetilde{T}
. Let us assume for now that the system
(A,\mu,T)
is one of the simplest examples of Gibbs-Markov map: a subshift of finite type endowed with a Gibbs measure. Then the regularity of
g
can be improved and the coboundary equation holds everywhere using a classical argument
[
21
, Chapter 1.2]
.

On the other hand, we want to take
f\in\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma],0}
, hence Lipschitz. By Theorem
1.1
, we then have
\mu_{[\Sigma]}
-almost everywhere

f=g_{|[\Sigma]}-g_{|[\Sigma]}\circ T_{[\Sigma]},

so that

\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}(f)=(\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}-\Id)(g_{|[\Sigma]}).

Then, since
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]})
in invertible on
\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma],0}
, the later equation admits a solution

g_{0}:=-(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}(f)\in\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma]}.

As the kernel of the action of
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]})
on
\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([\Sigma],\mu_{[\Sigma]})
is the space of constant functions by ergodicity,
g_{|[\Sigma]}-g_{0}
is constant almost everywhere, so that
g_{|[\Sigma]}
admits a Lipschitz version.

The same reasoning goes through with any finite
\Sigma\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
and yields that
g_{|[\Sigma^{\prime}]}
admits a Lipschitz version on
[\Sigma^{\prime}]
, whence
g
admits a locally Lipschitz version on
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
. In particular, taking for
g
this continuous version, the equality

f\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma]}=g-g\circ\widetilde{T}

holds everywhere, and not only
\widetilde{\mu}
-almost everywhere. As a consequence, for any point
(x,p)\in[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
of period
n\geq 1
,

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(f\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma]})\circ\widetilde{T}^{k}(x,p)=0.

(1.12)

This gives countably many obstructions to the existence of solutions to Equation (
1.11
). In addition, if the sites of
\Sigma
are far apart and
p\in\Sigma
, then there exist periodic orbits which intersect
[\Sigma]
only in
[p]
. Then, if
f
is constant on
[p]
, the sum of Equation (
1.12
) is zero if and only if
f(\cdot,p)\equiv 0
. As this holds for all
p\in\Sigma
, we get
f\equiv 0
: the only function
f
which is constant on all sites and satisfy the coboundary equation is the null function.

A solution of this quandary is to use not the Koopman operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
, but its dual
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
.

1.5.2
Transfer operator and Poisson equation

The transformation
\widetilde{T}
is assumed to be ergodic and recurrent, which implies that the Markov kernel
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
is also ergodic and recurrent. The operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
is dual to
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
with respect to
\widetilde{\mu}
, and thus is also ergodic and recurrent by
[
41
]
. The relevant version of the balayage identity is
[
41
, Proposition 0.1]
:

Proposition 1.15

(Balayage identity for the transfer operator)
.

Let
(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be measure-preserving, with
\widetilde{\mu}
a recurrent
\sigma
-finite measure. Let
B\subset\widetilde{A}
be such that
0<\widetilde{\mu}(B)\leq+\infty
. Let
f
,
g\in\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\mu})
be such that
f\equiv 0
on
B^{c}
and:

(\Id-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(g)=f.

Then:

(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{B})(g_{|B})=f_{|B}.

Note that, in Section
5
, we shall actually use the more general
[
41
, Lemma 1.7]
, which applies to equations such as
(\Id-z\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(g)=f
with
|z|<1
. This generalization is only used to deal with convergence issues.

By the discussion at the end of Sub-subsection
1.3.2
, for any
f\in\mathcal{B}_{[\Sigma],0}
, one can find a locally Lipschitz solution
g
to the equation
(\Id-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(g)=f
, which makes the balayage identity potentially useful. We still have two hurdles to cross:

•

If we can construct (approximations of) solutions to the equation
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\Sigma})(g)=f_{|[\Sigma]}
, how do we use them to extract data on
\mathcal{L}_{\Sigma}
, and in particular an approximation of the transition matrix
P_{\Sigma}
?

•

How do we construct (approximations of) solutions to the equation
(\Id-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(g\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma]})=f
?

Both of these problems will be addressed in an asymptotic regime, when the sites of
\Sigma
are far away one from the others. We delve deeper into these questions in the next two sub-subsections.

1.5.3
Towards the main theorem

Our solution to the first of these two problem is Theorem
0.1
. Let us recall that it states that, for an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map and a family
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, there is equivalence between:

•

there exists an irreducible (in the sense of Definition
1.8
) bi-L-matrix
R
such that
P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
;

•

there exists an irreducible (in the sense of Definition
1.13
) matrix
S
such that
Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}S+o(\varepsilon^{-1})
.

If in addition any of these properties holds, then
S=R_{0}^{-1}
.

Remark 1.16

(Theorem
0.1
and averaging operator)
.

Using the operators
\Pi_{*}
and
\Pi^{*}
as well as transfer operators, Theorem
0.1

states that, under an irreducibility condition, the finite-dimensional operators

(\Id-\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*})^{-1}
and
\Pi_{*}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\Pi^{*}
are equivalent.

Remark 1.17

(Intrinsic version of Theorem
0.1
)
.

The statement of Theorem
0.1
depends both on the parametrization of the subsets
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
and the precise speed of convergence of
P_{\varepsilon}
to
\Id
. While easy to state and convenient for computations, a more intrisic version can be crafted. First, notice that the irreductibility conditions
1.10
and
1.12
are satisfied on open cones in the relevant subspaces of
M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, or in other words, on projectively open sets. We can then restate Theorem
0.1
as follows.

Consider an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map and a family of finite subsets
(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
all of the same size and such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\min_{p\neq q\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}|p-q|=+\infty.

Then there is equivalence between:

•

(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is projectively relatively compact for Hypothesis
1.10
.

•

(Q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is projectively relatively compact for Hypothesis
1.12
.

If either condition is satisfied, then
(I-P_{\varepsilon})^{-1}_{|\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}Q_{\varepsilon}
.

The matrix
Q_{\varepsilon}
can be computed when one evaluates the quantities

\langle\Pi_{*}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f),g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)},

for
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
. Theorem
0.1
gives the desired asymptotics for
R
. The initial infinite-dimensional problem of inverting
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}
is reduced to the finite-dimensional problem of inverting
Q_{\varepsilon}
.

The proof of Theorem
0.1
spans three sections. First, it will be very convenient to assume that the induced system
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
is mixing. This shall be denoted as a separate hypothesis:

Hypothesis 1.18

(Mixing)
.

The system
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
is mixing.

Section
2
is devoted to the proof of the direct implication in Theorem
0.1
under Hypothesis
1.18
:

Lemma 1.19
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map (Hypothesis
1.2
). Let
I
be finite and, and let
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Assume furthermore that
([0],\mu_{[0]},T_{[0]})
is mixing (Hypothesis
1.18
).

If there exists an irreducible bi-L-matrix
R
such that
P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
(Hypothesis
1.10
), then
Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}R_{0}^{-1}+o(\varepsilon^{-1})
. In addition,
R_{0}^{-1}
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
.

Lemma
1.19
is the heart of the proof of Theorem
0.1
. Its proof is inspired by works on the hitting time of small target for hyperbolic systems
[
42
,
26
]
and invariant cones for fast-slow systems
[
12
]
; we refer the reader to the introduction of Section
2
for more details.

Once this is done, in Section
3
we shall remove this mixing assumption.
If
([0],\mu_{[0]},T_{[0]})
is not mixing, then it has a decomposition into periodic components. This step requires us to understand what effects the existence of such periodic components have on the whole extension
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
; to our knowledge, these structural results (in particular Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
) are new and of independent interest.

This analysis is independent from Section
2
. That said, we think it is clearer to give the proof of Lemma
1.19
with the additional mixing assumption, and then point out the differences non-mixing implies, than to write directly the proof of Lemma
1.19
in a non-mixing context. At the end of Section
3
, we shall have proved:

Lemma 1.20
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov

\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map (Hypothesis
1.2
).
Let
I
be finite and, and choose
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}

for all
\varepsilon>0
.

If there exists an irreducible bi-L-matrix
R
such that
P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)

(Hypothesis
1.10
), then
Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}R_{0}^{-1}+o(\varepsilon^{-1})
.
In addition,
R_{0}^{-1}
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
.

The final step is to reverse the implication, so as to get Theorem
0.1
. This is the goal of Section
4
. This uses the
|I|=2
case of the theorem (which is deduced directly from Lemma
1.20
) and a compactness argument.

1.5.4
Applications

We are given an ergodic an recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map and a family
(\sigma_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
of injection from
I
into
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. All is left is, given
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\Sigma}
, to estimate the integrals

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=|I|\langle(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f),\Pi^{*}(g)\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}([I],\mu_{I})}

when
\varepsilon
vanishes. Thanks to the balayage identity, this reduces formally to the computation of the quantities (identifying
I
with
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
)

\langle\Pi_{*}(\Id-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\left(\Pi^{*}(f)\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}\right),\Pi^{*}(g)\mathbf{1}_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu})}.

As with random walks, this can be done using the Fourier transform. The main difference is that this operation make so-called
twisted transfer operators
appear.

Definition 1.21

(Twisted transfer operators)
.

For
w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, define the twisted transfer operator:

\mathcal{L}_{w}(\cdot):=\mathcal{L}(e^{i\langle w,F\rangle}\cdot),

(1.13)

which acts on
\mathbb{L}^{p}(A,\mu)
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
, as well as on
\mathcal{B}
.

Our applications are done in two steps. In Section
5
, we shall use these twisted transfer operators to give a general expression for the quantities
\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}
. Proposition
5.3
states that, in our setting, for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}(g)\left(\int_{A}(\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\right)\;{\rm d}w,

where
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}(g)
is the Fourier transform of
(g\circ\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{-1})\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}
.

In the same spirit as the Nagaev-Guivarc’h proof of the central limit theorem for dynamical systems
[
36
,
37
,
22
]
, when the sites are far apart, the main contribution to these integrals will be given by the main eigenvalue
\lambda_{w}
of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
, for
w
close to
0
. We shall prove that, for any small enough neighborhood
U
of
0
,

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}} \int_{U}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}(g)\frac{1+\delta_{w}}{1-\rho\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}w+O_{U}(1)\left\|f\right\|\cdot\left\|g\right\|,

with
\delta_{w}
a small error term.

In Section
6
, we apply the later equation to different classes of jump function
F
when
\sigma_{t}(i)=t\sigma(i)+o(1)
for all
i\in I
. For
d=2
and
\left\|F\right\|\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(A,\mu)
, this yields Proposition
0.2
. For
d=1
and
F
in the basin of attraction of a Cauchy random variable
4
4

4

In addition to some technical constraints
, we get a similar asymptotics, stated in Proposition
6.8
. We are also able to give asymptotics for
P_{\varepsilon}
when
d=1
and
F
is square-integrable, in Proposition
6.1
. Finally, we give some partial results when
d=1
and
F
is in the basin of attraction of a Lévy random variable (i.e. when
F
has regularly varying tails of index in
(0,1)
). We are able to give an explicit expression for
S
, stated in Proposition
6.4
, but we could not find an explicit expression for its inverse. We are still able to compute the asymptotics for a fixed extension and fixed
(\sigma_{t})_{t>0}
, as done in Examples
6.5
and
6.6
.

2
From transition probabilities to potential

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
1.19
: if
([0],\mu_{[0]},T_{[0]})
is mixing and
P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
for some irreducible bi-L-matrix
R
, then
Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}R_{0}^{-1}+o(\varepsilon^{-1})
.

The transition matrix
P_{\varepsilon}
can be computed from the transfer operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
using the relation
{\vphantom{P}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{P}_{\varepsilon}=\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*}
(Equation (
1.8
)). From another point of view,
\Pi^{*}{\vphantom{P}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{P}_{\varepsilon}\Pi_{*}=(\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
could be seen as a finite-rank approximation of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. Such an approximation is reminiscent of works on metastable states by Keller and Liverani
[
29
]
(for two communicating systems), as well as by Dolgopyat and Wright
[
17
]
(for interval maps).

At first sight, this approximation is not very good: in general,
\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}}
is of order
1
, because of the non-trivial behaviour of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
on each site
[i]
. A solution would be to focus on functions which are constant on each site; however, this subspace is not stable under
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
.

On each site, the effect of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is roughly that of
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}
, which mixes exponentially quickly. The transitions between sites introduce an error of order
\varepsilon
. The interaction of these two dynamical effects imply the existence of cones of functions
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
(functions which are
\varepsilon
-close to being constant on each site) which are invariant under
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. By restricting ourselves to such cones, we shall be able improve the bounds on
(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. The goal of Subsection
2.1
is thus to prove that such cones are stable under the action of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, and moreover to prove a cone contraction property
which shall be instrumental in the next steps.

Remark 2.1

(Fast-slow systems)
.

Such families of cones of functions have some precedent in ergodic theory. One feature of the sequence of operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is that the evolution of
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 0}
has two characteristic time scales. At a time scale of
\Theta(1)
, the mixing property of
([0],\mu_{[0]},T_{[0]})
homogeneizes each site
[i]
, but there is little communication between distinct sites. The transitions between sites occur at a time scale of
\Theta(\varepsilon^{-1})
. When
\varepsilon
vanishes, these two time scales become decoupled, so that the transitions become independent.

The existence of two distinct time scales is also a feature of fast-slow systems. For instance,
consider a family of maps
F_{\varepsilon}
acting on
\Omega\times\mathbb{S}_{1}
such that

F_{\varepsilon}(x,\theta)=(f_{\varepsilon}(x,\theta),\theta+\varepsilon g(x,\theta))

and
x\mapsto f_{\varepsilon}(x,\theta)
is a family of uniformly hyperbolic transformations on
\Omega
. Then the first variable,
x
, evolves on a time scale of
\Theta(1)
, and the second variable,
\theta
, on a time scale of
\Theta(\varepsilon^{-1})
. When
\varepsilon
vanishes, these two time scales become decoupled, so that the evolution of
\theta
is averaged over the values of
x
.

In the context of fast-slow systems, such parametric families of cones – or, similarly, standard families – have been introduced. We refer the interested reader to
[
12
]
for an exposition of these techniques, and
[
15
,
9
,
13
]
for applications to partially hyperbolic systems and Sinai billiard maps. In particular, we stress the similarity between
[
12
, Proposition 3.1]
and Corollary
2.6
.

Once we have proved the stability of these cones, the error term between
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
and
(\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
will be improved. In Subsection
2.2
, together with a version of the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, this shall let us control the error between their products on a time scale of
\Theta(\varepsilon^{-1})
. In particular, if
f
is constant on each site, then, for all positive
t
, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}(f)
converges to
\Pi^{*}e^{-t{\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R}}\Pi_{*}f
. This exponential decay is typical of previous work on the hitting time of small targets
[
42
,
26
]
. Let us also point out
[
30
,
6
]
for settings where rare events correspond to strong changes in the system, and where the behaviour of the system after those changes has to be controlled.

Finally, in Subsection
2.3
, the exponential contraction at time scale
\Theta(1)
together with a coupling argument gets us exponential bounds on the decay of correlations which are uniform in
\varepsilon^{-1}t
. Note that this coupling argument is classical for Markov chains
[
35
, Chapter 5]
and has a long tradition in ergodic theory (present for instance in most applications of standard pairs; see e.g.
[
10
]
for applications to Sinai billiards maps). Once this is done, we are able to relate
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}
with the inverse of
R^{-1}
through a Laplace transform, finishing the proof of Lemma
1.19
.

2.1
Cone contraction at time scale
\Theta(1)

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map (Hypothesis
1.2
). Let
I
be finite and, and choose
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}

for all
\varepsilon>0
.

As announced earlier, we introduce cones of functions which are
\varepsilon
-close to being constant on each site, and prove that they are stable under some iterate of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, as well as a cone contraction property.

Definition 2.2

(Cones of functions)
.

For all
K\geq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
, let:

C_{K}(\varepsilon):=\left\{f:\ [I]\to\mathbb{R}:\ f\geq 0\text{ and }\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq K\varepsilon\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\right\}.

These subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{I}
are indeed convex cones, as we can quickly check:

Lemma 2.3
.

For all
K
,
\varepsilon\geq 0
, the set
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
is a convex cone.

Proof.

Let
K
,
\varepsilon\geq 0
. Given
f\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
and
\lambda\geq 0
,

\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})(\lambda f)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=\lambda\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq\lambda K\varepsilon\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}=K\varepsilon\left\|\lambda f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})},

so that
\lambda f\in\mathcal{B}_{I}
. In addition, for all
f
,
g\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
and
t\in[0,1]
:

\displaystyle\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})((1-t)f+tg)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle\leq(1-t)\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}+t\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})g\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle\leq K\varepsilon\left((1-t)\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}+t\left\|g\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\right)

\displaystyle=K\varepsilon\left\|(1-t)f+tg\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})},

where we used the fact that
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
is linear on nonnegative functions. Hence
(1-t)f+tg
also belongs to
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
.
∎

Another fundamental property of these cones is that, since any function in
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
is close for the
\mathcal{B}_{I}
norm to the finite dimensional space of functions constant on each
[i]
, many norms are equivalent on
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
. In particular,
we can control the
\mathcal{B}_{I}
norm (the strongest norm we shall use) with the
\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})
norm (the weakest norm we shall use).

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
K
,
\varepsilon\geq 0
. For all
f\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
,

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq(|I|+K\varepsilon)\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}.

(2.1)

Proof.

Let
K
,
\varepsilon\geq 0
. For all
f\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}+\left\|\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*}f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle\leq K\varepsilon\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}+|I|\left\|\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*}f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}

\displaystyle=(|I|+K\varepsilon)\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})},

where we used the fact that
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\infty}\leq|I|\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
on functions which are constant on each
[i]
.
∎

Given these preliminary results, we shall now prove that, under the assumptions of Lemma
1.19
, the cones
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
are contracted under some iterate of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
whenever
K
is large enough. Taking
\sigma=1
in the following proposition also yield their stability.

Proposition 2.5
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.10
.

For all
\sigma>0
, there exist
K_{\sigma}
,
n_{\sigma}\geq 0
such that, for all
K\geq K_{\sigma}
, for all
\varepsilon>0
and
n\geq n_{\sigma}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{\sigma K}(\varepsilon).

Proof.

Let
K\geq 0
and
\varepsilon
,
n
be positive. Define:

B_{n}:=\{(x,i)\in[I]:\ T_{\varepsilon}^{k}(x,i)\in[i]\ \forall\ 0\leq k\leq n\}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigcap_{k=0}^{n}T_{\varepsilon}^{-k}([i]),

the set of points which stay in the same site until time
n
. By Hypothesis
1.10
, there exists a constant
C
such that, for all
i\in I
and all
\varepsilon>0
,

\mu(x\in A:\ T_{\varepsilon}(x,i)\notin[i])=\sum_{j\neq i}(\delta_{ij}-\varepsilon R_{ij}+o(\varepsilon))=-\varepsilon\sum_{j\neq i}R_{ij}+o(\varepsilon)\leq C\varepsilon.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mu_{I}\left(\left(\bigcap_{k=0}^{n}T_{\varepsilon}^{-k}([i])\right)^{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mu_{I}\left(x:\ T_{\varepsilon}^{0}(x,i),\ldots,T_{\varepsilon}^{k-1}(x,i)\in[i],\ T_{\varepsilon}^{k}(x,i)\notin[i]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mu_{I}\left(x:T_{\varepsilon}^{k-1}(x,i)\in[i],\ T_{\varepsilon}^{k}(x,i)\notin[i]\right)

\displaystyle=n\mu_{I}\left(x:T_{\varepsilon}(x,i)\notin[i]\right)

\displaystyle\leq C|I|^{-1}n\varepsilon.

Summing over
i\in I
yields
\mu_{I}(B_{n}^{c})\leq Cn\varepsilon
.

Let
i\in I
and
f\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
. As the transfer operator preserves nonnegative functions,
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f)\geq 0
. Let us focus on the regularity of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f)
.

First, by definition of
B_{n}
, given any point
(x,i)\in B_{n}
, the trajectories of the points
(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i))
,
T_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i))
,
\ldots
,
T_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}^{n-1}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i))
under
\widetilde{T}
all return to
[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)]
before hitting any
[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j)]
with
j\neq i
. Hence, by recursion, for all
0\leq k\leq n
and all
x\in B_{n}
,

T_{[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]}^{k}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i))=(T_{[0]}^{k}(x),\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)),

so that:

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}})(\cdot,i)=\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}((f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}})(\cdot,i)).

(2.2)

From Equation (
2.2
) we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f)(\cdot,i)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}})(\cdot,i)+\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}})(\cdot,i)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}((f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}})(\cdot,i))+\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}})(\cdot,i)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}(f(\cdot,i))-\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}((f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}})(\cdot,i))+\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}})(\cdot,i).

We shall now use Corollaries
A.7
and
A.9
to control each term. Stopping times are defined in terms of the canonical filtration on
(A,\mu,T)
, while
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
are defined
via
the extension
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
. We thus need to express some characteristics of the trajectories
(T_{\varepsilon}^{n}(x,i))_{n\geq 0}
in terms of cylinders for
(A,\mu,T)
, as is done in the proof of Proposition
A.8
.

Let
\varphi_{[0]}(x):=\inf\{n\geq 1:\ \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}S_{k}F(x)=0\}
. Then
\varphi_{[0]}
is a stopping time in the sense of Definition
A.2
. Since
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is measure-preserving and recurrent, the map
T_{\varphi_{[0]}}=T_{[0]}
preserves
\mu
and is ergodic. Finally, by Hypothesis
1.18
, the map
T_{[0]}
is also mixing for
\mu
. Hence we can apply Corollary
A.9
, which asserts that there exist constants
C>0
and
\rho\in(0,1)
such that, for all
i\in I
,

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}(f(\cdot,i))-\int_{A}f(\cdot,i)\;{\rm d}\mu\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}\leq C\rho^{n}\left\|f(\cdot,i)-\int_{A}f(\cdot,i)\;{\rm d}\mu\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}\leq C\rho^{n}\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}.

(2.3)

Let
i\in I
. The set
B_{n}^{c}\cap[I]
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra

\alpha(\varphi_{[0]}^{(n)})
, since, using the identification
[i]\simeq A
,

B_{n}\cap[I]=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n-1}T_{[0]}^{-k}\left(\left\{\exists 0\leq\ell<\varphi_{[0]}(x),\ \exists j\in I\setminus\{i\},\ S_{\ell}F(x)=\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)\right\}\right).

As
\varphi_{[0]}^{(n)}
is a stopping time by Lemma
A.3
, we can apply Corollary
A.7
to get, for some constant
C
and all
i\in I
,

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}((f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}})(\cdot,i))\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}\leq C\mu(B_{n}^{c}\cap[i])\left\|f(\cdot,i)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

(2.4)

In the same way, fix
i
,
j\in I
and let
\varphi_{j,n,\varepsilon}(x):=\inf\{\ell\geq 1:\ \Card\{S_{\ell}F(x)\in\{j^{\prime}-j:\ j^{\prime}\in I\}=n\}
. The set
B_{n}^{c}\cap[j]
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra
\alpha(\varphi_{j,n,\varepsilon})
. By Corollary
A.7
, for some constant
C
,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}\cap[j]})(\cdot,i)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{j,n,\varepsilon}}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}\cap[j]\cap\{S_{\varphi_{j,n,\varepsilon}}F=i-j\}})\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\leq C\mu(B_{n}^{c}\cap[j])\left\|f(\cdot,j)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=C|I|\mu_{I}(B_{n}^{c}\cap[j])\left\|f(\cdot,j)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Summing over
j\in I
finally yields

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbf{1}_{B_{n}^{c}})(\cdot,i)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}\leq C|I|\mu_{I}(B_{n}^{c})\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}.

(2.5)

Equations (
2.1
), (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) together yield, for some positive constant
C
and all
n\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f)(\cdot,i)-\int_{A}f(\cdot,i)\;{\rm d}\mu\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\leq C\rho^{n}\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}+C\mu_{I}(B_{n}^{c})\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle\leq C(K\rho^{n}+n)\varepsilon\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}.

(2.6)

As
T_{\varepsilon}
preserves
\mu_{I}
and
f
is nonnegative,
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f)\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
. By Equation (
2.6
),

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{C(K\rho^{n}+n)}(\varepsilon).

(2.7)

Now, we finish the proof of Proposition
2.5
. Let
\sigma>0
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\sigma\leq 1
. Let
n_{\sigma}\geq 1
be such that
C\rho^{n_{\sigma}}\leq\sigma/2
. Let
K_{\sigma}:=4C\sigma^{-1}n_{\sigma}
. Then, for all
K\geq K_{\sigma}
and
\varepsilon>0
, for all
n_{\sigma}\leq n\leq 2n_{\sigma}
, by Equation (
2.7
),

C(K\rho^{n}+n)\leq C(K\rho^{n_{\sigma}}+2n_{\sigma})\leq\frac{\sigma K}{2}+\frac{\sigma K_{\sigma}}{2}\leq\sigma K,

so that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{\sigma K}(\varepsilon)
.

By recursion, let us prove that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{mn_{\sigma}}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{K}(\varepsilon)
for all
m\geq 0
. This is true for
m=0
, and if it holds for some
m\geq 0
, then

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{(m+1)n_{\sigma}}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n_{\sigma}}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{mn_{\sigma}}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n_{\sigma}}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{\sigma K}(\varepsilon)\subset C_{K}(\varepsilon),

which finishes this recursion.

Finally, for all
n\geq n_{\sigma}
, since
n_{\sigma}\leq n-n_{\sigma}\lfloor n/n_{\sigma}\rfloor+n_{\sigma}\leq 2n_{\sigma}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n-n_{\sigma}\lfloor n/n_{\sigma}\rfloor+n_{\sigma}}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n_{\sigma}(\lfloor n/n_{\sigma}\rfloor-1)}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n-n_{\sigma}\lfloor n/n_{\sigma}\rfloor+n_{\sigma}}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{\sigma K}(\varepsilon).\qed

As a consequence, if
K
is large enough and up to the loss of a constant factor,
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
is stable under
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}
for all
n\geq 0
. This corollary shall give us a good control on
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}
provided we know that
f\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
.

Corollary 2.6
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.10
.

There exists
K_{1}
and
\lambda_{1}\geq 1
such that, for all
K\geq K_{1}
, for all
n\geq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{\lambda_{1}K}(\varepsilon).

Proof.

Let
n_{1}
,
K_{1}
be given by Proposition
2.5
. By Proposition
2.5
, for all
K\geq K_{1}
, for all
n\geq n_{1}
and all
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{K}(\varepsilon).

In addition, by Equation (
2.7
), for all
n\leq n_{1}
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{C(K+n_{1})}(\varepsilon).

We choose
\lambda_{1}:=C(1+n_{1}/K_{1})
.
∎

2.2
Product of matrices

Given
f\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
, Corollary
2.6
asserts that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f
is close to being constant on each site for all
n\geq 0
; the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is then well approximated by the finite rank operator
\Pi^{*}{\vphantom{P}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{P}_{\varepsilon}\Pi_{*}
. However, to understand what happens at time scale
\varepsilon^{-1}
, we need to control a composition of a large number (of order
\varepsilon^{-1}
) of such operators. To keep reasonably sharp error bounds, we use a
version of the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
N\geq 1
. There exists a neighbourhood
U
of
0
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})
and a constant
C>0
such that, for all matrices
A
and
B
in
U
,

\left\|\ln(e^{A}e^{B})-A-B\right\|\leq C\left\|[A,B]\right\|.

(2.8)

The ability to bound
\ln(e^{A}e^{B})-A-B
using only commutators is natural if one thinks about the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula:

e^{A}e^{B}=e^{A+B+\frac{1}{2}[A,B]+\frac{1}{12}[A,[A,B]]+\frac{1}{12}[B,[B,A]]+\ldots}.

We prove it using an integral version of this formula.

Proof.

Whenever
A
and
B
are small enough,
\ln(e^{A}e^{B})
can be written with an integral Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula
[
23
, Theorem 3.3]
:

\ln(e^{A}e^{B})=A+\int_{0}^{1}u\left(e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}\right)\;{\rm d}t\ (B),

where
u(z)=\frac{z\ln(z)}{z-1}
. Set:

v(z):=\frac{z\ln(z)-z+1}{(z-1)^{2}}=\frac{u(z)-1}{z-1},\quad w(z):=\frac{e^{z}-1}{z}.

The functions
v
and
w
are analytic on a neighbourhood of the identity, and for all small enough
A
and
B
:

\displaystyle\ln(e^{A}e^{B})-A-B

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}u\left(e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}\right)\;{\rm d}t\ (B)-B

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(u-1)\left(e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}\right)\;{\rm d}t\ (B)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}v\left(e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}\right)\ (e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}-1)\ (B)\;{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}v\left(e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}\right)\ (e^{\ad_{A}}-1)\ (B)\;{\rm d}t
