\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}v\left(e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}\right)\;{\rm d}t\ w(\ad_{A})\ ([A,B]).

Since both
v\left(e^{\ad_{A}}e^{t\ad_{B}}\right)
and
w(\ad_{A})
are uniformly bounded for

A
and
B
on a neighbourhood of the identity, we get the claim.
∎

Using Lemma
2.7
recursively, we can control the product of a large number (of order
\Theta(\varepsilon^{-1})
) of matrices which are
o(\varepsilon)
-close from a reference matrix
R
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
R\in M_{N}(\mathbb{C})
. Let
\omega
be a nonnegative function such that
\omega(\varepsilon)=_{\varepsilon\to 0}o(\varepsilon)
. Let
(R_{\varepsilon,n})_{\varepsilon>0,n\geq 0}
be a family of matrices in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that

\sup_{n\geq 0}\left\|R_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|\leq\omega(\varepsilon).

For all
\varepsilon>0
and
t\geq 0
, let:

M_{\varepsilon}(t):=\prod_{n=0}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor-1}(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,n}),

where the matrices are multiplied in any order. Then the family of matrix-valued functions
(M_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
converges to
t\mapsto e^{-tR}
when
\varepsilon
vanishes, uniformly on all compacts of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and for fixed
\omega
uniformly in
(R_{\varepsilon,n})_{\varepsilon>0,n\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Let
R
and
(R_{\varepsilon,n})_{\varepsilon>0,n\geq 0}
be as in the hypotheses of the lemma.
Define:

R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n}:=\ln(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,n})+\varepsilon R.

Note that
\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon):=\sup_{n\geq 0}\left\|R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|=O(\omega(\varepsilon))
.

Let
U
be a neighbourhood of
0
in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})
and
C>0
as given by Lemma
2.7
.
We put
K:=2C\max\{\left\|R\right\|,1\}
. Let
t_{0}
be small enough that
U
contains
\{-tR:\ 0\leq t\leq t_{0}\}
.

We claim that, for all small enough
\varepsilon
, for all
n\leq\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t_{0}\rfloor
,
the matrix
\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,k})
belongs to
U
, and:

\left\|\ln\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,k})\right)+n\varepsilon R\right\|\leq(1+Kn\varepsilon)\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\frac{(1+2K\varepsilon)^{n}-1}{2K\varepsilon}.

(2.9)

The claim is true for
n=0
, as the left-hand side vanishes. Assume that the claim holds for
some
n\leq\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t_{0}\rfloor-1
, and put:

S_{\varepsilon,n}:=\ln\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,k})\right)+n\varepsilon R.

Define in the same way
S_{\varepsilon,n+1}
. We assume without loss of generality that
\varepsilon
is small enough that
\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon
. By Lemma
2.7
,

\displaystyle\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n+1}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\ln\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n}(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,k})\right)+(n+1)\varepsilon R\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\ln\left(e^{-\varepsilon R+R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n}}e^{-n\varepsilon R+S_{\varepsilon,n}}\right)+(n+1)\varepsilon R\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|-\varepsilon R+R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n}-n\varepsilon R+S_{\varepsilon,n}+(n+1)\varepsilon R\right\|+C\left\|[-\varepsilon R+R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n},-n\varepsilon R+S_{\varepsilon,n}]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|+\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|+2C\left(\varepsilon\left\|R\right\|\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|+\left\|R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|+n\varepsilon\left\|R^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|\left\|R\right\|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|+K\varepsilon\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|+Kn\varepsilon\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+K\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq(1+2K\varepsilon)\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|+(1+Kn\varepsilon)\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon).

Using the recursion hypothesis, we get:

\displaystyle\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n+1}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq(1+2K\varepsilon)(1+Kn\varepsilon)\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\frac{(1+2K\varepsilon)^{n}-1}{2K\varepsilon}+(1+Kn\varepsilon)\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq(1+K(n+1)\varepsilon)\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\frac{(1+2K\varepsilon)^{n+1}-1}{2K\varepsilon}.

Hence, we get the bound we want on
S_{\varepsilon,n+1}
. In addition,
n+1\leq\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t_{0}\rfloor
, so that:

\left\|S_{\varepsilon,n+1}\right\|\leq(1+Kt_{0})e^{2Kt_{0}}\frac{\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)}{2K\varepsilon},

which converges to
0
uniformly for
1\leq n+1\leq\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t_{0}\rfloor
. In particular, if
\varepsilon
is small enough (depending on
t_{0}
but not on
n
),
S_{\varepsilon,n+1}
belongs to
U
. This finishes the proof of our intermediate claim.

As a consequence, for all small enough
\varepsilon
and all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, the matrix
M_{\varepsilon}(t)
belongs to
U
and satisfies the inequality:

\left\|M_{\varepsilon}(t)-e^{-tR}\right\|\leq(1+Kt_{0})e^{2Kt_{0}}\frac{\omega^{\prime}(\varepsilon)}{2K\varepsilon}+\left\|e^{-tR}\right\|\max_{s\in[0,\varepsilon]}\left\|e^{sR}-\Id\right\|,

where the right-hand side converges to
0
when
\varepsilon
vanishes. The family of functions
(F_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
thus converges to
t\mapsto e^{-tR}
uniformly on
[0,t_{0}]
. In addition, since the error term only depends on
(R_{\varepsilon,n})_{\varepsilon>0,n\geq 0}
through
\omega
, so the convergence is uniform in
(R_{\varepsilon,n})_{\varepsilon>0,n\geq 0}
at fixed
\omega
.

Finally, let
T>0
. Divide the interval
[0,T]
into a finite number of subintervals of length less that
t_{0}
,
taking an increasing sequence
0=s_{0}<s_{1}<\ldots<s_{\ell}=T
with
s_{i+1}-s_{i}<t_{0}
. On each such subinterval, the intermediate claim yields:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{s\in[s_{i},s_{i+1}]}\left\|M_{\varepsilon}(s)M_{\varepsilon}(s_{i})^{-1}-e^{-(s-s_{i})R}\right\|=0.

The concatenation of these bounds for
0\leq i\leq\ell
yields the uniform convergence of
M_{\varepsilon}
on
[0,T]
,
and ends the proof of Lemma
2.8
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

If the family
(R_{\varepsilon,n})_{\varepsilon>0,n\geq 0}
does not depend on
n
, which in our future application is the case for random walks, then the conclusion of Lemma
2.8
follows directly from the identity

M_{\varepsilon}(t)=e^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor\ln\left(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,0}\right)},

and the fact that
\ln\left(\Id-\varepsilon R+R_{\varepsilon,0}\right)=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
.

2.3
Cone contraction at time scale
\Theta(\varepsilon^{-1})

In this subsection we prove Lemma
1.19
. Let us recall briefly the strategy: thanks to the stability estimates of Subsection
2.1
, in particular Corollary
2.6
, and to Lemma
2.8
, we shall prove that, for all piecewise constant
f
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f=e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}f.

Then, a coupling argument referenced at the start of the section (for similar arguments, see e.g.
[
35
, Chapter 5]
in the context of Markov chains, and
[
10
]
in the context of hyperbolic dynamics and standard pairs) yields tension: for some constants
C
,
c>0
, if in addition
\int_{[I]}f\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}=0
, then

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f\right\|_{\infty}\leq Ce^{-ct}\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}.

Integrating over
t\geq 0
then relates
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}
, and thus
Q_{\varepsilon}
, with
R_{0}^{-1}
.

Remark 2.10

(Perturbative argument and the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
)
.

As already mentioned, another direction of attack for this problem is to try and adapt the perturbative methods of G. Keller and C. Liverani
[
29
]
, in particular in the form used by D. Dolgopyat and P. Wright
[
17
]
. In this setting, one views
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
as a perturbation of

\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{\oplus|I|}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{B}_{I}&\to&\mathcal{B}_{I}\\
(f(\cdot,i))_{i\in I}&\mapsto&(\mathcal{L}_{[0]}(f(\cdot,i)))_{i\in I}\end{array}\right.,

whose spectrum
\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{\oplus|I|}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}_{I})
is a
|I|
-fold copy of
\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{[0]}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})
.

This approach is likely to give a more accurate description of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, although with no incidence on Theorem
0.1
. For instance, under Hypothesis
1.18
, we can hope to get an eigendecomposition

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon},

with
\left\|\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq Ce^{-cn}
for some
C
,
c>0
and all
\varepsilon>0
, and
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
of finite rank, and almost conjugated with
(\Id-\varepsilon{\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})
. In particular, the spectrum of
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
should be
o(\varepsilon)
-close to that of
(\Id-\varepsilon{\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})
, with eigenfunctions close to those of
\Pi^{*}{\vphantom{R}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{R}\Pi_{*}
. If this holds, the bound of Equation
2.14
below may be improved to

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I,0}}\leq Ce^{-\rho\varepsilon n}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I,0}},

with
\rho<\rho_{R}
and
C
depends only on
\rho
.

1
\Lambda^{-1}
0
\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{[0]}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})
0
\rho_{R}
\Sp(R\curvearrowright\mathbb{C}^{I})
1
\Lambda^{-1}
0
\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}_{I})

Figure 4
:
Conjectural picture for the relationship between the spectra of
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}
,
R
and
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
with
|I|=5
. The spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is a perturbation of magnitude
\Theta(\varepsilon)
of a five-fold copy of
\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{[0]}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})
, and the spectrum of
R
can be recovered by zooming in a window of size
\varepsilon
around
1\in\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}_{I})
, then applying a central symmetry.

Let us finally mention the recent work of H. Tanaka
[
50
]
, who recently investigated such multidimensional spectral degenerescences in the context of equilibrium measures of subshifts of finite type.

Our first step is to show that we can indeed apply Lemma
2.8
in our context.

Proposition 2.11
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.10
.

For all
K\geq 0
, there exists a nonnegative function
\omega
such that
\omega(\varepsilon)=_{\varepsilon\to 0}o(\varepsilon)
, and with the following property.

Let
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of functions from
[I]
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
f_{\varepsilon}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
for all small enough
\varepsilon
. Then there exists a sequence of square matrices
(R_{\varepsilon,n})_{\varepsilon>0,n\geq 0}
such that

\sup_{n\geq 0}\left\|R_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|\leq\omega(\varepsilon)

and, for all
n\geq 0
,

\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}=(\Id-\varepsilon({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})+R_{\varepsilon,n})(\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}).

Proof.

Let
K\geq 0
and
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be as in the hypotheses of Proposition
2.11
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
K\geq K_{1}
, where
K_{1}
is given by Corollary
2.6
. By Corollary
2.6
, there exists
\lambda_{1}\geq 1
such that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\in C_{\lambda_{1}K}(\varepsilon)
for all small enough
\varepsilon
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\in C_{\lambda_{1}K}(\varepsilon)
and
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}=\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
,

\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{1}K\varepsilon\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}.

(2.10)

Set
g_{\varepsilon,n}:=(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}
. By construction,
\Pi_{*}g_{\varepsilon,n}=0
. For all
i
,
j\in I
,

\left|\int_{[j]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[j]}\circ T_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}\mu\right|\leq\left\|g_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|_{\infty}\mu(x\in A:\ T_{\varepsilon}(x,i)\in[j])=\left\|g_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|_{\infty}P_{\varepsilon,ij}.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
preserves
\mu_{I}
, for all
i\in I
,

\displaystyle\int_{[i]}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(g_{\varepsilon,n})\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=|I|\int_{[I]}\mathbf{1}_{[i]}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(g_{\varepsilon,n})\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=|I|\int_{[I]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[i]}\circ T_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in I}\int_{[j]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[i]}\circ T_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}\mu-\sum_{j\in I}\int_{[i]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[j]}\circ T_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{[i]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\;{\rm d}\mu+\sum_{j\neq i}\left(\int_{[j]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[i]}\circ T_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}\mu-\int_{[i]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[j]}\circ T_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}\mu\right),

so that, by Equation (
2.10
),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{[i]}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(g_{\varepsilon,n})\;{\rm d}\mu-\int_{[i]}g_{\varepsilon,n}\;{\rm d}\mu\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|g_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|_{\infty}\sum_{j\neq i}\left(P_{\varepsilon,ij}+P_{\varepsilon,ji}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{1}K\varepsilon\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}O(\varepsilon).

Since
\Pi_{*}g_{\varepsilon,n}=0
, this later inequality can be read as

\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}g_{\varepsilon,n}\right\|_{\infty}=KO(\varepsilon^{2})\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
.

Recall that
{\vphantom{P}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{P}_{\varepsilon}=\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*}
. We apply

\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
to
g_{\varepsilon,n}
:

\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}={\vphantom{P}}^{\mathit{t}}{P}_{\varepsilon}\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}+\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}g_{\varepsilon,n}=(\Id-\varepsilon({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})+o(\varepsilon))\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}+KO(\varepsilon^{2})\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}.

As
f_{\varepsilon}
is nonnegative,
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}=\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}
,
so that

\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}=(\Id-\varepsilon({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})+o(\varepsilon)+KO(\varepsilon^{2}))\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon},

and the error term
o(\varepsilon)+KO(\varepsilon^{2})
only depends on
K
.
∎

Our proximate goal is to understand the sequence
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 0}
when
f_{\varepsilon}
has average
0
. In order to apply Proposition
2.11
, we shall write a function with average
0
as the difference of two functions in
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
. This can be efficiently formalized by introducing an auxiliary family of norms on
\mathcal{B}_{I}
.

Let
K
,
\varepsilon>0
. Given a real function
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I}
, we define its
(K,\varepsilon)
-norm as

\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{+},f_{-}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)\\
f=f_{+}-f_{-}\end{subarray}}\max\{\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})},\left\|f_{-}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\}.

For complex-valued
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I}
, we put
\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}:=\max\{\left\|\Re f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon},\left\|\Im f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\}
. For instance, if
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
is constant on each
[i]
, then
\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}=\max\{\left\|\Re f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})},\left\|\Im f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\}/2
.

Lemma 2.12
.

For any
K
,
\varepsilon>0
, the
(K,\varepsilon)
-norm is equivalent to the
\mathcal{B}_{I}
-norm.

Proof.

Let
K
,
\varepsilon>0
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I}
with
\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}<+\infty
. Assume that
f
is real-valued. Let
\delta>0
, and write
f=f_{+}-f_{-}
with
\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}}
,
\left\|f_{-}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}+\delta
, and
f_{+}
,
f_{-}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
. Then
\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f_{+}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq K\varepsilon\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}}
, so that, by Lemma
2.4
,

\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq(|I|+K\varepsilon)\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq(|I|+K\varepsilon)(\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}+\delta)

and similarly for
f_{-}
. Hence,
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq 2(|I|+K\varepsilon)(\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}+\delta)
. Since this is true for all
\delta>0
, we get
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq 2(|I|+K\varepsilon)\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}
. If
f
is complex-valued, by taking real and imaginary parts, we get

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq 4(|I|+K\varepsilon)\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.

(2.11)

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I}
be real. Let
C:=1+2/(K\varepsilon)
. We choose
f_{+}:=f+C\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}
and
f_{-}:=C\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}
. Then
f_{-}\in C_{0}(\varepsilon)
and

\frac{2\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}}{K\varepsilon}\leq f_{+}\leq\frac{2(1+K\varepsilon)\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}}{K\varepsilon}.

We deduce from the lower bound on
f_{+}
:

\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f_{+}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq 2\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq K\varepsilon\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}.

Hence
f_{+}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
. Using the upper bound on
f_{+}
,

\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq\frac{2(1+K\varepsilon)}{K\varepsilon}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}.

Doing the same with the imaginary part of
f
finally yields:

\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq\frac{2(1+K\varepsilon)}{K\varepsilon}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}.\qed

Thanks to Lemma
2.8
, we now apply
Proposition
2.11
.

Corollary 2.13
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.10
.

Let
K
,
C
,
T>0
.

Let
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of functions in
\mathcal{B}_{I}
. Assume that
\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq C
. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}=0,

(2.12)

where the convergence is uniform for fixed
K
and
C
.

Proof.

Let
K
,
C
,
T>0
. Let
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of functions in
\mathcal{B}_{I}
such that
\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq C
.

Let
(\varepsilon_{k})
be a positive sequence converging to
0
. For all
k
, there exist
f_{k,+}
,
f_{k,-}
,
f_{k,i+}
,
f_{k,i-}
in
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
such that
f_{\varepsilon_{k}}=f_{k,+}-f_{k,-}+if_{k,i+}-if_{k,i-}
and
\left\|f_{k,+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq 2C
, and likewise for
f_{k,-}
,
f_{k,i+}
,
f_{k,i-}
.

Note that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}

\displaystyle\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{k,+}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\prod_{n=0}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor-1}(\Id-\varepsilon_{k}({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})+R_{\varepsilon_{k},n})-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)\to\ell^{1}(I)}\left\|\Pi_{*}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)},

where
(R_{\varepsilon_{k},n})_{k,n\geq 0}
is a sequence of matrices obtained by Proposition
2.11
.
The quantity
\left\|\Pi_{*}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}
is equal to
|I|\cdot\left\|f_{k,+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
, and thus is bounded by
2C|I|
.
By Lemma
2.8
,

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{k,+}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}=0,

where the convergence is uniform in
C
. The same holds for
f_{k,-}
,
f_{k,i+}
,
f_{k,i-}
. Summing all four limits yields

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon_{k}}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon_{k}}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}=0.

Since the subsequence
(\varepsilon_{k})
is arbitrary, this finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary
2.13
gives us convergence of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}
to
e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}
, in a relatively weak sense, but uniformly on
all compacts in
t
. As announced, we now implement a coupling argument to show that
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0,\varepsilon>0}
is exponentially tight.

Proposition 2.14
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.10
.

For any
\rho\in(0,\rho_{R})
and
K\geq 0
, there exist constants
C
,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that, for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and
n\geq 0
, the operator norm of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}
acting on
(\mathcal{B}_{I,0},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{K,\varepsilon})

is no larger than
Ce^{-\rho\varepsilon n}
.

Proof.

Let
\rho^{\prime}\in(\rho,\rho_{R})
. Since
R
is an irreducible bi-L-matrix, so is
{\vphantom{R}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{R}
. By Lemma
1.9
, there exists a constant
C
such that, for all
t\geq 0
,

\left\|e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}-\pi\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)\to\ell^{1}(I)}\leq Ce^{-\rho^{\prime}t},

where
\pi(F)=|I|^{-1}\left(\sum_{I}F\right)\mathbf{1}
is the eigenprojection associated with the eigenvalue
0
of
{\vphantom{R}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{R}
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,1)
, to be fixed at the end of this proof. Let
K_{\sigma}
and
n_{\sigma}
be given by Lemma
2.5
. Let
T>0
be such that
C|I|e^{-\rho^{\prime}T}\leq\sigma/3
. We assume without loss of generality that
K\geq K_{\sigma}
.

A renormalization map

Given
f_{+}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
, we define :

\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}(f_{+}):=\sigma^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{+}-(1-\sigma)\int_{[I]}f_{+}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}\right).

Our intermediate goal is to prove that
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}
maps
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
into itself if
\varepsilon
is small enough.

Let
C^{\prime}>0
. Let
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be a sequence of positive numbers converging to
0
, and
(f_{k,+})_{k\geq 0}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon_{k})
with
\left\|f_{k,+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq C^{\prime}
. By Corollary
2.13
,

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{k,+}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}=0,

uniformly for fixed
K
and
C^{\prime}
. By our choice of
T
,

e^{-T({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{k,+}\geq\pi(\Pi_{*}f_{k,+})-Ce^{-\rho^{\prime}T}\left\|\Pi_{*}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}\geq\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{3}\right)\int_{[I]}f_{k,+}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}.

Hence, if
k
is large enough, then

\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{k,+}\geq\left(1-\frac{2\sigma}{3}\right)\int_{[I]}f_{k,+}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}.

If
k
is large enough, then
\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}T\geq n_{\sigma}
, so that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{k,+}\in C_{\sigma K}(\varepsilon_{k})
. This has two consequences. The first is that, if
k
is large enough,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{k,+}

\displaystyle\geq\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{k,+}-\sigma K\varepsilon_{k}\int_{[I]}f_{k,+}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\sigma)\int_{[I]}f_{k,+}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}.

In particular,
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon_{k}}(f_{k,+})\geq 0
. The second consequence is that, if
k
is large enough, then

\displaystyle\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon_{k}}(f_{k,+})\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-1}\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma^{-1}\sigma K\varepsilon_{k}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\lfloor\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{k,+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}

\displaystyle=K\varepsilon_{k}\left\|\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon_{k}}(f_{k,+})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}.

Hence,
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon_{k}}(f_{k,+})\in C_{K}(\varepsilon_{k})
. Since
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon_{k}}
is linear,
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon_{k}}(C_{K}(\varepsilon_{k}))\subset C_{K}(\varepsilon_{k})
for all large enough
k
. Finally, since
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is arbitrary,
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}(C_{K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{K}(\varepsilon)

for all small enough
\varepsilon
.

Bound on the operator norm

Let
\varepsilon
be small enough that
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}
preserves
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
. Let
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
, which we assume without loss of generality to be real. Let
f_{+}
,
f_{-}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
be such that
f=f_{+}-f_{-}
and
\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
,
\left\|f_{-}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq 2\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor}f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{+}-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{-}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{+}-(1-\sigma)\int_{[I]}f_{+}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}\right)-\left(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor}f_{+}-(1-\sigma)\int_{[I]}f_{+}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma\left(\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}(f_{+})-\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}(f_{-})\right).

By recursion, we get, for all
m\geq 0
:

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{m\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor}f=\sigma^{m}\left(\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{+})-\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{-})\right).

The functions
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{+})
and
\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{-})
belong to
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
and have the same integral as
f_{+}
.

Let
k\geq 0
. By Corollary
2.6
, without loss of generality, there exists a constant
\lambda_{1}\geq 1

such that, and all
\varepsilon>0
,
k\geq 0
, the functions
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{+})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{-})
belong to
C_{\lambda_{1}K}(\varepsilon)
. Hence, setting

\tilde{f}_{+}:=(\lambda_{1}-1)\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}+\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{+})

and likewise for
f_{-}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{m\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor+k}f=\sigma^{m}(\tilde{f}_{+}-\tilde{f}_{-}).

By construction,

\displaystyle\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\tilde{f}_{+}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle=\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{+})\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{1}K\varepsilon\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\Phi_{\sigma,\varepsilon}^{m}(f_{+})\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}K\varepsilon\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}

\displaystyle=K\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{f}_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}.

Since the same estimate holds for
\tilde{f}_{-}
, the functions
\tilde{f}_{+}
,
\tilde{f}_{-}
belong to
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
and their
\mathbb{L}^{1}
-norm is bounded by
\lambda_{1}\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq 2\lambda_{1}\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}
.
Hence,

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{m\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor+k}f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq 2\lambda_{1}\sigma^{m}\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.

(2.13)

Let
n\geq 0
. Take
m:=\lfloor\varepsilon T^{-1}n\rfloor
and
k:=n-m\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}T\rfloor
.
Equation (
2.13
) implies :

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq 2\lambda_{1}\sigma^{\lfloor\varepsilon T^{-1}n\rfloor}\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.

Finally, recall that the only constraint on
T
is that
C|I|e^{-\rho^{\prime}T}\leq\sigma/3
. Hence,
we can take
T:=\rho^{\prime-1}|\ln(\sigma/(3C|I|))|
, so that:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}e^{-|\ln(\sigma)|\lfloor\rho^{\prime}\varepsilon|\ln(\sigma/(3C|I|))|^{-1}n\rfloor}\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}\sigma^{-1}e^{-\rho^{\prime}\frac{\ln(\sigma)}{\ln(\sigma)-\ln(3C|I|)}\varepsilon n}\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.

Choose
\sigma
small enough that

\rho^{\prime}\frac{\ln(\sigma)}{\ln(\sigma)-\ln(3C|I|)}\leq\rho\quad;

then

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq 2\lambda_{1}\sigma^{-1}e^{-\rho\varepsilon n}\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.\qed

Remark 2.15

(Bound in operator norm)
.

The bound on the operator norm of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}
in Proposition
2.14
is given for the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}
For the original Lipschitz norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}
, which does not depend on
\varepsilon
, for any
\rho<\rho_{R}
, the bounds in the proof of Lemma
2.12
yield a constant
C
such that:

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I,0}\to\mathcal{B}_{I,0}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}e^{-\rho\varepsilon n}.

(2.14)

While not necessary for the remainder of this article, notice that Lemma
2.12
and Proposition
2.14
give an explicit lower bound on the spectral gap of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. By ergodicity of
([I],\mu_{I},T_{\varepsilon})
, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
has
1
as a simple eigenvalue, corresponding to constant eigenfunctions.
Define the
spectral gap
of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
acting on
\mathcal{B}_{I}
as, equivalently, one minus the spectral radius of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
acting on
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
, that is

\min\{1-|\lambda|:\ \lambda\in\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}_{I}),\ \lambda\neq 1\}.

Corollary 2.16
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.10
.

When
\varepsilon
goes to
0
, the spectral gap of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
acting on
\mathcal{B}_{I}

is at least
\rho_{R}\varepsilon(1-o(1))
.

Integrating over
t\geq 0
, we can now relate
S
with
R_{0}^{-1}
.

Corollary 2.17
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.10
.

Let
K\geq 0
. Let
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of functions in
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
.
Assume that
\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}<+\infty
. Then, in
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
,

\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*}({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0}^{-1})\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}+o(\varepsilon^{-1}).

(2.15)

Proof.

Changing the time scale by a factor of
\varepsilon^{-1}
, we get:

\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}t.

By Corollary
2.13
, on all compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}=0.

By Proposition
2.14
, there exists a constant
C
such that
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}
. We write
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}=f_{+}-f_{-}

with
f_{+}
,
f_{-}\in C_{K}(\varepsilon)
and
\left\|f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}
,
\left\|f_{-}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq 2\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}
.
Up to taking a larger value of
K
, the function
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{+}
belongs to

C_{\lambda_{1}K}(\varepsilon)
for all
t
by Corollary
2.6
. Hence,

\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{+}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq\lambda_{1}K\varepsilon\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{+}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\leq 2\lambda_{1}K\varepsilon\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.

The function
f_{-}
satisfies a similar estimate. It follows that

\left\|(\Id-\Pi^{*}\Pi_{*})\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq 4\lambda_{1}K\varepsilon\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.

Hence,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}-\Pi^{*}e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=0.

(2.16)

In addition, by Proposition
2.14
again, there exist constants
C
,
\rho>0
such that the operator norm of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}
acting on
(\mathcal{B}_{I,0},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{K,\varepsilon})
is no larger than
Ce^{-\rho\varepsilon n}
. Using Equation (
2.11
),

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}\leq 4(1+K\varepsilon)\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}\leq 4(1+K\varepsilon)Ce^{\rho\varepsilon}e^{-\rho t}\left\|f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}.

This gives the tightness needed for the convergence of integrals:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\|\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}t-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Pi^{*}e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}t\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=0.

Finally, denoting by
{\vphantom{R}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0}
the action of
{\vphantom{R}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{R}
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
, to which
\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}
belongs,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Pi^{*}e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}t=\Pi^{*}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0})}\;{\rm d}t\right)\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}=\Pi^{*}({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0}^{-1})\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}.

We get the claim by multiplying both side by
\varepsilon^{-1}
:

\left\|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}\;{\rm d}t-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*}({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0}^{-1})\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=o(\varepsilon^{-1}).\qed

We now finish the proof of Lemma
1.19
.

Proof of Lemma
1.19
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
, the quantity
\left\|\Pi^{*}f\right\|_{K,\varepsilon}

is bounded jointly in
K
and
\varepsilon
, and Corollary
2.17
applies.
By Lemma
1.14
,
{\vphantom{R}}{}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0}^{-1}
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
. This yields, for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\langle f,S(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+o(\varepsilon^{-1})

\displaystyle=\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\langle\Pi_{*}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f),g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\langle\Pi_{*}\Pi^{*}({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0}^{-1})\Pi_{*}\Pi^{*}(f),g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+o(\varepsilon^{-1})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\langle f,R_{0}^{-1}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+o(\varepsilon^{-1}),

whence
S=R_{0}^{-1}
.
∎

3
The ergodic case

All the work in Section
2
was done under Hypothesis
1.18
, that is, that the first return map
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
is mixing. This hypothesis is inconvenient, as we would like to work only with conditions involving the initial data: the system
(A,\mu,T)
and the function
F
.

If
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is recurrent and ergodic, then
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
is well-defined and ergodic. Hence, ergodicity comes freely, and we would like to use this property instead of the stronger mixing hypothesis. This is the goal of this section.

3.1
Structure of extensions with non-mixing first return maps

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
with jump function
F
. Assume that
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic and recurrent. As the induced map
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
is well-defined and ergodic, by Corollary
A.10
, there exists an integer
M\geq 1
and a partition
A=\bigsqcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}A_{\ell}
such that each
A_{\ell}
is
\alpha^{*}
-measurable,

T_{[0]}(A_{\ell})\subset A_{\ell+1}
for all
\ell
, and
T_{[0]}^{M}:A_{\ell}\to A_{\ell}
is mixing for all
\ell
.

At first, we show the constraints that the existence of a non-trivial period for
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
impose on the whole extension
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
. We introduce a colouration of
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
.

Definition 3.1

(Color of a site)
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
. Assume that
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic and recurrent. Let
A=\bigsqcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}A_{\ell}
be the decomposition of
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
associated with its period
M
. For
\widetilde{\mu}
-almost every
(x,p)\in[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
, let

\widetilde{\varphi}_{[0]}(x,p):=\inf\{n\geq 1:\ \widetilde{T}^{n}(x,p)\in[0]\}.

(3.1)

The
colour
of
(x,p)
is defined as the element
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\widetilde{T}^{\widetilde{\varphi}_{[0]}(x,p)}(x,p)\in A_{\ell}
. We shall denote it by
C(x,p)
.

Interestingly, the structural constraints we get are different depending on whether
d=1
or
d=2
, the later case being simpler. As the following proposition states, in the
d=2
case, all sites have the same colour, up to a neighbourhood of
[0]
. In the
d=1
case, we may need two colours. This difference comes from the fact that the plane minus a point is connected, while the line minus a point has two connected components.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
with jump function
F
. Assume that
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic and recurrent.

If
d=2
, or
d=1
and
\left\|F\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(A,\mu)}=+\infty
, then there
exists
R>0
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
such that
C(x,p)=\ell
whenever
\left\|p\right\|\geq R
.

If
d=1
and
\left\|F\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(A,\mu)}<+\infty
, then there
exists
R>0
and
\ell_{-}
,
\ell_{+}\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
(not necessarily distinct)
such that
C(x,p)=\ell_{-}
whenever
p\leq-R
and
C(x,p)=\ell_{+}
whenever
p\geq R
.

Proof.

We work under the hypotheses of Proposition
3.2
. First, let us note that
C\circ\widetilde{T}(x,p)=C(x,p)
for all
(x,p)\in[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\setminus[0]
.

By ergodicity and recurrence,
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is swept by the forward images of
[0]
, that is,

[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]=\bigcup_{x\in A}\bigsqcup_{k=0}^{\varphi_{[0]}(x)-1}\widetilde{T}^{k}(x,0).

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
. The property that
T_{[0]}(A_{\ell-1})=A_{\ell}
implies that, for all
x\in A_{\ell-1}
and all
0\leq k<\varphi_{[0]}(x)
,

\widetilde{T}^{\widetilde{\varphi}_{[0]}(\widetilde{T}^{k}(x,0))}\circ\widetilde{T}^{k}(x,0)=\widetilde{T}^{\varphi_{[0]}(x)-k}\circ\widetilde{T}^{k}(x,0)=T_{[0]}(x)\in A_{\ell},

so that
C(\widetilde{T}^{k}(x,0))=\ell
. Doing this for all
\ell
yields
\{C=\ell\}=\bigcup_{x\in A_{\ell-1}}\bigsqcup_{k=0}^{\varphi_{[0]}(x)-1}\widetilde{T}^{k}(x,0)
: modulo a negligible subset, the points of colour
\ell
are exactly the forward images of
A_{\ell-1}
up to time
\varphi_{[0]}-1
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
. We can also write the later fact as

\{C=\ell\}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{+\infty}\widetilde{T}^{k}(A_{\ell-1}\cap\{\varphi_{[0]}>k\}).

Since
\varphi_{[0]}
is a stopping time,
\{\varphi_{[0]}>k\}
is
\sigma(\alpha^{(k)})
-measurable.
The set
A_{\ell-1}
is also
\sigma(\alpha^{*})
-measurable by Corollary
A.10
, and thus also
\sigma(\alpha^{(k)})
-measurable. The function
S_{k}F
is constant on each cylinder of length
k
, so that we can write it as a function of the cylinder. Hence, setting
B(p,\ell):=\{x\in A:\ C(x,p)=\ell\}
, we have

B(p,\ell)=\bigcup_{k=0}^{+\infty}\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\alpha^{(k)}\\
a\subset A_{\ell-1}\cap\{\varphi_{[0]}>k\}\\
S_{k}(a)=p\end{subarray}}T^{k}(a).

(3.2)

By the big image property of Gibbs-Markov maps, for all
k\geq 0
and nonempty
a\in\alpha^{(k)}
, we have
\mu(T^{k}(a))\geq\delta_{0}:=\inf_{a\in\alpha}\mu(T(a))>0
. Each subset
T^{k}(a)
in Equation (
3.2
) has measure at least
\delta_{0}
. Hence, for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
, we have a dichotomy:

•

either
\mu(B(p,\ell))=0
;

•

or
\mu(B(p,\ell))\geq\delta_{0}>0
.

In other words, on each site, either a colour is absent, or it is present in a quantity of at least
\delta_{0}
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
\ell
such that
\mu(B(p,\ell))>0
. From the expression in Equation (
3.2
), the set
B(p,\ell)
is
\sigma(\alpha^{*})
-measurable, so that
\left\|\mathbf{1}_{B(p,\ell)}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}=1
. Since
T_{[0]}
is ergodic, by Corollary
A.10
, there exists
M\geq 1
,
C>0
and
\rho\in(0,1)
, independent from
p
and
\ell
, such that

\left\|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n+k}(\mathbf{1}_{B(p,\ell)})-\mu(B(p,\ell))\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq C\rho^{n}\left\|\mathbf{1}_{B(p,\ell)}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}=C\rho^{n}.

Hence, there exists
n_{0}>0
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{k}(\mathbf{1}_{B(p,\ell)})\geq\delta_{0}/2>0
almost everywhere for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
\ell
such that
\mu(B(p,\ell))>0
. But the support of the function
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{k}(\mathbf{1}_{B(p,\ell)})
is
\bigcup_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}T_{[0]}^{k}(B(p,\ell))
, so that, almost everywhere,

\bigcup_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}T_{[0]}^{k}(B(p,\ell))=A.

Let us define
\varphi_{[p]\to[0]}(x):=\inf\{n\geq 1:T^{n}_{[\{p,0\}]}(x,p)\in[0]\}
. Since the probability of hitting
[0]
before going back to
[p]
converges to
0
as
p
goes to infinity by
[
40
, Lemma 4.17]
, the random variable
\varphi_{[p]\to[0]}
converges in distribution to
+\infty
when
p
goes to infinity. In particular, there exists
r_{0}>0
such that
\mu(\varphi_{[p]\to[0]}<n_{0})<\delta_{0}/n_{0}
whenever
\left\|p\right\|>r_{0}
.

Assume that
\left\|p\right\|>r_{0}
. Then

\mu\left(\bigcup_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}T_{[0]}^{k}\left(B(p,\ell)\cap\{\varphi_{[p]\to[0]}\geq n_{0}\}\right)\right)\geq 1-n_{0}\mu(\varphi_{[p]\to[0]}<n_{0})>1-\delta_{0}.

In addition, for
x\in B(p,\ell)\cap\{\varphi_{[p]\to[0]}\geq n_{0}\}
and
0\leq k<n_{0}
, we have
C(T_{[\{0,p\}]}^{k}(x,p))=C(x,p)=\ell
, and thus
\mu(B(p,\ell))>1-\delta_{0}
. By the dichotomy on
\mu(B(p,\ell))
, we thus have
\mu(B(p,\ell^{\prime}))=0
for
\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell
, so that
\mu(B(p,\ell))=1
. In other words, if
\left\|p\right\|>r_{0}
, then
[p]
is monochromatic.

Let
\leftrightarrow
be the nearest neighbor relation in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. For all
q\leftrightarrow 0
, the random variable
\varphi_{[p]\to[p+q]}
does not depend on
p
, while
\varphi_{[p]\to[0]}
converges in distribution to
+\infty
as
p
goes to infinity. In particular, there exists
r_{1}
such that
\mu(\varphi_{[p]\to[p+q]}<\varphi_{[p]\to[0]})>0
for all
\left\|p\right\|>r_{1}
and
q\leftrightarrow 0
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
\left\|p\right\|>R:=\max\{r_{0}+1,r_{1}\}
. Then
[p]
is monochromatic; let
\ell
be its colour. For each
p^{\prime}\leftrightarrow p
, we have
\mu(\varphi_{[p]\to[p^{\prime}]}<\varphi_{[p]\to[0]})>0
, so that
[p^{\prime}]
also contains the colour
\ell
. But
\left\|p^{\prime}\right\|>r_{0}
, so
[p^{\prime}]
is also monochromatic, and thus is the same colour as
[p]
.

At this point, our conclusion depends on the dimension.

•

For the case
d=2
: the graph
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus B(0,R)
is connected, so that
[\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus B(0,R)]
is monochromatic.

•

For the case
d=1
: the graph
\mathbb{Z}\setminus B(0,R)
has two connected components, so that the sets
[(-\infty,-R)\cap\mathbb{Z}]
and
[(R,+\infty)\cap\mathbb{Z}]
are both monochromatic.

In the later case, assume furthermore that
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(A,\mu)}=+\infty
; without loss of generality, assume that the essential supremum of
F
is
+\infty
. Then there exists
p\in(-\infty,-R)\cap\mathbb{Z}
and
q\in(R,+\infty)\cap\mathbb{Z}
such that
\mu([p]\cap\widetilde{T}^{-1}([q]))>0
. In particular,
[p]
and
[q]
have the same colour, so
[\mathbb{Z}\setminus B(0,R)]
is monochromatic.
∎

Proposition
3.2
gives very strong constraints on the structure of a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension whose induced map in
[0]
is not mixing. We now prove that, under these constraints, the induced map
T_{[\Sigma]}
itself has a decomposition in periodic components, as long as the elements of
\Sigma
are far enough apart.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
with jump function
F
. Assume that
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic and recurrent. Let
M\geq 1
be the period of
([0],\mu,T_{[0]})
. Then there exists
R>0
with the following property. Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be non-empty and such that
\min_{p\neq q\in\Sigma}\left\|p-q\right\|\geq R
.

If
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus B(0,r)]
is monochromatic for large enough
r
(and in particular if
d=2
, or
d=1
and
\left\|F\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(A,\mu)}=+\infty
), then
T_{[\Sigma]}(A_{k}\times\Sigma)\subset A_{k+1}\times\Sigma
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
. In addition, there exists
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
such that, almost surely, transitions between sites occur only from
A_{\ell-1}\times\Sigma
to
A_{\ell}\times\Sigma
.

If
[\mathbb{Z}\setminus B(0,r)]
is bichromatic for large enough
r
, sort
\Sigma
in increasing order, for instance:

\Sigma=\{\ldots,\sigma_{-1},\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots\}=\{\sigma_{n}:n\in I\}.

Then
T_{[\Sigma]}
sends
\bigsqcup_{n\in I}A_{k+n(\ell_{+}-\ell_{-})}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}
to
\bigsqcup_{n\in I}A_{k+n(\ell_{+}-\ell_{-})+1}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
. In addition, transitions occur only from
A_{\ell_{+}-1}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}
to
A_{\ell_{-}}\times\{\sigma_{n+1}\}
or from
A_{\ell_{-}-1}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}
to
A_{\ell_{+}}\times\{\sigma_{n-1}\}
.

Proof.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be such an extension, and choose
R
as in Proposition
3.2
. Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying the conditions of
Proposition
3.3
.

First case: monochromatic extensions

We start with the first case. Since the extension is monochromatic away from
[0]
, let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
be its colour. Let
p\in\Sigma
and
(x,p)\in[p]
.

If
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[p]
, then
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)=(T_{[0]}(x),p)
. Given
k
such that
x\in A_{k}
, we get
T_{[0]}(x)\in A_{k+1}
, and thus
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in A_{k+1}\times\Sigma
.

Otherwise, let
q\in\Sigma
be such that
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[q]
. Then
T_{[\Sigma-q]}(x,p-q)
is the first return to
[0]
of the orbit of
(x,p-q)
under
\widetilde{T}
, and
(x,p-q)\in[\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus B(0,R)]
. Since the colour of the later is
\ell
, this means that
T_{[\Sigma-q]}(x,p-q)\in A_{\ell}\times\{0\}
, and thus
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in A_{\ell}\times\{q\}
. In addition, using again the monochromaticity of
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus B(0,R)]
, the first return of
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)
to
[p]
belongs to
A_{\ell}\times\{p\}
. But, since this first return is
(T_{[0]}(x),p)
, this implies that
x\in A_{\ell-1}
.

Second case: bichromatic extensions

We now tackle the second case. By Proposition
3.2
, the extension is a
\mathbb{Z}
-extension. It has colour
\ell_{-}
on the large enough negatives and
\ell_{+}\neq\ell_{-}
on the large enough positives numbers. Let
p\in\Sigma
and
(x,p)\in[p]
.

If there exists non-adjacent sites
p<q<r
in
\Sigma
such that
\mu([p]\cap T_{[\Sigma]}^{-1}([r]))>0
, then
p-q
and
r-q
have the same colour, so
\ell_{+}=\ell_{-}
; the same holds if
\mu([r]\cap T_{[\Sigma]}^{-1}([q]))>0
. This contradicts the bichromaticity of the extension. Hence the only allowed transitions are between adjacent sites in
\Sigma
.

Again, if
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[p]
, then
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)=(T_{[0]}(x),p)
. Given
k
such that
x\in A_{k}
, we get
T_{[0]}(x)\in A_{k+1}
, and thus
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in A_{k+1}\times\Sigma
.

Otherwise, let
q\in\Sigma
be such that
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in[q]
. Assume without loss of generality that
q>p
. Then the same arguments as in the first case yield
T_{[\Sigma]}(x,p)\in A_{\ell_{-}}\times\{q\}
and
(x,p)\in A_{\ell_{+}-1}\times[p]
.

Let us sort the elements of
\Sigma
in increasing order, indexing them either by
I=\{0,\ldots,|\Sigma|-1\}
,
I=\mathbb{N}
or
I=\mathbb{Z}
; this let us write, for instance,
\Sigma=\{\ldots,\sigma_{-1},\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots\}
. A transition from
\sigma_{n}
to
\sigma_{n+1}
starts from
A_{\ell_{+}-1}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}
and ends in
A_{\ell_{-}}\times\{\sigma_{n+1}\}
, while a transition from
\sigma_{n}
to
\sigma_{n-1}
starts from
A_{\ell_{-}-1}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}
and ends in
A_{\ell_{+}}\times\{\sigma_{n-1}\}
. In particular,
T_{[\Sigma]}
sends
\bigsqcup_{n\in I}A_{k+n(\ell_{+}-\ell_{-})}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}

to
\bigsqcup_{n\in I}A_{k+n(\ell_{+}-\ell_{-})+1}\times\{\sigma_{n}\}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

Remark 3.4

(Examples of monochromatic and bichromatic extensions)
.

Constructing a monochromatic
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension is easy. Choose a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
with a non-trivial period, and jump only at a given instant in the period. For instance, take

•

A=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{Z}_{/5\mathbb{Z}}
,

•

\mu
is proportional to the Lebesgue measure on
A
,

•

T(x,k)=(3x,k+1)
,

•

F(x,0)=-1
if
x\in[0,1/3)
, then
F(x,0)=+1
if
x\in[2/3,1)
and
F(x,k)=0
otherwise.

Then
A_{k}=\mathbb{T}\times\{k\}
and
\ell=1
.

A_{1}
A_{2}
A_{3}
A_{4}
A_{0}

Figure 5
:
On the left: the allowed transitions for the transformation
T
and the five elements of the partition in periodic components
\{A_{0},A_{1},\ldots,A_{4}\}
of
T_{[0]}
. On the right: the
\mathbb{Z}
-extension of
T
and its allowed transitions. The five shades of blue correspond to the five elements of the partition
\{A_{0}\times\mathbb{Z},A_{1}\times\mathbb{Z},\ldots,A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}\}
of
[\mathbb{Z}]
. All allowed transitions lead to a subset one shade darker, or from the darkest shade to the lightest.

The analyses in the proofs of Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
give a guide to construct a bichromatic extension. We tweak the Gibbs-Markov map defined above. We still take
A=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{Z}_{/5\mathbb{Z}}
and

\mu
proportional to the Lebesgue measure, but:

T(x,k)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}(2x,3)&\text{ if }&x\in[0,1/2)\text{ and }k=0\\
(2x,1)&\text{ if }&x\in[0,1/2)\text{ and }k=2\\
(2x,k+1)&\text{ otherwise}&\\
\end{array}\right..

We choose for
F
:

F(x,k)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}+1&\text{ if }&x\in[0,1/2)\text{ and }k=0\\
-1&\text{ if }&x\in[0,1/2)\text{ and }k=2\\
0&\text{ otherwise}&\\
\end{array}\right..

Then
A_{k}=\mathbb{T}\times\{k\}
,
\ell_{+}=1
and
\ell_{-}=3
.

:
On the left: the allowed transitions for the transformation
T
and the five elements of the partition in periodic components
\{A_{0},A_{1},\ldots,A_{4}\}
of
T_{[0]}
. On the right: the
\mathbb{Z}
-extension of
T
and its allowed transitions. The five shades of blue correspond to the five elements of the partition
\left\{\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{k-2n}\times\{n\}:\ k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/5\mathbb{Z}}\right\}
of
[\mathbb{Z}]
. All allowed transitions lead to a subset one shade darker, or from the darkest shade to the lightest.

Remark 3.5

(Structure of bichromatic extensions)
.

As per Proposition
3.2
, a bichromatic extension can occur only if
d=1
and
\left\|F\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(A,\mu)}<+\infty
. A reasonable conjecture is that such extensions can only occur if
d=1
and there exists a bounded coboundary
u\circ T-u
such that
F+u\circ T-u
takes its values in
\{-1,0,1\}
.

Remark 3.6

(Non Gibbs-Markov maps)
.

The structure of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extensions when the induced map
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
has some periodicity may be different for maps which are not Gibbs-Markov. An interesting example is that of piecewise expanding maps of the interval with a compatible jump function. Then
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
is still a piecewise expanding map of the interval (with infinitely many pieces).

Up to additional conditions to check
[
34
,
46
]
, we may expect its transfer operator to act quasi-compactly on the space of functions with bounded variation, from which we can develop a similar description of its spectrum. In particular, if
(A,\mu,T_{[0]})
is not mixing, then it has a decomposition in periodic components, from which we can construct a colouring of
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
. However, our argument in this article relies heavily on the big image property of Gibbs-Markov maps, which is not satisfied for general piecewise expanding maps of the interval. In this case, how are Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
to be adapted? Are
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
-extensions of piecewise expanding maps either monochromatic or bichromatic?

3.2
End of the proof of Lemma
1.20

We now finish the proof of Lemma
1.20
. We explain the main adaptations to make to the proof of Lemma
1.19
, first in the case of monochromatic extensions, and then in the case of bichromatic extensions.

Proof of Lemma
1.20
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
and
1.10
. Let
M\geq 1
be the period of
([0],\mu,T_{[0]})
and
(A_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}
a decomposition of
A
in periodic components for
T_{[0]}
.

First case: monochromatic extensions

By Hypothesis
1.10
, the probability of transition between different sites in
[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]

converges to
0
. By recurrence and ergodicity,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\min_{p\neq q\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\left\|p-q\right\|=+\infty
. We can thus apply Proposition
3.3
: there exists
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
such that, for all small enough
\varepsilon>0
, the map
T_{\varepsilon}
sends each
A_{k}\times I
into
A_{k+1}\times I
, and all transitions between distinct sites occur from
A_{\ell-1}\times I
to
A_{\ell}\times I
. Hence,
T_{\varepsilon}^{M}(A_{k}\times I)=A_{k}\times I
for all
k
.

Let us fix some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
, and work with the measure-preserving dynamical system
(A_{k}\times I,M\mu_{I|A_{k}\times I},T_{\varepsilon}^{M})
. A suitable Banach space is given by the space
\mathcal{B}_{I,k}
of functions in
\mathcal{B}_{I}
supported on
A_{k}\times I
(recall that
A_{k}
is
\sigma(\alpha^{*})
-measurable, so multiplication by
\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}\times I}
is continuous on
\mathcal{B}_{I}
). We define operators
\Pi_{k,*}:\mathcal{B}_{I,k}\to\mathbb{C}^{I}
and
\Pi_{k}^{*}:\mathbb{C}^{I}\to\mathcal{B}_{I,k}
in the same way as we defined
\Pi_{*}
and
\Pi^{*}
, that is respectively by averaging in each
A_{k}\times\{i\}
and by finding the preimage that is constant on each
A_{k}\times\{i\}
. From there a definition of cones
C_{k,K}(\varepsilon)
is straightforward.

The operator
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}
acts quasi-compactly on
\mathcal{B}
, and thus so does
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{M}
. It follows that the action of
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{M}
on the space
\mathcal{B}_{k}
of functions in
\mathcal{B}
supported on
A_{k}
is also quasi-compact. Since
(A_{k},M\mu_{|A_{k}},T_{[0]}^{M})
is mixing, the action
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{M}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}_{k}
has a spectral gap.

Let
i\neq j\in I
. Let
\ell^{\prime}
be to smallest integer larger than
k
and congruent to
\ell
modulo
M
. A point
(x,i)\in A_{k}\times\{i\}
belongs to
T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}(A_{k}\times\{j\})
if and only if
T_{[0]}^{\ell^{\prime}-k-1}(x,i)
belongs to
T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([j])
, so that

\displaystyle M\mu_{|A_{k}}(x:\ (x,i)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}(A_{k}\times\{j\}))

\displaystyle=M\mu\left(T_{[0]}^{1+k-\ell^{\prime}}(\{x:\ (x,i)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([j])\})\right)

\displaystyle=M\mu([i]\cap T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([j])\})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon MR_{ij}+o(\varepsilon).

In addition, if
(x,i)\in A_{k}\times\{i\}
and
T_{\varepsilon}^{M}(x,i)\in A_{k}\times\{i\}
, then
T_{\varepsilon}^{n}(x,i)\in A_{k}\times\{i\}
for all
0\leq n\leq M
, so that
T_{\varepsilon}^{M}(x,i)=T_{[0]}^{M}(x,i)
.

From there, the proof of Proposition
2.5
follows mostly unchanged, replacing
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
by
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{M}
and
C_{K}(\varepsilon)
by
C_{k,K}(\varepsilon)
. More explicitly, for all
\sigma>0
, there exist
K_{\sigma}
,
n_{\sigma}\geq 0
such that, for all
K\geq K_{\sigma}
, for all
\varepsilon>0
and
n\geq n_{\sigma}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{Mn}(C_{k,K}(\varepsilon))\subset C_{k,\sigma K}(\varepsilon).

Corollary
2.6
follows directly. Subsection
2.2
is unchanged as it makes no reference to the dynamics. Corollary
2.13
follows with only the obvious adaptations, and so does Proposition
2.14
. We get a variant of Equation (
2.12
):

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Pi_{k,*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{M\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}M^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{k,*}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}=0\quad\forall(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\in C_{k,K}(\varepsilon).

The map
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}:\mathcal{B}_{k,I}\to\mathcal{B}_{k+n,I}
is continuous with a norm of at most
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}\to\mathcal{B}_{I}}
, since we are only restricting
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{n}
to functions supported on
A_{k}
. We can also apply Proposition
2.11
to get that

\Pi_{k+n,*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}=(1+O(\varepsilon))\Pi_{k,*}f_{\varepsilon},

where
n=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor-M\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}M^{-1}t\rfloor
and the
O(\varepsilon)
term depends only on the choice of
K
. This yields

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Pi_{k+\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor,*}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor}f_{\varepsilon}-e^{-t({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R})}\Pi_{k+\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}t\rfloor,*}f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\ell^{1}(I)}=0\quad\forall(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\in C_{k,K}(\varepsilon).

Proposition
2.14
also carries over:

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{Mn}\right\|_{(\mathcal{B}_{k,I,0},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{k,K,\varepsilon})\to(\mathcal{B}_{k,I,0},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{k,K,\varepsilon})}\leq Ce^{-\rho M\varepsilon n}

for some constants
C
,
\rho>0
(which can be taken arbitrarily close to
\rho_{R}
), all
n\geq 0
and all small enough
\varepsilon>0
. Again, using the boundedness of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}:\mathcal{B}_{k,I}\to\mathcal{B}_{k+n,I}
, up to increasing the constants
C
and
K
, we get

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\right\|_{(\mathcal{B}_{k,I,0},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{k,K,\varepsilon})\to(\mathcal{B}_{k+n,I,0},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{k+n,K,\varepsilon})}\leq Ce^{-\rho\varepsilon n}.

The statement of Corollary
2.17
can be adapted by replacing
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
by
\mathcal{B}_{k,I,0}
. However, we want in the end a statement for functions constant on each
[i]\in[I]
, so this is not satisfactory. Some caution is needed at this point; what we get is that, given
K\geq 0
and a family on functions
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\in\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}\mathcal{B}_{k,I,0}
such that
\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{k,K,\varepsilon}
,

\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Pi^{*}({\vphantom{R}}^{\mathit{t}}{R}_{0}^{-1})\Pi_{*}f_{\varepsilon}+o(\varepsilon^{-1}),

where the equality holds in
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
. The space
\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}\mathcal{B}_{k,I,0}
is of codimension
M-1
in
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
: if we want the series
\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}
to actually be summable, we need
f_{\varepsilon}
to have zero average on each
A_{k}\times I
, and not just on the whole of
[I]
. Under this slight modification, the proof of Corollary
2.17
can be adapted up to straightforward modifications, starting from

\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}f_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathbf{1}_{A_{k+n}\times I}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(f_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}\times I}),

and working with each
k
separately. Finally, given
f\in\mathbb{C}^{I}_{0}
, the function
\Pi^{*}f
do belong to the space
\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}}\mathcal{B}_{k,I,0}
, which finishes the proof of Lemma
1.20
in the case of monochromatic extensions.

Second case: bichromatic extensions

As with monochromatic extensions, under Hypothesis
1.10
, the minimal distance between sites in
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
converges to
+\infty
, so that we can apply Proposition
3.3
.

Let us index the sites in
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
in increasing order by
I:=\{0,\ldots,|\Sigma_{\varepsilon}|-1\}
. This reindexing conjugate both the matrices
P_{\varepsilon}
and
Q_{\varepsilon}
by the same permutation matrix, which depends on
\varepsilon>0
. Since there are only finitely many permutation matrices, it is enough to prove the theorem on the subsequences of
\varepsilon>0
corresponding to any fixed permutation. Hence, up to restriction to suitable subsequences, we assume simultaneously that Hypothesis
1.10
holds and that the indexing of
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
is increasing.

By Proposition
3.3
, there exist
\ell_{-}\neq\ell_{+}\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
such that
all transitions for
T_{\varepsilon}
occur from
A_{\ell_{+}-1}\times\{n\}
to
A_{\ell_{-}}\times\{n+1\}
or from
A_{\ell_{-}-1}\times\{n\}
to
A_{\ell_{+}}\times\{n-1\}
. We set
\widetilde{A}_{k}:=\bigsqcup_{n\in I}A_{k+n(\ell_{+}-\ell_{-})}\times\{n\}
so that
T_{\varepsilon}^{M}
preserves all the subsets
\widetilde{A}_{k}
for all small enough
\varepsilon
.

The adaptation is then mostly the same as in the monochromatic case, replacing
A_{k}\times I
by
\widetilde{A}_{k}
. There are two significant differences. The first is that jumps upwards and jumps downwards happen at different times, so the argument which in the monochromatic case yielded

M\mu_{|A_{k}}(x:\ (x,i)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}(A_{k}\times\{j\}))=\varepsilon MR_{ij}+o(\varepsilon)

does not work immediately. Here is how this argument can be fixed. First, note that, from times
k=0
to
k=M
, a trajectory cannot get up then down, or down then up. Indeed, a trajectory of the first kind would have to go through
A_{\ell_{+}-1}\times\{n\}
, then
A_{\ell_{-}}\times\{n+1\}
, then
A_{\ell_{-}-1}\times\{n+1\}
, then
A_{\ell_{+}}\times\{n\}
, which requires at least
M+1
steps. The same reasoning applies to trajectories of the second kind.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/M\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\ell^{\prime}_{-}
(respectively
\ell^{\prime}_{+}
) be the least integer strictly larger than
k
and congruent to
\ell_{-}
(respectively
\ell_{+}
) modulo
M
. Assume without loss of generality that
\ell^{\prime}_{-}<\ell^{\prime}_{+}
. Then

\displaystyle M\mu_{|A_{k}}(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([\{0,\ldots,n-1\}]))

\displaystyle=M\mu(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n-1]))

\displaystyle=\varepsilon MR_{n,n-1}+o(\varepsilon),

with the same argument as in the monochromatic case. In addition,

\displaystyle M\mu_{|A_{k}}

\displaystyle\left(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([\{n+1,\ldots,|I|\}])\right)

\displaystyle=M\mu_{|A_{k}}\left(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([\{n+1,\ldots,|I|\}])\text{ and }(x,n)\notin T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([\{0,\ldots,n-1\}])\right)

\displaystyle=M\int_{A_{\ell_{+}-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\{x:(x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n+1])\}}\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{\ell_{+}-\ell_{-}}\left(\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}_{\{x:(x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n-1])\}}\right)\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=M\mu(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n-1]))

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-M\int_{A_{\ell_{+}-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\{x:(x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n+1])\}}\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{\ell_{+}-\ell_{-}}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{x:(x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n-1])\}}\right)\;{\rm d}\mu.

By Corollary
A.7
,

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{\ell_{+}-\ell_{-}}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{x:(x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n-1])\}}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}}\leq C\mu\left(x:(x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n-1])\right)=O(\varepsilon),

so that

\displaystyle M\mu_{|A_{k}}(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([\{n+1,\ldots,|I|\}]))

\displaystyle=M(1+O(\varepsilon))\mu\left(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}([n+1])\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon MR_{n,n+1}+o(\varepsilon).

Finally, the same reasoning let us show that the measure of points which undergo at least two jumps before time
M
is in
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, so that

\displaystyle M\mu_{|A_{k}}(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([n-1]))

\displaystyle=M\mu_{|A_{k}}(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([\{0,\ldots,n-1\}]))+O(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon MR_{n,n-1}+o(\varepsilon),

and the same for
M\mu_{|A_{k}}(x:\ (x,n)\in T_{\varepsilon}^{-M}([n+1]))
.

The second difference is that, when adapting Proposition
2.5
, we work with the action of
\mathcal{L}_{[0]}^{M}
on
\mathcal{B}_{k+n(\ell_{+}-\ell_{-})}
, which depends on the site
n
. In the end, this does not matter: as
k
takes only finitely many values, there are finitely many such actions, so that all relevant controls are uniform in
n
.
∎

4
From potential to transition probabilities

We now finish the proof of Theorem
0.1
.

In Sections
2
(under the mixing Hypothesis
1.18
) and
3
,
knowing an asymptotic development
P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
,
we were able to deduce an asymptotic development
Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}R_{0}^{-1}+o(\varepsilon^{-1})
. However, in practice, we can compute
Q_{\varepsilon}
and want to estimate
P_{\varepsilon}
.
Since the direct direction comes from Lemma
1.20
, we shall
work under Hypothesis
1.12
:

Q_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}S+o(\varepsilon^{-1}),

where
S
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
.

In order to deduce the asymptotics of
P_{\varepsilon}
from those of
Q_{\varepsilon}
, we shall
consider an adherence point
R
of
\varepsilon^{-1}(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})
. Then, an application of
Lemma
1.19
yields that
R_{0}=S^{-1}
, and thus Theorem
0.1
.
The two main obstructions are:

•

the existence of an adherence point of
\varepsilon^{-1}(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})
. We shall prove that
\varepsilon^{-1}(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})
stays bounded when
\varepsilon
vanishes. This is the object of Subsection
4.1
.

•

in order to apply Lemma
1.19
, the matrix
R
needs to be irreducible. This is the object of Subsection
4.2
.

Our main tool is that Lemma
1.19
implies Theorem
0.1
whenever we induce on two sites, i.e. whenever
|I|=2
. This let us transfer precious information from
Q_{\varepsilon}
to
P_{\varepsilon}
.

4.1
Bootstrapping

We start our analysis with the case
|I|=2
, which can be deduced directly from Section
2
. The general case will follow.

If
|I|=2
, then
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
is
1
-dimensional, so
Q_{\varepsilon}
is a scalar, which we shall denote by
q_{\varepsilon}
. The matrix
P_{\varepsilon}
is bi-stochastic,
and thus can be written as

P_{\varepsilon}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1-p_{\varepsilon}&p_{\varepsilon}\\
p_{\varepsilon}&1-p_{\varepsilon}\end{array}\right)=\Id-p_{\varepsilon}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-1\\
-1&1\end{array}\right)=:\Id-p_{\varepsilon}R

(4.1)

for some constant
p_{\varepsilon}>0
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.12
. Assume furthermore that the matrix
S
from Hypothesis
1.12
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
. Assume finally that
|I|=2
, so that
S=s
is a scalar. Then

p_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon}{2s}.

Proof.

In the
|I|=2
case, Hypothesis
1.12
reads
q_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}s
.
The irreducibility of
S
in the sense of Definition
1.13
is equivalent to the property that
s>0
.

As a consequence,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}q_{\varepsilon}=+\infty
. This is only possible if the distance between the two sites of
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
grows to
+\infty
when
\varepsilon
vanishes. By
[
40
, Lemma 4.17]
, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}p_{\varepsilon}=0
: indeed, if the two sites are very far away one from the other, then the probability of transitioning from one to the other gets very small.

The matrix
R
in equation (
4.1
) is irreducible. Hence, by Lemma
1.19
,

q_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{p_{\varepsilon}}R_{0}^{-1}.

But
R(f)=2f
for all
f\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
, or in other words
R_{0}=2\Id
, so

q_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2p_{\varepsilon}}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}s,

from which the claim follows.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

The
|I|=2
case was one of the main results of
[
40
]
, where it was proved using an argument on the distributional asymptotics of the Birkhoff sums for
\widetilde{T}
of
\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)]}-\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j)]}
. Lemma
4.1
is already a more general variant of
[
40
, Corollary 2.9]
.

In addition, much of the proof of Lemma
1.19
can be simplified in the case
|I|=2
, since we can replace
matrices by scalars, giving a more streamlined proof of Lemma
4.1
. More generic tools, for instance from
[
29
]
, may be applicable.

The boundedness of the family
\varepsilon^{-1}(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})
in the general case readily follows.

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.12
. Assume furthermore that the matrix
S
from Hypothesis
1.12
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
.

Then
\Id-P_{\varepsilon}=O(\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

Let
i\neq j\in I
. Let
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}:=\{\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i),\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j)\}\subset\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. The potential operator
\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon}
associated with the induced map on
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}
can be recovered from
Q_{\varepsilon}
in the following way:

•

Take a function
f\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\{i,j\}}
. Extend
f
to a function
f\mathbf{1}_{\{i,j\}}\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
by setting
f\mathbf{1}_{\{i,j\}}(k):=0
for
k\notin\{i,j\}
.

•

Apply
Q_{\varepsilon}
to
f\mathbf{1}_{\{i,j\}}
to get a function
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
.

•

Restrict
g
to
\{i,j\}
, to get a function
g_{|\{i,j\}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\{i,j\}}
.

•

Add a constant to
g_{|\{i,j\}}
to get the function
\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon}f\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\{i,j\}}
.

The family
(\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
thus has the asymptotics

\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}\widehat{S}+o(\varepsilon^{-1}),

where
\widehat{S}
is the restriction of
S
to
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\{i,j\}}
composed with the projection onto
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\{i,j\}}
parallel to constants. Since
(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})
is supported on
\{i,j\}
and has zero sum,

\langle(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j}),\widehat{S}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\langle(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j}),S(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}>0,

so that
\widehat{S}
is also irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
. By Lemma
4.1
,

\mu\left((\widetilde{T}^{n}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)))_{n\geq 1}\text{ reaches }[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j)]\text{ before going back to }[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)]\right)\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon}{\langle(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j}),\widehat{S}(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}}.

(4.2)

But the left hand-side is larger than or equal to
(P_{\varepsilon})_{ij}
, so that all the off-diagonal terms of
P_{\varepsilon}
are in
O(\varepsilon)
.
∎

4.2
Irreducibility of the bi-L-matrix

We now end the proof of Theorem
0.1
when there are more than two sites. Up to extraction of a subsequence, we may assume that
\Id-P_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
. The matrix
R
is a bi-L-matrix. We want to show that
R_{0}^{-1}=S
. This is a consequence of Lemma
1.19
if
R
is irreducible. Our next focus is to prove
that
R
is indeed irreducible.

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume Hypotheses
1.2
,
1.18
and
1.12
.
Assume furthermore that the matrix
S
from Hypothesis
1.12
is irreducible
in the sense of Definition
1.13
.

Let
R
be an adherence point of
(\varepsilon^{-1}(\Id-P_{\varepsilon}))_{\varepsilon>0}
.
Then
R
is an irreducible bi-L-matrix.

Proof.

Let
R
be such an adherence point. Since
P_{\varepsilon}
is bi-stochastic by Lemma
1.4
and the space of bi-L-matrices is closed,
R
is a bi-L-matrix.

By Equation (
4.2
), under these hypotheses, for any two distinct
i
,
j\in I
,

\mu\left((\widetilde{T}^{n}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)))_{n\geq 1}\text{ reaches }[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j)]\text{ before going back to }[\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)]\right)=\Theta(\varepsilon).

Inducing on
[\Sigma_{\varepsilon}]
yields

\mu\left((T_{\varepsilon}^{n}(x,i))_{n\geq 1}\text{ reaches }[j]\text{ before going back to }[i]\right)=\Theta(\varepsilon).

We proceed by contradiction: if
R
is reducible, we prove that there exist
i
and
j
such that the above probability is an
o(\varepsilon)
.

Let
\sim_{R}
be the relation on
I
generated by
i\sim_{R}j
if
R_{ij}<0
and
i\sim_{R}i
. The relation
\sim_{R}
is reflexive and transitive. Since the counting measure is in the kernel of
R
, there are no absorbing subsets of
I
, so the relation
\sim_{R}
is also symmetric.

Assume that
R
is not irreducible. Let
I_{1}
be an equivalence class for
\sim_{R}
. Let
i\in I_{1}
and
j\in I\setminus I_{1}
. By definition of
\sim_{R}
, we have
P_{\varepsilon,k\ell}=o(\varepsilon)
for all
k\in I_{1}
and
\ell\in I\setminus I_{1}
. Setting

B_{\varepsilon,1}=\{(x,k)\in[I_{1}]:\ T_{\varepsilon}(x,k)\in[I\setminus I_{1}]\},

the set of points in
[I_{1}]
which exit
[I_{1}]
under
T_{\varepsilon}
, we get
\mu_{I_{1}}(B_{\varepsilon,1})=o(\varepsilon)
.

We want to estimate the probability, starting from
[i]
, to hit
[j]
before going
back to
[i]
. Let
T_{\varepsilon,1}:[I_{1}]\to[I_{1}]
be the map obtained by inducing
T_{\varepsilon}
on
[I_{1}]
. Let

\varphi_{[i]}^{T_{\varepsilon,1}}(x):=\inf\{n\geq 1:\ T_{\varepsilon,1}^{n}(x,i)\in[I]\}.

Note that

\left\{ (T_{\varepsilon}^{n}(x,i))_{n\geq 1}\text{ reaches }[j]\text{ before going back to }[i]\right\}\subset\left\{ \exists 0\leq n<\varphi_{[i]}^{T_{\varepsilon,1}}(x):\ T_{\varepsilon,1}^{n}(x,i)\in B_{\varepsilon,1}\right\},

whence, using Kac’s formula,

\displaystyle\mu\left( (T_{\varepsilon}^{n}(x,i))_{n\geq 1}\text{ reaches }[j]\text{ before going back to }[i]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left( \exists 0\leq n<\varphi_{[i]}^{T_{\varepsilon,1}}(x):\ T_{\varepsilon,1}^{n}(x,i)\in B_{\varepsilon,1}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\max_{0\leq n<\varphi_{[i]}^{T_{\varepsilon,1}}(x)}\mathbf{1}_{B_{\varepsilon,1}}\circ T_{\varepsilon,1}^{n}(x,i)\;{\rm d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A}\sum_{0\leq n<\varphi_{[i]}^{T_{\varepsilon,1}}(x)}\mathbf{1}_{B_{\varepsilon,1}}\circ T_{\varepsilon,1}^{n}(x,i)\;{\rm d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=|I_{1}|\mu_{I_{1}}(B_{\varepsilon,1})

\displaystyle=o(\varepsilon).

This gives the contradiction we want. Hence, our additional assumption is false:
R
is indeed irreducible.
∎

Let us wrap up to proof of Theorem
0.1
.

Proof of Theorem
0.1
.

The direct implication is the content of Lemma
1.19
; we prove the converse direction. Assume Hypotheses
1.2
and
1.12
. Assume furthermore that the matrix
S
from Hypothesis
1.12
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
.

By Lemma
4.3
, the family
(\varepsilon^{-1}(\Id-P_{\varepsilon}))_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded. Let
R
be one if its adherence points. By Lemma
4.4
,
R
is an irreducible bi-L-matrix.

Let
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be converging to
0
such that
P_{\varepsilon_{k}}=\Id-\varepsilon_{k}R+o(\varepsilon_{k})
. Since
R
is a bi-L-matrix, it is null on constant functions and preserves
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
. By Lemma
1.19
, we have
S=R_{0}^{-1}
, whence
R_{0}=S^{-1}
. This characterizes the action of
R
on
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
. Hence the matrix
R
is entirely characterized by
S
, and thus the adherence point of
(\varepsilon^{-1}(\Id-P_{\varepsilon}))_{\varepsilon>0}
is unique.
This proves that
P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon R+o(\varepsilon)
, and finishes the proof of Theorem
0.1
.
∎

5
Fourier transform and perturbations of the transfer operator

Let
(A,\mu,T)
be an ergodic Gibbs-Markov map with transfer operator
\mathcal{L}
acting on
\mathcal{B}
. Let
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be Markov, and assume that the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic. Let
(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Theorem
0.1
relates the transition matrices
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
to the potential operators
(Q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
.

Our goal is to compute, in a few cases, the asymptotics of the operators
(Q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
, and in particular the operator
S
appearing in Hypothesis
1.12
; from there we shall deduce, at least in some cases, the asymptotics of the transition matrices
(P_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
. First, we shall develop general tools involving perturbations of transfer operators.

In order to circumvent some summability issues which may arise in the computation of

(\Id-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{n},

we shall approximate this operator by

(\Id-\rho\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\rho^{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{n}

with
|\rho|<1
, and then take the limit as
\rho\to 1
. However, the requires working with a twisted Poisson equation and complicates significantly the relationship between
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. This will be the object of Subsection
5.1
, where we shall prove the following:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with image
\Sigma
.

Let
Q_{\sigma}:\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
be defined, as in Equation (
1.9
), by :

\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}:=|I| \int_{[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]})^{-1}\left(\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(i)]}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in I}g_{j}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(j)]}\right)\;{\rm d}\mu_{[\Sigma]}.

Then:

\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\sum_{n\geq 0} \rho^{n}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\mu\left(S_{n}F=\sigma(j)-\sigma(i)\right).

For random walks, the computation of
Q_{\varepsilon}=Q_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}
involves taking the Fourier transform of the distribution of
F
, or in other words, uses the characteristic function of
F
. As mentioned in Sub-subsection
1.5.4
, in a dynamical context, the role of this characteristic function is taken by the main eigenvalue of twisted transfer operators. We recall that these operators are defined, for
w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}_{2\pi\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, by

\mathcal{L}_{w}(\cdot)=\mathcal{L}(e^{i\langle w,F\rangle}\cdot),

(5.1)

which acts on
\mathbb{L}^{p}(A,\mu)
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
, as well as on
\mathcal{B}
.

In addition, we also define a Fourier transform for functions defined on
I
, transporting them first to
\Sigma
before taking their Fourier transform on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
:

Definition 5.2

(Fourier transform operator)
.

Let
d\geq 1
,
I
be finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
an injective map. We define for all
f\in\mathbb{C}^{I}
:

\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(f)(w):=\sum_{j\in I}f_{j}e^{-i\langle w,\sigma(j)\rangle}\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}

(5.2)

the Fourier transform of the function taking value
f_{j}
on
\sigma(j)
and
0
everywhere else. We also set
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)(w):=\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(f)(-w)
.

Given a family
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
of injective maps, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}
the corresponding Fourier transform operators.
