We want to understand the behaviour of
\sum_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{n}
using Fourier transform, which implies understanding the behaviour of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
for all
w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. This will be done in two steps. In Subsection
5.2
,
we explore the eigenvalues of modulus
1
of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
in order to ensure that
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}
is well-defined. Then, in Subsection
5.3
, we apply this study together with Proposition
5.1
to get the proposition mentioned in Sub-subsection
1.5.4
:

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with image
\Sigma
. Then, for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
:

\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\left(\int_{A}(\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\right)\;{\rm d}w.

(5.3)

In particular, let
U
be a small enough neighbourhood of
0\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Let
\lambda_{w}
be the main eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
such that
\lambda_{0}=1
, which is well-defined and continuous on
U
. Then:

\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}} \int_{U}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\frac{1+\delta_{w}}{1-\rho\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}w+O_{U}(1)\left\|f\right\|\cdot\left\|g\right\|,

(5.4)

where
w\mapsto\delta_{w}
is continuous on a neighbourhood of
0\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
with
\delta_{0}=0
, and the error term
O_{U}(1)
is uniform in
\rho
and
\sigma
but depends on
U
.

Conjecture 5.4
.

If
|1-\lambda_{w}|\geq c\left\|w\right\|^{2}
for some
c>0
, or even
|1-\lambda_{w}|\geq c\left\|w\right\|^{3-\iota}
for some
c
,
\iota>0
, then Equation (
5.4
) can be simplified, using the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(f)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(f)
are both
O(\left\|w\right\|)
near
0
:

\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}} \int_{U}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\frac{1+\delta_{\rho,w}}{1-\rho\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}w=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}} \int_{U}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\frac{1+\delta_{w}}{1-\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}w.

The condition
|1-\lambda_{w}|\geq c\left\|w\right\|^{2}
is true for ergodic random walks, and all examples in Section
6
. A general argument still eludes the author.

Equation (
5.4
) is exactly what we need to compute the asymptotics of
(Q_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
from the asymptotics of the main eigenvalue of the twisted transfer operators
\mathcal{L}_{w}
. Applications will be given in Section
6
, where we shall explore a few examples with more constraints on the family
(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
and the jump function
F
: extensions with square-integrable jumps in dimensions
1
and
2
, as well as extensions with jumps in the basin of attraction of a Lévy stable distribution in dimension
d=1
.

5.1
Twisted Poisson equations

Our first goal is to prove Proposition
5.1
. Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with image
\Sigma
.

Definition 5.5
.

For all
\rho\in B(0,1)
, let
Q_{\sigma,\rho}:\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
be the operator such that, for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\langle f,Q_{\sigma,\rho}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}:=\int_{[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\rho^{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\left(\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(i)]}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in I}g_{j}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(j)]}\right)\;{\rm d}\widetilde{\mu}.

Note that the sum is well-defined since
\rho\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
is a strict contraction on
\mathbb{L}^{1}([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu})
.
We define
Q_{\sigma,1}
as in Equation (
1.9
), replacing
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}

by
\Sigma
.

Note that, for all
n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\left(\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(i)]}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in I}g_{j}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(j)]}\right)\;{\rm d}\widetilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=\int_{[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]}\left(\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(i)]}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in I}g_{j}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(j)]}\right)\circ\widetilde{T}^{n}\;{\rm d}\widetilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\int_{[\sigma(i)]}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(j)]}\circ\widetilde{T}^{n}\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\mu\left(S_{n}F=\sigma(j)-\sigma(i)\right),

so we need to prove that
\lim_{\rho\to 1}Q_{\sigma,\rho}=Q_{\sigma,1}
.

Note that
[I]
is endowed with a partition
\alpha_{\Sigma}
generated by
\varphi_{[\Sigma]}
. More precisely, elements of
\alpha_{\Sigma}
in
[i]\subset[I]
are elements of the partition
\alpha_{i,\Sigma}
generated by the stopping time

\varphi_{i,[\Sigma]}:=\inf\{n\geq 1:\ \sigma(i)+S_{n}F\in\Sigma\}.

That said, we will need the following

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with image
\Sigma
.

Let
\varphi:[I]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
a nonnegative function constant on the elements of the partition
\alpha_{\Sigma}
of
[I]
. Then:

•

the family of operators
(\mathcal{L}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
acting on
\mathcal{B}_{I}
and defined by
\mathcal{L}_{k}(f):=\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}(e^{-k\varphi}f)
is continuous in the operator topology.

•

for
k
small enough, there exists a continuous family of eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}
of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
such that
\lambda_{0}=1
.

•

setting
H(k):=\int_{[I]}(1-e^{-k\varphi})\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}
, we have
(1-\lambda_{k})\sim_{k\to 0}H(k)
.

•

\left\|\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}_{k}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}\to\mathcal{B}_{I}}=O(H(k))
.

Proof.

We start by proving a strengthened version of the last point. We introduce the auxiliary semi-norm:

|f|_{\Lip^{1}([I])}:=\sum_{p\in\Sigma}\sum_{a\in\alpha_{p,\Sigma}}\mu(a)|f(\cdot,p)|_{\Lip(a)},

and the auxiliary norm
\left\|f\right\|_{\Lip^{1}([I])}:=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}+|f|_{\Lip^{1}([I])}
.

Let
k
,
k^{\prime}\geq 0
and
f\in\mathcal{B}_{I}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\|e^{-k\varphi}f-e^{-k^{\prime}\varphi}f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|e^{-\min\{k,k^{\prime}\}\varphi}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([I],\mu_{I})}\left\|1-e^{-|k-k^{\prime}|\varphi}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([I],\mu_{I})}

\displaystyle\leq H(|k-k^{\prime}|)\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([I],\mu_{I})}.

In addition, seeing
\varphi
as a function from
\alpha_{\Sigma}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\left|e^{-k\varphi}f-e^{-k^{\prime}\varphi}f\right|_{\Lip^{1}([I])}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\alpha_{\Sigma}}\mu_{I}(a)\left|(e^{-k\varphi}-e^{-k^{\prime}\varphi})f\right|_{\Lip(a)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\alpha_{\Sigma}}\mu_{I}(a)\left|e^{-k\varphi(a)}-e^{-k^{\prime}\varphi(a)}\right|\cdot|f|_{\Lip(a)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{a\in\alpha_{\Sigma}}\mu_{I}(a)\left|1-e^{-|k-k^{\prime}|\varphi(a)}\right|\right)\sup_{a\in\alpha_{\Sigma}}|f|_{\Lip(a)}

\displaystyle\leq H(|k-k^{\prime}|)\max_{i\in I}\sup_{a\in\alpha^{*}}|f(\cdot,i)|_{\Lip(a)},

where we used in the last step that
\alpha^{*}
is coarser than
\alpha_{i,\Sigma}
for all
i\in I
. Writing
(e^{-k\varphi}\cdot)
the operator of multiplication by
e^{-k\varphi}
, these two computations together yield

\left\|(e^{-k\varphi}\cdot)-(e^{-k^{\prime}\varphi}\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}\to\Lip^{1}([I])}\leq H(|k-k^{\prime}|).

By Proposition
A.11
, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}:\Lip^{1}([I])\to\mathcal{B}_{I}
is bounded. Composition of the multiplication operators and
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}
gives

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{k}-\mathcal{L}_{k^{\prime}}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}\to\mathcal{B}_{I}}=O(H(|k-k^{\prime}|)),

finishing the proof of the fourth point.

By the dominated convergence theorem,
\lim_{0^{+}}H=0
, finishing the proof of the first point.

Since
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic, so is
([I],\mu_{I},T_{\Sigma})
, so
1
is an isolated eigenvalue of multiplicity
1
of
\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}
. The second point follows from
[
28
, Part IV.3.5]
.

All is left to prove is the third point. Let
(h_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be the family of eigenfunctions of
(\mathcal{L}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
corresponding the the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
and such that
\mu_{I}(h_{k})=1
, for all small enough
k
. Then
(h_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is continuous and
h_{0}=\mathbf{1}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle 1-\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{[I]}(\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}-\mathcal{L}_{k})h_{k}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=\int_{[I]}(1-e^{-k\varphi})h_{k}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=H(k)+\int_{[I]}(1-e^{-k\varphi})(h_{k}-\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}.

Again using
[
28
, Part IV.3.5]
,
\left\|\mathbf{1}-h_{k}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=O\left(\left\|\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]}-\mathcal{L}_{k}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}\to\mathcal{B}_{I}}\right)=O(H(k))
,
so that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{[I]}(1-e^{-k\varphi})(h_{k}-\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|1-e^{-k\varphi}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{1}([I],\mu_{I})}\left\|\mathbf{1}-h_{k}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([I],\mu_{I})}

\displaystyle\leq H(k)\left\|\mathbf{1}-h_{k}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}

\displaystyle=O(H(k)^{2}),

whence
(1-\lambda_{k})\sim_{k\to 0}H(k)
.
∎

We now proceed to the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

By
[
41
, Lemma 1.7]
, for all
\rho\in(0,1)
and
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\frac{1}{|I|}\int_{[\Sigma]}(\Id-\rho\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\left(\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(i)]}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in I}g_{j}\mathbf{1}_{[\sigma(j)]}\right)\;{\rm d}\widetilde{\mu}=\int_{[I]}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{I}(e^{\ln(\rho)\varphi_{[\Sigma]}}\cdot))^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f)\cdot\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

where the operator
(\Id-\rho\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}
is defined on
\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu})
and the operator
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{I}(e^{\ln(\rho)\varphi_{[\Sigma]}}\cdot))^{-1}

on
\mathbb{L}^{\infty}([I],\mu_{I})
.

We set
k:=-\ln(\rho)\geq 0
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{k}:=\mathcal{L}_{I}(e^{-k\varphi_{[\Sigma]}}\cdot)
. By Lemma
5.6
, there exists an eigendecomposition of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
, continuous in
k
, such that

\mathcal{L}_{k}=\lambda_{k}h_{k}\otimes\mu_{I,k}+Z_{k},

with
\mu_{I,k}(h_{k})=\mu_{I}(h_{k})=1
for all
k
and
Z_{k}
the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
to
\Ker(\mu_{k})\subset\mathcal{B}_{I}
. Note that
Z_{k}
converges to the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{I}
to
\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
, and
h_{k}
to
\mathbf{1}
as well as
\mu_{I,k}
to
\mu_{I}
.

We then get for
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\langle f,Q_{\sigma,\rho}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to 0^{+}}|I|\int_{[I]}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{k})^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f)\cdot\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to 0^{+}}\left(|I|\int_{[I]}(\Id-Z_{k})^{-1}(\Pi^{*}(f)-\mu_{I,k}(\Pi^{*}(f))h_{k})\cdot\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.+|I|\int_{[I]}\frac{\Pi^{*}(g)h_{k}\mu_{I,k}(\Pi^{*}(f))}{1-\lambda_{k}}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}\right).

On the one hand, since
\Pi^{*}(f)\in\mathcal{B}_{I,0}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to 0^{+}}|I|\int_{[I]}(\Id-Z_{k})^{-1}

\displaystyle(\Pi^{*}(f)-\mu_{I,k}(\Pi^{*}(f))h_{k})\cdot\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to 0^{+}}|I|\int_{[I]}(\Id-Z_{k})^{-1}(\Pi^{*}(f))\cdot\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=|I|\int_{[I]}(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{[\Sigma]})^{-1}\Pi^{*}(f)\cdot\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}.

On the other hand,
\left\|\mathbf{1}-h_{k}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}_{I}}=O(H(k))
,
as well as
\mu_{I,k}(\Pi^{*}(g))=O(H(k))
and
(1-\lambda_{k})\sim_{k\to 0^{+}}H(k)
,
where
H(k)=\int_{[I]}(1-e^{-k\varphi_{[\Sigma]}})\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}
.
Hence

\displaystyle|I|\int_{[I]}\frac{\Pi^{*}(g)h_{k}\mu_{I,k}(\Pi^{*}(f))}{1-\lambda_{k}}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}

\displaystyle=\int_{[I]}\Pi^{*}(g)h_{k}\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}\cdot O(1)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{[I]}\Pi^{*}(g)\;{\rm d}\mu_{I}+O(H(k))\right)O(1)

\displaystyle=O(H(k)).

The claim follows from the fact that
\lim_{0^{+}}H=0
.
∎

5.2
Transfer operators and periodicities

After taking a Fourier transform, we will have to work with the inverse of twisted transfer operators
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}
. We need to find the values of
w
such that
1\in\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{w}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})
in order to control these inverse operators. This leads us to a refinement of the spectral analysis done in
[
40
]
, in particular
[
40
, Proposition 3.2]
, so that we can remove the assumption of aperiodicity present in that article.

The action of
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathcal{B}
is quasi-compact: it admits
1
as a simple eigenvalue, has spectral radius
1
and essential spectral radius
\Lambda^{-1}<1
, where
\Lambda
is the expansivity constant of the Gibbs-Markov map. The essential
spectral radius of the operators
\mathcal{L}_{w}
is also bounded by
\Lambda^{-1}
, as they satisfy a Lasota-Yorke inequality with the same constants.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map

(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
with jump function
F
. Assume that
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic.

Let
M:=|\{\lambda\in\Sp(\mathcal{L}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B}):\ |\lambda|=1\}|
. Then there exist an integer
n\geq 1
and an element

w_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
of primitive order
n
such that

H:=\{(w,\lambda)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}:\ \lambda\in\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{w}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})\}=\left\{\left(kw_{0},e^{2\pi i\frac{k}{Mn}}\right):\ k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/Mn\mathbb{Z}}\right\}.

In addition, each of these eigenvalues is simple.

Proof.

The argument of
[
52
, Lemma 3.1]
can be adapted to prove that
H
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The map
T
is not invertible. However, if
h
is a non-zero function such that
\mathcal{L}_{w}(h)=\lambda h
for some
(w,\lambda)\in H
, then, by the equality case of the triangular inequality,
h
is constant on almost every subset
T^{-1}(x)
for
x\in A
,
so that the computations in the proof of
[
52
, Lemma 3.1]
stay valid. In addition, each of these eigenvalues is simple.

Let
w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be such that
1\in\Sp(\mathcal{L}_{w}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})
. Let
h_{w,1}
be an eigenfunction for this eigenvalue. Then
\mathcal{L}(e^{i\langle w,F\rangle}h_{w,1})=h_{w,1}
. Define
f(x,p):=e^{-i\langle w,p\rangle}h_{w,1}(x)
on
[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
. As we check,
f
is invariant under the transfer operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(f)(x,p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y,q)\in\widetilde{T}^{-1}(\{(x,p)\})}\frac{f(y,q)}{|\Jac(T)(y)|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in T^{-1}(\{x\})}\frac{f(y,p-F(y))}{|\Jac(T)(y)|}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\langle w,p\rangle}\sum_{y\in T^{-1}(\{x\})}\frac{e^{i\langle w,F\rangle(y)}h_{w,1}(y)}{|\Jac(T)(y)|}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\langle w,p\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{w}(h_{w,1})(x)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\langle w,p\rangle}h_{w,1}(x)

\displaystyle=f(x,p).

Since
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic,
f
is constant, and in particular the map
p\mapsto e^{-i\langle w,p\rangle}
is constant. Hence
w=0
in
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. In other words, the canonical projection
H\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
is injective. Hence
H
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
\mathbb{S}_{1}
, and thus cyclic.

Let
\mathbb{U}_{M}
be the group of peripheral eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}
. Then
\{0\}\times\mathbb{U}_{M}

is a subgroup of
H
, so
M
divides
|H|
. Setting
n:=|H|/M
, there exists
w_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}

such that

H=\left\{\left(kw_{0},e^{2\pi i\frac{k}{Mn}}\right):\ k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/Mn\mathbb{Z}}\right\}.

Taking
k=n
, we get that
\left(nw_{0},e^{2\pi i\frac{1}{M}}\right)\in H
. But its image under the second projection is
e^{2\pi i\frac{1}{M}}\in\Sp(\mathcal{L}\curvearrowright\mathcal{B})
, so by injectivity of the second projection,
nw_{0}=0
. In addition,
kw_{0}\neq 0
for
0<k<n
; otherwise, we would get an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}
not in
\mathbb{U}_{M}
.
∎

Lemma
5.7
let us control the operators
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}
.

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be a Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map

(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
with jump function
F
. Assume that
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic.

Then, for any neighbourhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

\sup_{w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus U}\left\|(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}}<+\infty.

(5.5)

Proof.

For
w\neq 0
, the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
does not contain
1
by Lemma
5.7
, so that
(\Id-\mathcal{L}_{w})
is invertible. By the discussion following the proof of
[
21
, Corollary 4.1.3]
, the family of operators
(\mathcal{L}_{w})_{w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}
is continuous, so the family of operators
\left((\Id-\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}\right)_{w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}
is also continuous. The result follows by relative compactness of
\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus U
in
\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.
∎

5.3
Fourier transform and twisted transfer operators

As announced, we now prove Proposition
5.3
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with image
\Sigma
.

Let
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
. By Proposition
5.1
,

\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\sum_{n\geq 0} \rho^{n}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\mu\left(S_{n}F=\sigma(j)-\sigma(i)\right).

Let
n\geq 0
and
i
,
j\in I
. Using the Fourier transform,

\displaystyle\mu\left(S_{n}F=\sigma(j)-\sigma(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\mathbf{1}_{0}(S_{n}F+\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}e^{i\langle w,S_{n}F+\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\rangle}\;{\rm d}w\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}e^{i\langle w,\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\rangle}\int_{A}e^{i\langle w,S_{n}F\rangle}\;{\rm d}\mu\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}e^{i\langle w,\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\rangle}\int_{A}\mathcal{L}_{w}^{n}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\;{\rm d}w.

Summing over
i
,
j\in I
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}

\displaystyle\mu\left(S_{n}F=\sigma(j)-\sigma(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}e^{i\langle w,\sigma(i)\rangle}\right)\left(\sum_{j\in I}g_{j}e^{-i\langle w,\sigma(j)\rangle}\right)\left(\int_{A}\mathcal{L}_{w}^{n}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\right)\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\left(\int_{A}\mathcal{L}_{w}^{n}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\right)\;{\rm d}w,

whence, weighting by
\rho^{n}
and summing over
n
,

\displaystyle\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\sum_{n\geq 0}\rho^{n}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\left(\int_{A}\mathcal{L}_{w}^{n}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\right)\;{\rm d}w,

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\left(\int_{A}(\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\right)\;{\rm d}w.

This proves Equation (
5.3
).

Since
w\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{w}
is continuous and
1
is an isolated eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}
, for any small enough neighbourhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, there exists a continuous eigendecomposition:

\mathcal{L}_{w}=\lambda_{w}h_{w}\otimes\mu_{w}+Z_{w},

with
\mu_{w}(h_{w})=\mu(h_{w})=1
for all
w\in-U
, as well as
\lambda_{0}=1
. In addition, up to taking a smaller neighbourhood
U
, the operators
\left((\Id-\rho Z_{w})^{-1}\right)_{\rho\geq 0,w\in U}
are uniformly bounded by continuity. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{A}(\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\frac{\mu_{w}(\mathbf{1})h_{w}}{1-\rho\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}\mu+\int_{A}(\Id-\rho Z_{w})^{-1}(\mathbf{1}-\mu_{w}(\mathbf{1})h_{w})\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{w}(\mathbf{1})}{1-\rho\lambda_{w}}+O(1).

In addition, the family of operators
\left((\Id-\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}\right)_{w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus U}
is bounded by Corollary
5.8
. By continuity, the family of operators
\left((\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}\right)_{\rho\geq 0,w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus U}
is also bounded, and

\sup_{\rho\geq 0,w\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus U}\left|\int_{A}(\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\right|<+\infty.

Putting everything together, we get

\langle f,Q_{\sigma}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\sigma}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}(g)\frac{\mu_{w}(\mathbf{1})}{1-\rho\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}w+O_{U}(1)\left\|f\right\|\cdot\left\|g\right\|.

Setting
\delta_{w}:=\mu_{w}(\mathbf{1})-1
, which converges to
0
when
w
vanishes, yields Proposition
5.3
.
∎

6
Computation of the potential operators

With the groundwork done in Section
5
, we are ready to compute the asymptotics of the transition matrices in a few important cases. We shall consider sets
(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
with a limiting shape as introduced in Subsection
1.4.1
. We shall also add classical assumptions on the tails of the distribution the jump
F
, for instance square-integrability or being in the basin of attraction of a Lévy stable distribution.

In this section, we assume that
([\mathbb{Z}^{d}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\mu,T)
with jump function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We choose
(\sigma_{t})_{t>0}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that, for all
i\in I
,

\sigma_{t}(i)=t\sigma(i)+o(t),

as in Figure
2
. This point of view, where
t
is of the same order as the distance between sites of
\Sigma_{t}
, seems more intuitive, and shall be used to write the propositions. Letting
\varepsilon:=t^{-1}
, the asymptotics
t\to+\infty
correspond to asymptotic
\varepsilon\to 0
as in the previous parts of this work, and the variable
\varepsilon
shall be used in lieu of
t
in the demonstrations.

Once this setting is fixed, most computations rely on the same strategies as
[
40
, Subsection 3.5]
. An important difference is that we compute a matrix
S
such that
Q_{\varepsilon}\sim a(\varepsilon)S
for some function
a(\varepsilon)
, and then invert
S
, while
[
40
]
only involved the computation of scalars. This adds a layer of linear algebra, which we solve in a few cases, but not in full generality. As a consequence, we split this section in four cases:

•

square-integrable jump functions in dimension
1
(Subsection
6.1
);

•

jump functions in the basin of attraction of a Lévy stable distribution with parameter in
(1,2)
in dimension
1
(Subsection
6.2
), for which the author was not able to invert
S
in full generality;

•

some jump functions in the basin of attraction of a Cauchy distribution (Subsection
6.3
);

•

square-integrable jump functions in dimension
2
(Subsection
6.4
), with a behaviour
in fine
similar to the Cauchy case but quite different computations.

There are a few specialized cases which we do not write down, although they may be of some practical interest. In particular, we leave to the interested reader the situation where
F
has tails with regular variation of index
2
, and thus lies in the basin in attraction of a Gaussian, while not being square-integrable. This specific situation can occur as a toy model for Lorentz gases in infinite horizon, for which the diffusion channels give rise to supercritical diffusion
[
8
,
49
]
.

6.1
Computation of the potential:
d=1
, square integrable jumps

In addition to the general setting of Section
6
, we assume in this Subsection that
F
is square-integrable and centered, the later condition being already implied by the first condition and the ergodicity of the extension.

Let
\Var
be the asymptotic variance
F
:

\Var:=\int_{A}F^{2}\;{\rm d}\mu+2\sum_{n\geq 1}\int_{A}F\circ T^{n}\cdot F\;{\rm d}\mu,

(6.1)

where the infinite series is taken in the sense of Cesàro. Since
([\mathbb{Z}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic,
F
is not
a coboundary and thus
\Var
is positive
[
21
, Théorème 4.1.4]
.

We shall now get our first application of Theorem
0.1
:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\mu,T)
with jump function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}
. Assume furthermore that
F\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(A,\mu)
. Let
\Var>0
be that asymptotic variance of
F
defined by Equation (
6.1
).

Let
I=\{1,\ldots,|I|\}
and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be injective and increasing. For
t>0
, let
\sigma_{t}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\sigma_{t}(i)=t\sigma(i)+o(t)
for all
i\in I
. Let
P_{t}
be the transition matrix of the Markov chain induced on
\Sigma_{t}:=\sigma_{t}(I)
. Then

P_{t}=_{t\to+\infty}\Id-\frac{\Var}{t}R+o(t^{-1}),

where
R
is the irreducible, tridiagonal, symmetric bi-L-matrix defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcll}R_{i,i+1}&=&-\frac{1}{2(\sigma(i+1)-\sigma(i))}&\quad\forall\ 1\leq i<|I|,\\
R_{i,i-1}&=&-\frac{1}{2(\sigma(i)-\sigma(i-1))}&\quad\forall\ 1<i\leq|I|,\\
R_{ij}&=&0&\quad\text{whenever }|i-j|\geq 2,\\
R_{ii}&=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in I\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\left|R_{ij}\right|&\quad\forall\ i\in I.\end{array}\right.

(6.2)

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
6.1
, and setting
\Delta_{i}^{j}:=|\sigma(j)-\sigma(i)|
,
the transition matrices
(P_{t})_{t>0}
have the following graphical representation:

P_{t}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{1}^{2}}&\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{1}^{2}}&0&\ldots&0&0\\
\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{1}^{2}}&1-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{1}^{2}}-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{2}^{3}}&\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{2}^{3}}&\ldots&0&0\\
0&\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{2}^{3}}&1-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{2}^{3}}-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{3}^{4}}&\ldots&0&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\ldots&1-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{|I|-2}^{|I|-1}}-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{|I|-1}^{|I|}}&\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{|I|-1}^{|I|}}\\
0&0&0&\ldots&\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{|I|-1}^{|I|}}&1-\frac{2\Var}{t\Delta_{|I|-1}^{|I|}}\end{array}\right)+o(t^{-1}).

Proof.

We assume the setting of Proposition
6.1
. By
[
21
, Théorème 4.1.4]
, the main eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
admits the expansion

\lambda_{w}=1-\frac{\Var w^{2}}{2}+o(w^{2}).

(6.3)

We set
\varepsilon:=t^{-1}
. By Proposition
5.3
, for any small enough neighbourhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{T}
,
for all
\varepsilon>0
, for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\displaystyle\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\int_{A}(\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w})^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{U}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{1+\delta_{w}}{1-\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}w+O_{U}(1)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|.

Note that
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)(w)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)(w)
are both in
O(|w|)
while the denominator is in
\Theta(w^{2})
by Equation (
6.3
), so the integrand is bounded for each
\varepsilon>0
. This justifies that we took the limit in Equation (
5.4
).

Let us set

1+\delta^{\prime}_{w}:=(1+\delta_{w})\frac{\Var w^{2}}{2(1-\lambda_{w})},

so that
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
is also a continuous function of
w
with
\lim_{w\to 0}\delta^{\prime}_{w}=0
. In addition, we choose
U=(-\eta,\eta)
for small enough
\eta>0
. Then:

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{1}{\pi\Var}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{1+\delta^{\prime}_{w}}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w+O_{\eta}(1)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|.

(6.4)

We split this integral in three parts:

•

the integral involving the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on a small neighbourhood
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
of
0
, which shall be negligible because both
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
and the term
\left(\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)(w)
are small near
0
.

•

the integral involving the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, which shall be negligible because
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
is small and
w^{2}
is large.

•

the integral without the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
, which is the main contribution
to
\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}
.

Control of
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on the neighbourhood
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

To control the part of the integral (
6.4
) involving
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, remark that, since both
f
and
g
have zero sum,
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
are in
O(w)
for all
\varepsilon>0
. More precisely,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}e^{iw\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\left(e^{iw(\varepsilon^{-1}\sigma(i)+o(\varepsilon^{-1}))}-1\right)

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon^{-1}|w|\left\|f\right\|),

(6.5)

and likewise for
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
. Hence

\displaystyle\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{\delta^{\prime}_{w}}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}O\left(\varepsilon^{-2}w^{2}\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\right)\frac{\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}\left|\delta^{\prime}_{w}\right|}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=O\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}\left|\delta^{\prime}_{w}\right|\right).

Control of the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

On
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, we get

\displaystyle\left| \int_{(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{\delta^{\prime}_{w}}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}\frac{\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}\left|\delta^{\prime}_{w}\right|}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w\cdot\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}\left|\delta^{\prime}_{w}\right|\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1,1)}\frac{1}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w\cdot\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}\left|\delta^{\prime}_{w}\right|).

Main contribution to the integral (
6.4
).

Now let us focus on the remaining term. Let us split
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
along its frequencies:

\displaystyle\left(\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)(w)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}e^{iw\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)}\right)\left(\sum_{j\in I}g_{j}e^{-iw\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}e^{iw(\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\left(e^{iw(\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j))}-1\right).

Injecting this expression into the main part of Equation (
6.4
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi\Var}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}

\displaystyle\frac{\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi\Var}\int_{0}^{\eta}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\frac{\cos\left(w(\sigma_{\varepsilon}(i)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(j))\right)-1}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi\Var}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\int_{0}^{\eta}\frac{\cos\left(\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))\right)-1}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\varepsilon\pi\Var}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\left(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1)\right)\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-1}(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))\eta}\frac{\cos(w)-1}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=_{\varepsilon\to 0}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\Var}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|+o(\varepsilon^{-1}),

were the last line comes from the well-known integral
[
18
, Chap. XVII.4,
4.11
]

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\cos(w)-1}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w=-\frac{\pi}{2}.

Asymptotics of the potential
Q_{\varepsilon}
.

Adding the terms involving
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
to the main contribution yields

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left|\varepsilon\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+\frac{1}{\Var}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\varepsilon O_{\eta}(1)+O(\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}\left|\delta_{w}\right|)\right)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|

\displaystyle=O(\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta_{w}|)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|;

since the above holds for all small enough
\eta>0
and
\lim_{w\to 0}\delta_{w}=0
, we get finally that

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\Var}\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|.

Let
S:\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}^{I}
be the operator defined by

\langle f,S(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=-\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|.

Then
S
is symmetric and irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
. Indeed, for all
i\neq j\in I
,

\langle\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j},S(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=2\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|>0.

Let
R^{\prime}
be the unique irreducible bi-L-matrix such that
R^{\prime}_{0}=S^{-1}
. By Theorem
0.1
,

P_{\varepsilon}=\Id-\varepsilon\Var R^{\prime}+o(\varepsilon);

setting
t=\varepsilon^{-1}
, all is left is to check that
R^{\prime}=R
, or in other words, that
R_{0}=S^{-1}
.

Asymptotics of the transition matrix
P_{\varepsilon}
.

We now use the assumption that
I=\{1,\ldots,|I|\}
and that
\sigma
is increasing, which always holds up to permutation of
I
. We shall work with a convenient basis for
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
. Set
e_{i}^{i+1}:=\mathbf{1}_{i+1}-\mathbf{1}_{i}
for
1\leq i<|I|
. Set also
\Delta_{i}^{j}:=\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|
.

Let
\widehat{S}:\mathbb{C}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}^{I}
such that
\widehat{S}_{ij}=-\Delta_{i}^{j}
, so that
S
is the composition of
\widehat{S}_{|\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}:\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}^{I}
with the projection parallel to constants from
\mathbb{C}^{I}
to
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
. Then, for
1\leq i<|I|
and
j\in I
,

(\widehat{S}e_{i}^{i+1})_{j}=\Delta_{i}^{j}-\Delta_{i+1}^{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}-\Delta_{i}^{i+1}&\text{ if }&j\leq i,\\
\Delta_{i}^{i+1}&\text{ if }&j\geq i+1.\end{array}\right.

For
j<i
, the non-zero coefficients of the
j
th line of
R
are all in the range
\{1,\ldots,i\}
, so that

(R\widehat{S}e_{i}^{i+1})_{j}=-\Delta_{i}^{i+1}(R\mathbf{1})_{j}=0.

Likewise,
(R\widehat{S}e_{i}^{i+1})_{j}=0
for
j\geq i+2
. The last two cases
j=i
and
j=i+1
finally yield
R\widehat{S}e_{i}^{i+1}=e_{i}^{i+1}
for all
i
. As
R
preserves
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
, its restriction
R_{0}
to
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
is the inverse of
S
.
∎

Remark 6.2

(Construction of
R
)
.

One can also use a probabilistic argument which illuminates the choice of the matrix
R
. As the asymptotics is, up to the variance, the same for all processes with
\mathbb{L}^{2}
increments, it is enough to compute
R
in a specific example. For the simple
random walk, in particular,
R
can be computed explicitly by a martingale argument.

6.2
Computation of the potential:
d=1
, Lévy jumps

We are now interested in processes in the basin of attraction of a Lévy stable distribution. We recall the definition of regular variation:

Definition 6.3

(Regular and slow variation)
.

For any real
\alpha
, a real-valued, measurable, positive function
L
defined on an interval
[x_{0},+\infty)
is said to have
regular variation of index
\alpha
at infinity
if, for all
x>0
,

\lim_{y\to+\infty}\frac{L(xy)}{L(y)}=x^{\alpha}.

A function
L
defined on an interval
(0,x_{0}]
is said to have
regular variation of index
\alpha
at
0
if
x\mapsto L(1/x)
has regular variation of index
-\alpha
at infinity.

A function with regular variation of index
0
is also said to have
slow variation
.

In addition to the general setting of Section
6
, we assume that there exist
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
c_{-}
,
c_{+}\geq 0
not both zero and
L
with regular variation of index
\alpha
at infinity such that

\displaystyle\mu\left(F\geq x\right)

\displaystyle\sim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{c_{+}}{L(x)},

\displaystyle\mu\left(F\leq-x\right)

\displaystyle\sim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{c_{-}}{L(x)}.

In this setting, we are able to give explicit asymptotics for the potential matrices, but not to invert them in general. We aim to prove:

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\mu,T)
with jump function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}
. Assume furthermore that there exists
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
c_{-}
,
c_{+}\geq 0
not both zero and
L
with regular variation of index
\alpha
at infinity such that

\displaystyle\mu\left(F\geq x\right)

\displaystyle\sim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{c_{+}}{L(x)},

\displaystyle\mu\left(F\leq-x\right)

\displaystyle\sim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{c_{-}}{L(x)}.

Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be injective. For
t>0
, let
\sigma_{t}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\sigma_{t}(i)=t\sigma(i)+o(t)
for all
i\in I
. Let
P_{t}
be the transition matrix of the Markov chain induced on
\Sigma_{t}:=\sigma_{t}(I)
and
Q_{t}:\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
the associated family of potential matrices. Then, for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\displaystyle\langle f,Q_{t}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=_{t\to+\infty}\frac{\sin(\alpha\pi)}{\pi\left[c_{+}^{2}+c_{-}^{2}+2c_{+}c_{-}\cos(\alpha\pi)\right]}\frac{L(t)}{t}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|^{\alpha-1}\left(c_{+}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma(j)>\sigma(i)}+c_{-}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma(j)<\sigma(i)}\right)+o\left(\frac{L(t)}{t}\right).

(6.6)

As a consequence, there exists an irreducible bi-L-matrix
R
such that

P_{t}=_{t\to+\infty}\Id-\frac{t}{L(t)}R+o\left(\frac{t}{L(t)}\right).

(6.7)

Note that, as soon as
c_{+}\neq c_{-}
(i.e.
F
is in the basin of attraction of an asymmetric Lévy distribution), the operator
S
is not symmetric, and thus
R
is not symmetric either.

Proof.

The structure of this demonstration follows that of the proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We assume the setting of Proposition
6.1
. By
[
4
, Theorem 5.1]
5
5

5

In that theorem,
(A,\mu,T)
is assumed to be mixing; however, ergodicity is enough, as can be seen e.g. by working with
(A,\mu,T^{M})
for
M\geq 1
the period of
T
. In addition, we correct here a typographic mistake in the expression of
\Re\ln\lambda_{w}
.
, there exist
\vartheta>0
,
\zeta\in[-1,1]
and a neighbourhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
such that, for all
w\in U
,

\lambda_{w}=e^{-\vartheta(1-i\zeta\tan(\pi\alpha/2)\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}}+o(L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}).

Without loss of generality,
L
is assumed to be continuous. The sign function
\sgn
in the above equation takes value
1
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
and
-1
on
\mathbb{R}_{-}^{*}
. The constants
\vartheta
and
\zeta
can in addition be expressed as functions of the initial data on
F
:

\displaystyle\vartheta

\displaystyle=(c_{-}+c_{+})\Gamma(1-\alpha)\cos\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right),

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{+}-c_{-}}{c_{+}+c_{-}}.

In particular, the parameter
\vartheta
quantifies the dispersion of
F
, while
\zeta
quantifies its asymmetry. To make computations slightly lighter, we put
\zeta^{\prime}:=\zeta\tan(\pi\alpha/2)
.

We set
\varepsilon:=t^{-1}
. By Proposition
5.3
, for any small enough neighbourhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{T}
,
for all
\varepsilon>0
, for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\displaystyle\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{U}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{1+\delta_{w}}{1-\lambda_{w}}\;{\rm d}w+O_{U}(1)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|.

Note that
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)(w)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)(w)
are both in
O(|w|)
while the denominator is bounded from below by
c^{\prime}|w|^{2}
for some
c^{\prime}>0
by Equation (
6.3
) and Potter’s bound
[
7
, Theorem 1.5.6]
.This justifies that we took the limit in Equation (
5.4
).

Let us set

1+\delta^{\prime}_{w}:=(1+\delta_{w})\frac{\vartheta(1-i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}}{1-\lambda_{w}},

so that
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
is also a continuous function of
w
with
\lim_{w\to 0}\delta^{\prime}_{w}=0
. In addition, we choose
U=(-\eta,\eta)
for small enough
\eta>0
. Then:

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{(1+i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w))(1+\delta^{\prime}_{w})}{\vartheta(1+\zeta^{\prime 2})}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w+O_{\eta}(1)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|.

(6.8)

As in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, we split this integral in three parts, which can be dealt with roughly in the same way, with a few adaptations.

Control of
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on the neighbourhood
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

As in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, for some constant
C\geq 0
,

\left| \int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\delta^{\prime}_{w}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w\right|\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}w^{2}L(w^{-1})\;{\rm d}w.

But the function
w\mapsto w^{2}L(w^{-1})
has regular variation of index
2-\alpha>0
near
0
, so by Karamata’s theorem
[
27
]
(alternatively,
[
7
, Proposition 1.5.8]
),

\left| \int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\delta^{\prime}_{w}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w\right|\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1}).

Control of the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

For all
\iota\in(0,\alpha-1)
,

\displaystyle\Bigg| \int_{(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{w}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\varepsilon\int_{1}^{+\infty}L(\varepsilon^{-1}w^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq C_{\iota}\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\varepsilon\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{L(\varepsilon^{-1})}{w^{\alpha-\iota}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=O\left(\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})\right),

where we used Potter’s bound
[
7
, Theorem 1.5.6]
to obtain the penultimate line, and the fact that
\alpha-\iota>1
to get the last line.

Main contribution to the integral (
6.8
).

Now let us focus on the remaining term. Again we split
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
along its frequencies. For all
i
,
j\in I
:

\displaystyle\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}

\displaystyle\left(e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))}-1\right)(1+i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{-\varepsilon^{-1}\eta}^{\varepsilon^{-1}\eta}\left(e^{iw(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))}-1\right)(1+i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w))L(\varepsilon^{-1}|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left(e^{iw(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))}-1\right)(1+i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w))}{|w|^{\alpha}}\;{\rm d}w+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})),

where we used on the last line the fact that
L(\varepsilon^{-1}|w|^{-1})\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}L(\varepsilon^{-1})|w|^{-\alpha}
to ensure pointwise convergence, and Potter’s bounds and the dominated convergence theorem to ensure the convergence of the integrals. From there we get

\displaystyle\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}

\displaystyle\left(e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))}-1\right)(1+i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left[\frac{\left(e^{iw(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))}-1\right)(1+i\zeta^{\prime})}{w^{\alpha}}+\frac{\left(e^{-iw(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))}-1\right)(1-i\zeta^{\prime})}{w^{\alpha}}\right]\;{\rm d}w+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1}))

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)|^{\alpha-1}\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Re\left[(1+i\zeta^{\prime})e^{-i\frac{(\alpha-1)\pi}{2}\sgn(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))}\right]+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1}))

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)|^{\alpha-1}\Gamma(1-\alpha)\left[\cos\left(\frac{(\alpha-1)\pi}{2}\right)+\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))\sin\left(\frac{(\alpha-1)\pi}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})).

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)|^{\alpha-1}\Gamma(1-\alpha)\sin\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)\left[ 1+\zeta\sgn(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))\right]+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})).

where the integrals are evaluated using
[
18
, Chap. XVII.4,
4.11
]
. Reintroducing the constant factors of Equation (
6.8
) and replacing
\vartheta
and
\zeta
by their values then yield:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}

\displaystyle\left(e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))}-1\right)\frac{1+i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w)}{\vartheta(1+\zeta^{\prime 2})}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)|^{\alpha-1}\frac{2\Gamma(1-\alpha)\sin\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)\left[ 1+\frac{c_{+}-c_{-}}{c_{+}+c_{-}}\sgn(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))\right]}{2\pi(c_{+}+c_{-})\Gamma(1-\alpha)\cos\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)\left(1+\left(\frac{c_{+}-c_{-}}{c_{+}+c_{-}}\right)^{2}\tan^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)|^{\alpha-1}\frac{\sin\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)\left[ (c_{+}+c_{-})+(c_{+}-c_{-})\sgn(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))\right]}{\pi\left((c_{+}+c_{-})^{2}\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)+(c_{+}-c_{-})^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\alpha\pi)\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|^{\alpha-1}\left(c_{+}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma(j)>\sigma(i)}+c_{-}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma(j)<\sigma(i)}\right)}{\pi\left(c_{+}^{2}+c_{-}^{2}+2c_{-}c_{+}\cos(\alpha\pi)\right)}\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})).

Let us set

\langle f,S(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}:=\sum_{i,j\in I}f_{i}g_{j}\frac{\sin(\alpha\pi)\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|^{\alpha-1}\left(c_{+}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma(j)>\sigma(i)}+c_{-}\mathbf{1}_{\sigma(j)<\sigma(i)}\right)}{\pi\left(c_{-}^{2}+c_{+}^{2}+2c_{-}c_{+}\cos(\alpha\pi)\right)}.

After summing over
i
,
j\in I
, we obtain

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{1+i\zeta^{\prime}\sgn(w)}{\vartheta(1+\zeta^{\prime 2})}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w=\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})\langle f,S(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})).

Then, as in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, we can control the error terms involving
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
to get

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})\langle f,S(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+o(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})),

which, after replacing
\varepsilon
by
t^{-1}
, finishes the proof of the first part of Proposition
6.4
.

In addition, for all
i\neq j\in I
,

\langle\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j},S(\mathbf{1}_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{j})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{(c_{+}+c_{-})\left|\sin(\alpha\pi)\right|}{\pi\left[c_{+}^{2}+c_{-}^{2}+2c_{+}c_{-}\cos(\alpha\pi)\right]}\left|\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)\right|^{\alpha-1}>0,

so that
S
is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
. By Theorem
0.1
, there exists an irreducible bi-L-matrix
R
such that

P_{\varepsilon}=_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Id-\frac{1}{\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})}R+o\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})}\right);

replacing
\varepsilon
by
t^{-1}
finishes the proof.
∎

Example 6.5

(Asymmetric Lévy distributions)
.

While we have no general explicit expression for
R
, it can be computed on given examples. Let us consider for instance the case where
c_{-}=0
and, without loss of generality,
c_{+}=1
. This corresponds to a Lévy distribution which is maximally asymmetric, supported of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Take, for instance,
I=\{A,B,C\}
with
(\sigma(A),\sigma(B),\sigma(C))=(-1,0,1)
.

Set
e_{1}:=\mathbf{1}_{C}-\mathbf{1}_{B}
and
e_{2}:=\mathbf{1}_{B}-\mathbf{1}_{A}
. One computes

\begin{array}[]{llll}\langle e_{1},S(e_{1})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}&=\frac{|\sin(\alpha\pi)|}{\pi};&\langle e_{1},S(e_{2})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}&=0;\\
\langle e_{2},S(e_{1})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}&=-(2-2^{\alpha-1})\frac{|\sin(\alpha\pi)|}{\pi};&\langle e_{2},S(e_{2})\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}&=\frac{|\sin(\alpha\pi)|}{\pi}.\end{array}

Since
\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=2
if
i=j
and
-1
otherwise, we can write
S
in the basis
(e_{1},e_{2})
:

S=\frac{|\sin(\alpha\pi)|}{3\pi}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2^{\alpha-1}&1\\
2^{\alpha}-3&2\end{array}\right),

whence

S^{-1}=R_{0}=\frac{\pi}{|\sin(\alpha\pi)|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&-1\\
3-2^{\alpha}&2^{\alpha-1}\end{array}\right).

We can then recover, in the basis
(\mathbf{1}_{A},\mathbf{1}_{B},\mathbf{1}_{C})
,

R=\frac{\pi}{|\sin(\alpha\pi)|}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-(2^{\alpha-1}-1)&-(2-2^{\alpha-1})\\
-1&2^{\alpha-1}&-(2^{\alpha-1}-1)\\
0&-1&1\end{array}\right),

(6.9)

and
P_{t}=_{t\to+\infty}\Id-\frac{t}{L(t)}R+o(tL(t^{-1}))
by Proposition
6.4
. The first term of the last line is
0
, which means that transitions from
\sigma_{t}(C)\sim t
to
\sigma_{t}(A)\sim-t
avoiding
0
have low probability (in
o(tL(t^{-1}))
). Transitions from
\sigma_{t}(A)\sim-t
to
\sigma_{t}(C)\sim t
avoiding
0
have a much higher probability.

This is expected: one can find random walks under these constraints whose jumps are supported on
[-1,+\infty)
, in which case transitions from
\sigma_{t}(C)
to
\sigma_{t}(A)
avoiding
0
are forbidden.

The matrix
R
of Equation (
6.9
) has other interesting features. If
\alpha
is close to
2
, as expected, the matrix
R
is close to the one of the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
case, written in Proposition
6.1
. Transitions occur mostly between neighbours, that is, between
\sigma_{t}(A)
and
\sigma_{t}(B)
or between
\sigma_{t}(B)
and
\sigma_{t}(C)
. If
\alpha
is close to
1
, on the other hand, the transitions occur mostly in a circular way: from
\sigma_{t}(A)
to
\sigma_{t}(C)
, or from
\sigma_{t}(C)
to
\sigma_{t}(B)
, or from
\sigma_{t}(B)
to
\sigma_{t}(A)
.

Example 6.6

(Symmetric Lévy distributions)
.

Let us present briefly the symmetric case. Without loss of generality, we assume that
c_{-}=c_{+}=1
. We take
I=\{A,B,C\}
with
(\sigma(A),\sigma(B),\sigma(C))=(-1,0,1)
, as in Example
6.5
. Similar computations yield

R=\frac{2\pi}{2^{\alpha-1}(4-2^{\alpha-1})|\tan\left(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\right)|}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}2&-2^{\alpha-1}&-(2-2^{\alpha-1})\\
-2^{\alpha-1}&2^{\alpha}&-2^{\alpha-1}\\
-(2-2^{\alpha-1})&-2^{\alpha-1}&2\end{array}\right),

(6.10)

and
P_{t}=_{t\to+\infty}\Id-\frac{t}{L(t)}R+o(tL(t^{-1}))
by Proposition
6.4
.

If
\alpha
is close to
2
, as expected, the matrix
R
is close to the one of the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
case, written in Proposition
6.1
. If
\alpha
is close to
1
, on the other hand, the transitions are close to the symmetric Cauchy case, written in Proposition
6.8
.

6.3
Computation of the potential:
d=1
, Cauchy jumps

We include some distributions for the jumps in the basin of attraction of a
1
-stable law. These are of particular interest for at least two reasons. First, they occur in geometric settings, such at the geodesic flow on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
with a translation-invariant hyperbolic metric
[
2
,
3
]
. In addition, in the context of the current work, we can compute explicitly the asymptotics of their transition matrices
(P_{t})_{t>0}
and not just the asymptotic potential operator
S
.

Getting exhaustive conditions ensuring that a given distribution is in the basin of attraction of a
1
-stable law is a notoriously difficult problem. As in
[
40
, Corollary 2.9]
, we choose to make a spectral assumption which, while very restrictive
6
6

6

In particular, the
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
terms appearing in
[
2
, Theorem 2]
must cancel up to an error in
o(L(|w|^{-1})^{-1})
.
, includes symmetric distributions. The work of J. Aaronson and M. Denker offers some explicit conditions on the tails of
F
which guarantee that Hypothesis
6.7
is satisfied
[
2
]
.

Hypothesis 6.7

(Basin of attraction of
1
-stable distributions)
.

There exist
\vartheta>0
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
and
L
with regular variation of index
1
at infinity such that

\int_{A}e^{iwF}\;{\rm d}\mu=_{w\to 0}e^{-\vartheta(1-i\zeta\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}}+o(L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}).

(6.11)

Furthermore, we then set

J(t):=\int_{C}^{t}\frac{L(s)}{s^{2}}\;{\rm d}s,

which is well-defined for all large enough
C
and all
t\geq C
.

We then get:

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\mu,T)
with jump function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}
. Assume furthermore that
F
satisfies Hypothesis
6.7
.

Let
I
be non-empty and finite and
\sigma:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be injective. For
t>0
, let
\sigma_{t}:I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\sigma_{t}(i)=t\sigma(i)+o(t)
for all
i\in I
. Let
P_{t}
be the transition matrix of the Markov chain induced on
\Sigma_{t}:=\sigma_{t}(I)
.
Then

P_{t}=_{t\to+\infty}\Id-\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{J(t)}R+o(J(t)^{-1}),

where
R
is the irreducible, symmetric bi-L-matrix defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcll}R_{ij}&=&-\frac{1}{|I|}&\quad\text{whenever }i\neq j\in I,\\
R_{ii}&=&\frac{|I|-1}{|I|}&\quad\forall\ i\in I.\end{array}\right.

(6.12)

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
6.8
, and setting

a(t):=\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{|I|J(t)},

the transition matrices
(P_{t})_{t>0}
have the following graphical representation:

P_{t}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1-(|I|-1)a(t)&a(t)&\ldots&a(t)&a(t)\\
a(t)&1-(|I|-1)a(t)&\ldots&a(t)&a(t)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a(t)&a(t)&\ldots&1-(|I|-1)a(t)&a(t)\\
a(t)&a(t)&\ldots&a(t)&1-(|I|-1)a(t)\end{array}\right)+o(J(t)^{-1}).

It is possible to get less trivial limit matrices if one looks at shapes whose points lie at different spatial scales. One may consider, for instance,
L(t)\sim Ct
, so that
J(t)\sim C\ln(t)
, and
\left\|\sigma_{t}(i)-\sigma_{t}(j)\right\|\sim t^{\max\{r(i),r(j)\}}
for some function
r:I\to(0,+\infty)
.

Before proving Proposition
6.8
, we introduce a technical lemma:

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
([\mathbb{Z}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
be an ergodic and recurrent Markov
\mathbb{Z}
-extension of a Gibbs-Markov map
(A,\mu,T)
with jump function
F:A\to\mathbb{Z}
. Assume furthermore that
F
satisfies Hypothesis
6.7
. Then, for all large enough
C
,

\int_{C}^{+\infty}\frac{L(t)}{t^{2}}\;{\rm d}t=+\infty.

Proof.

By Halmos’ recurrence theorem
[
24
]
, if
([\mathbb{Z}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is recurrent, then

\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\widetilde{\mu}((x,p)\in[0]\text{ and }\widetilde{T}^{n}(x,p)\in[0])=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mu(S_{n}F=0)=+\infty.

As in the proof of Proposition
5.3
, we can express this sum using the Fourier transform and twisted transfer operators. For all small enough
\eta>0
, such that in particular
|\mu_{w}(\mathbf{1})|\leq 2\pi
on
(-\eta,\eta)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mu(S_{n}F=0)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\rho^{n}\mu(S_{n}F=0)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\rho^{n}\int_{A}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}e^{iwS_{n}F}\;{\rm d}w\;{\rm d}\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\rho^{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}\int_{A}\mathcal{L}_{w}^{n}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}\int_{A}\left(\Id-\rho\mathcal{L}_{w}\right)^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\;{\rm d}\mu\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\limsup_{\rho\to 1^{-}}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\frac{|\mu_{w}(\mathbf{1})|}{|1-\rho\lambda_{w}|}\;{\rm d}w+O(1)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\frac{1}{|1-\lambda_{w}|}\;{\rm d}w+O(1).

By
[
3
, Theorem 2]
, under Condition (
6.11
) and with the same constants,

\lambda_{w}=e^{-\vartheta(1-i\zeta\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}}+o(L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}).

Taking real parts and possibly a lower value of
\eta
, the function
|1-\lambda_{w}|
has a lower bound in
\vartheta L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}/2
, and in addition

\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\frac{2}{\vartheta L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}}\;{\rm d}w=\frac{4}{\vartheta}\int_{\eta^{-1}}^{+\infty}\frac{L(w)}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w,

which must diverge.
∎

We can now proceed to the proof of Proposition
6.8
.

Proof of Proposition
6.8
.

The structure of this demonstration follows that of the proofs of Propositions
6.1
and
6.4
.

We assume the setting of Proposition
6.8
. In particular, using Hypothesis
6.7
, let
\vartheta>0
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
and
L
with regular variation of index
1
at infinity such that

\int_{A}e^{iwF}\;{\rm d}\mu=_{w\to 0}e^{-\vartheta(1-i\zeta\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}}+o(L(|w|^{-1})^{-1}).

We set
\varepsilon:=t^{-1}
. With exact the same reasoning as in the beginning of the proof of Proposition
6.4
, we get for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
:

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\frac{(1+i\zeta\sgn(w))(1+\delta^{\prime}_{w})}{\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w+O_{\eta}(1)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|,

(6.13)

where
\lim_{w\to 0}\delta^{\prime}_{w}=0
.

As in the proof of Proposition
6.4
, we split this integral in three parts; however these parts are different:

•

the whole integral on a small neighbourhood
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
of
0
, which shall be negligible because
\left(\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)(w)
is small near
0
, with a small additional gain thanks to
[
7
, Proposition 1.5.9b]
.

•

the integral involving the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, which shall be negligible because
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
is small and
L(|w|^{-1})
is large.

•

the integral on
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
without the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
, which is the main contribution to
\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}
.

Another meaningful difference will reside in the computation of the asymptotics of the main part: the non-zero frequencies of
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
will yield a negligible contribution by an argument
à la
Riemann-Lebesgue.

Control of the integral on the neighbourhood
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

The exact same computation as in the proof of Proposition
6.4
yields

\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)(1+\delta^{\prime}_{w})L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w=\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|O\left(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})\right).

In addition, since
t^{-2}L(t)
is not integrable by Lemma
6.9
, we can apply
[
7
, Proposition 1.5.9b]
to get that
t^{-1}L(t)=o(J(t))
, or by a change of variable

\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)(1+\delta^{\prime}_{w})L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w=\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|o\left(J(\varepsilon^{-1})\right).

Control of the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.

On
(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, we get

\displaystyle\Bigg| \int_{(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{w}L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}L(w^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\int_{\eta^{-1}}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\frac{L(w)}{w^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\left[J(\varepsilon^{-1})-J(\eta^{-1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|J(\varepsilon^{-1}).

Main contribution to the integral (
6.13
).

Now let us focus on the remaining term. Again we split
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
along its frequencies. Without loss of generality,
L
can be chosen increasing. The function
w\mapsto L(w^{-1})
is then decreasing on
(\varepsilon,\eta)
. By the Riemann-Stieltjes version of the integration by parts
[
5
, Theorem 7.6]
, whenever
i\neq j\in I
,

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}

\displaystyle e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))}L(w^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1)}\left[e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))}L(w^{-1})\right]_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1)}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j)+o(1))}\left[L(w^{-1})\right]^{\prime}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=O\left(\varepsilon L(\varepsilon^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=o\left(J(\varepsilon^{-1})\right),

where we again applied Lemma
6.9
and
[
7
, Proposition 1.5.9b]
. The integral over
(-\eta,-\varepsilon)
can be dealt with in the same way. If
i=j
, then

\displaystyle\int_{(-\eta,\eta)\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}w(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i)+o(1))}(1+i\zeta\sgn(w))L(|w|^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=2\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}L(w^{-1})\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=2\left[ J(\varepsilon^{-1})-J(\eta^{-1})\right]

\displaystyle\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2J(\varepsilon^{-1}).

Finally, summing up everything,

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}\langle f,g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|o(J(\varepsilon^{-1}))+\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in[-\eta,\eta]}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|O(J(\varepsilon^{-1})).

By the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, making
\eta
smaller as
\varepsilon
vanishes, we get

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}\langle f,g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|o(J(\varepsilon^{-1})),

or, in other words,

Q_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}\Id_{\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}.

Asymptotics of the transition matrix
P_{\varepsilon}
.

The identity is irreducible in the sense of Definition
1.13
. Hence we can apply Theorem
0.1
with
S=\Id_{\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}
:

(\Id-P_{\varepsilon})_{\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}\Id_{\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}.

The last step is to get the asymptotics for
P_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{C}^{I}
. Writing
\varpi_{I}
the projection parallel to constants onto
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
,

\displaystyle P_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}\otimes\mu_{I}+(P_{\varepsilon})_{\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}\to\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}\varpi_{I}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}\otimes\mu_{I}+\left(1-\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}\right)\varpi_{I}+o\left(J(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}\right)\Id+\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}\mathbf{1}\otimes\mu_{I}+o\left(J(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\right).

In other words, the diagonal terms of
P_{\varepsilon}
are all

1-\frac{|I|-1}{|I|}\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}+o\left(J(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\right),

while the off-diagonal terms are all

\frac{1}{|I|}\frac{\pi\vartheta(1+\zeta^{2})}{J(\varepsilon^{-1})}+o\left(J(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\right);

replacing
\varepsilon
by
t^{-1}
finishes the proof.
∎

6.4
Computation of the potential:
d=2
, square integrable jumps

Finally, we tackle
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-extensions with square-integrable jumps. In addition to the general setting of Section
6
, we assume in this Subsection that
F
is square-integrable and centered, the later condition being already implied by the first condition and the ergodicity of the extension.

Let
\Cov
be the asymptotic covariance matrix of
F
, defined for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
by:

\langle u,\Cov(u)\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=\int_{A}\langle F,u\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}\;{\rm d}\mu+2\sum_{n\geq 1}\int_{A}\langle F,u\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\circ T^{n}\cdot\langle F,u\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\;{\rm d}\mu,

(6.14)

where the infinite series is taken in the sense of Cesàro. Since
([\mathbb{Z}^{2}],\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
is ergodic,
\langle F,u\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
is not a coboundary whenever
u\neq 0
(otherwise the sums
S_{n}\langle F,u\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
would be
almost surely bounded), and thus
\Cov
is positive definite
[
21
, Théorème 4.1.4]
.

The behaviour of the transition matrices is then similar to the Cauchy case (Proposition
6.8
), with a statement given in the introduction (Proposition
0.2
) and which we shall now prove.

Proof of Proposition
0.2
.

The structure of this demonstration is very close to that of Proposition
6.8
. We assume the setting of Proposition
0.2
and set
\varepsilon:=t^{-1}
. The main eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
then admits the expansion
[
21
, Théorème 4.1.4]

\lambda_{w}=1-\frac{\langle w,\Cov(w)\rangle}{2}+o(\left\|w\right\|^{2}).

(6.15)

With the exact same reasoning as in the beginning of the proof of Proposition
6.1
, but with a two-dimensional Fourier transform, we get for all
f
,
g\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}
and all small enough neighborhood
U
of
0
:

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{U}\left(\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)(w)\frac{1+\delta^{\prime}_{w}}{\langle w,\Cov(w)\rangle}\;{\rm d}w+O_{U}(1)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|,

(6.16)

where
\lim_{w\to 0}\delta^{\prime}_{w}=0
.

We choose
U=\sqrt{\Cov}B(0,\eta)
for small enough
\eta>0
. Then, after a change of variables:

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}\sqrt{\det(\Cov)}}\int_{B(0,\eta)}\left(\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)(\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w)\frac{1+\delta^{\prime}_{\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w}}{\left\|w\right\|^{2}}\;{\rm d}w+O_{\eta}(1)\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|.

As in the proof of Proposition
6.8
, we split this integral
in three parts:

•

in dimension
2
, since both
f
and
g
have zero sum, the contribution of a small ball
B(0,\varepsilon)
is in
O(1)
.

•

the integral involving the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)
, which shall be negligible because
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
is small and
\left\|w\right\|^{2}
is large.

•

for the remainder, we split
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
along its different frequencies. Again, the frequency
0
has to be considered separately, and will give the main contribution to
\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}
. The other frequencies will have a lower contribution thanks to cancellations
à la
Riemman-Lebesgue. Computations will be handled in polar coordinates, thanks to our choice of neighbourhood
U
.

For the third part and non-zero frequencies, we will subdivise the region
B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)
in two parts: one close to the line orthogonal to the frequency vector, which shall be small, and the remaining part of the annulus, on which the Riemann-Lebesgue argument works out.

\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(q)-\sigma(p))
A
A
A
A
B
B
C
C
\varepsilon
\eta

Figure 7:
Regions of integration for non-zero frequencies. Light blue (
A
): negligible by a Riemann-Lebesgue argument. Middle blue (
B
): near the perpendicular to
\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(q)-\sigma(p))
; negligible because the area is arbitrarily small. Dark blue (
C
): a disc of radius
\varepsilon
; negligible because the area is small and the function
\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)
cancels the denominator.

Control of the integral on the neighbourhood
B(0,\varepsilon)
.

Our usual computations, as in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, give:

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,\varepsilon)}\left|\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right|(\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w)\frac{\left|1+\delta_{\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w}\right|}{\left\|w\right\|^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{B(0,\varepsilon)}1\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|.

Control of the error term
\delta^{\prime}_{w}
on
B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)
.

On
B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)
, we get

\displaystyle\Bigg| \int_{B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)}\left(\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)

\displaystyle(\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w)\frac{\delta_{\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w}}{\left\|w\right\|^{2}}\;{\rm d}w\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in\sqrt{\Cov}\cdot\overline{B}(0,\eta)}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\int_{B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)}\frac{1}{\left\|w\right\|^{2}}\;{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in\sqrt{\Cov}\cdot\overline{B}(0,\eta)}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\frac{2\pi}{r}\;{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi C\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in\sqrt{\Cov}\cdot\overline{B}(0,\eta)}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|\cdot\left|\ln(\varepsilon)\right|.

Main contribution to the integral (
6.16
).

Now let us focus on the remaining term. Again we split
\left(\widecheck{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}(f)\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)(\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w)
along its frequencies. Fix some small
\kappa>0
. For
i\neq j\in I
,

\int_{B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)}\frac{e^{i\langle\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w,\varepsilon^{-1}(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))+o(\varepsilon^{-1})\rangle}}{\left\|w\right\|^{2}}\;{\rm d}w\\
=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{1}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\frac{e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}r\langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle}}{r}\;{\rm d}r\;{\rm d}\theta.

For fixed
\theta\in\mathbb{S}_{1}
, the inner integral can be bounded in two ways. First, we have the crude but general:

\left| \int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\frac{e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}r\langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle}}{r}\;{\rm d}r\right|\leq\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\frac{1}{r}\;{\rm d}r=\ln(\eta)-\ln(\varepsilon)\leq\left| \ln(\varepsilon)\right|.

A second way uses an integration by parts:

\displaystyle\left| \int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}r\langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle}}{r}\;{\rm d}r\right|

\displaystyle=\left| \left[\frac{e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}r\langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle}}{\left(i\varepsilon^{-1}\langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle\right)r}\right]_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.+\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\frac{e^{i\varepsilon^{-1}r\langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle}}{\left(i\varepsilon^{-1}\langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle\right)r^{2}}\;{\rm d}r\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{\left| \langle(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}(\sigma(j)-\sigma(i))+o(1)\rangle\right|}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\eta}\right).

(6.17)

Let
K_{\eta,\varepsilon,i,j}
be the set of values of
\theta
such that the right hand-side of Equation (
6.17
) is smaller or equal to
\sqrt{|\ln(\varepsilon)|}
. Then
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\Leb(K_{\eta,\varepsilon,i,j})=2\pi
, whence

\displaystyle\left| \int_{B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{e^{i\langle\sqrt{\Cov}^{-1}w,\varepsilon^{-1}(\sigma(i)-\sigma(j))+o(\varepsilon^{-1})\rangle}}{\left\|w\right\|^{2}}\;{\rm d}w\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{K_{\eta,\varepsilon,i,j}}\sqrt{|\ln(\varepsilon)|}\;{\rm d}\theta+\int_{\mathbb{S}_{1}\setminus K_{\eta,\varepsilon,i,j}}|\ln(\varepsilon)|\;{\rm d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\sqrt{|\ln(\varepsilon)|}+\Leb(\mathbb{S}_{1}\setminus K_{\eta,\varepsilon,i,j})|\ln(\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle=o\left(|\ln(\varepsilon)|\right).

The zero frequency term is comparatively easier to compute:

\int_{B(0,\eta)\setminus B(0,\varepsilon)}\frac{1}{\left\|w\right\|^{2}}\;{\rm d}w=2\pi\int_{\varepsilon}^{\eta}\frac{1}{r}\;{\rm d}r=2\pi\left(\ln(\eta)-\ln(\varepsilon)\right)\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\pi|\ln(\varepsilon)|.

Finally, summing up everything,

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{|\ln(\varepsilon)|}{\pi\sqrt{\det(\Cov)}}\langle f,g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|\max_{w\in\sqrt{\Cov}\cdot\overline{B}(0,\eta)}|\delta^{\prime}_{w}|O(|\ln(\varepsilon)|)+\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|o(|\ln(\varepsilon)|).

By the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
6.1
,
making
\eta
smaller as
\varepsilon
vanishes, we get

\langle f,Q_{\varepsilon}(g)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}=\frac{|\ln(\varepsilon)|}{\pi\sqrt{\det(\Cov)}}\langle f,g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}+\left\|f\right\|\left\|g\right\|o(|\ln(\varepsilon)|),

or, in other words,

Q_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{|\ln(\varepsilon)|}{\pi\sqrt{\det(\Cov)}}\Id_{\mathbb{C}_{0}^{I}}.

The computation of the asymptotics of
P_{\varepsilon}
from those of
Q_{\varepsilon}
are then the same as in the end of the proof of Proposition
6.8
.
∎

Appendix A
Gibbs-Markov maps

In this appendix, we compile definitions and technical resutls relative to Gibbs-Markov maps. In what follows,
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
is a Gibbs-Markov map (see Definition
1.5
).

A.1
Markov partitions and stopping times

We aim to define various partitions and filtrations related to Gibbs-Markov maps, so as to introduce stopping times. The central definition is that of a
cylinder
.

Definition A.1

(Cylinder)
.

For
n\geq 0
, a
cylinder
of length
n
is a non-trivial element of
\alpha_{n}:=\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}T^{-k}\alpha
.
It is given by a unique finite sequence
(a_{k})_{0\leq k<n}
of elements of
\alpha
such that
T(a_{k})\cap a_{k+1}
is non-negligible for all
0\leq k<n-1
. Such a cylinder shall be denoted by
[a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1}]
.

We denote by
\alpha^{*}
the image partition, generated by
\{T(a):\ a\in\alpha\}
. Since
T
is Markov,
\alpha^{*}
is coarser than
\alpha
.

With any Markov map comes a natural filtration given by
\mathcal{F}_{n}:=\sigma(\alpha_{n})
for all
n\geq 0
. The
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is trivial, while
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}:=\bigvee_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_{n}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
(A,d)
. From this filtration we define
stopping times
.

Definition A.2

(Stopping time)
.

Let
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
be a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
\varphi:\ A\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}
be measurable. We say that
\varphi
is a
stopping time
if
\{\varphi\leq n\}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
, and
1\leq\varphi<+\infty
almost surely.

To any stopping time
\varphi
we associate a countable partition of
A
given by:

\alpha_{\varphi}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\{\overline{a}\in\alpha_{n}:\ \mu(\overline{a})>0\text{ and }\overline{a}\subset\{\varphi=n\}\},

and a transformation defined by:

T_{\varphi}(x):=T^{\varphi(x)}(x),

which is well-defined almost everywhere.

In this article, and in opposition to other ressources, we shall always require stopping times to be positive and finite, for the following reasons. If we did not require stopping times to be positive, then
\varphi\equiv 0
would be a stopping time (and the only stopping time such that
\mu(\varphi=0)>0
), for which the partition the partition
\alpha_{\varphi}
would be trivial. If we did not require stopping times to be finite, then
T_{\varphi}
may not be well-defined.

The iterates of
T_{\varphi}
can be directly related to stopping times. For any
n\geq 1
, we define:

\varphi^{(n)}=S_{n}^{T_{\varphi}}\varphi:=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi\circ T_{\varphi}^{k}.

(A.1)

Then
T_{\varphi}^{n}=T_{\varphi^{(n)}}
. As the following lemma asserts,
\varphi^{(n)}
is again a stopping time.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
be a Gibbs-Markov map. Let
\varphi
be a stopping time and
n\geq 1
. Then
\varphi^{(n)}
is a stopping time.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
. For
n=1
, the function
\varphi^{(1)}=\varphi
is a stopping time. Let
n\geq 1
and assume that
\varphi^{(n)}
is a stopping time. We have

\varphi^{(n+1)}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\varphi\circ T_{\varphi}^{k}=\varphi+\varphi^{(n)}\circ T_{\varphi},

so that, for all
k\geq 0
:

\left\{\varphi^{(n+1)}\leq k\right\}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}\left\{\varphi\leq j\right\}\cap T^{-j}\left\{\varphi^{(n)}\leq k-j\right\}.

Let
0\leq j\leq k
. Both
\varphi
and
\varphi^{(n)}
are stopping times, so
\left\{\varphi\leq j\right\}\in\mathcal{F}_{j}
and
\left\{\varphi^{(n)}\leq k-j\right\}\in\mathcal{F}_{k-j}
. It follows that

\left\{\varphi\leq j\right\}\cap T^{-j}\left\{\varphi^{(n)}\leq k-j\right\}\in\mathcal{F}_{j}\vee\mathcal{F}_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}_{k},

and thus
\left\{\varphi^{(n+1)}\leq k\right\}
is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-measurable.
∎

The Markov partitions
\alpha
and
\alpha_{\varphi}
can be refined by considering iterates of the transformation. For any stopping time
\varphi
and any
n\geq 1
, we define:

\alpha_{\varphi}^{(n)}:=\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}T_{\varphi}^{-k}\alpha_{\varphi}.

With these notations,
\alpha_{\varphi}^{(n)}=\alpha_{\varphi^{(n)}}
. We also denote by
\alpha^{(n)}:=\alpha_{\mathbf{1}}^{(n)}
the partition generated by cylinders of length
n
.

A.2
Stopping times and regularizing effect of
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}

To each stopping time
\varphi
is associated a transformation
T_{\varphi}
, and thus a transfer operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
defined by

\int_{A}\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}(f)\cdot g\;{\rm d}\mu=\int_{A}f\cdot g\circ T_{\varphi}\;{\rm d}\mu\quad\forall\ f\in\mathbb{L}^{1}(A,\mu),\ \forall\ g\in\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(A,\mu).

The transformation
T_{\varphi}
in general does not preserve
\mu
, thus in general
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{1})\neq\mathbf{1}
.
The operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
still acts quasi-compactly on the Banach space
\mathcal{B}
introduced in Definition
1.6
. To make the statement more flexible, we introduce local Lipschitz seminorms:

Definition A.4

(Local Lipschitz seminorms)
.

Let
(A,\alpha,d,\mu,T)
be a Gibbs-Markov map and
f:A\to\mathbb{C}
. The function
D_{\alpha}f:A\to\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{+\infty\}
is defined by

D_{\alpha}f(x):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y,z\in a\\
